      e) 2, 6, 0, 6, 4                     f) 0, 32
      g) (2, 11), (2, 32), (2, 36), (1, 108), (2, 14), (2, 15)
      h) 11a, y² = x³ − x, y² = x³ + 1, 14a, 15a
      i) −2, 6, 2 (= a_p of y² = x³ − x)   j) −4, 2, 8 (= a_p of y² = x³ + 1)

M10. Galois representations.
     a) char poly of ρ_{11a,ℓ}(Frob_p) for p = 2, 3, 5, 7
     b) factor them mod 5 (p = 2, 3, 7)
     c) trace and determinant of ρ_{37a,ℓ}(Frob₅)
     d) image of ρ̄_{E,2} for y² = x³ − x, x³ + 1, x³ − 3x + 1, x³ − 2,
        x³ + x + 1, x³ + 4, 15a, 37a
     e) ψ₃ for y² = x³ − x, y² = x³ + 1, y² = x³ + 4, y² = x³ − 432
     f) #GL₂(F_ℓ) for ℓ = 2, 3, 5, 7
     g) [Q(E[ℓ]) : Q] if ρ̄_{E,ℓ} is surjective, ℓ = 3, 5
     h) rank over ℤ_ℓ of T_ℓ(G_m), T_ℓE, T_ℓA (dim A = g)
     i) det ρ_{f,ℓ} for f of weight k; for Δ
     j) ρ̄_{Δ,691} (semisimplification)
     k) the Frobenius of y² = x³ − x over F₃ satisfies which equation?
     l) is ρ_{E,ℓ} ramified at 11 for E = 11a, ℓ ≠ 11? at 3?
     Answers:
       a) X² + 2X + 2, X² + X + 3, X² − X + 5, X² + 2X + 7
       b) (X − 1)(X − 2), (X − 1)(X − 3), (X − 1)(X − 2) (roots 1, p)
       c) −2 and 5
       d) 1, ℤ/2, ℤ/3, S₃, S₃, S₃, 1, S₃
       e) 3x⁴ − 6x² − 1; 3x(x³ + 4); 3x(x³ + 16); 3x(x³ − 1728)
       f) 6, 48, 480, 2016           g) 48, 480
       h) 1, 2, 2g                   i) χ_ℓ^{k−1}; χ_ℓ¹¹
       j) 1 ⊕ χ₆₉₁¹¹                  k) φ² + 3 = 0
       l) yes (bad reduction, v(j) < 0); no (good reduction)

M11. Artin representations, twists, CM, Sato-Tate, Langlands.
     a) Chebotarev: density of p splitting in Q(i); in Q(∛2, ζ₃); of p
        with x³ − 2 irreducible mod p
     b) tr ρ(Frob_p), ρ the S₃-rep of Q(∛2, ζ₃), for p = 5, 7, 31
     c) Artin conductor of that ρ; weight of the attached form
     d) a_p of y² = x³ − 25x (twist of y² = x³ − x by 5) for p = 13, 17,
        29, 37
     e) a_p of y² = x³ + 1 for p = 5, 11, 17 (reason?)
     f) the Dirichlet character attached to Q(√5), Q(i), Q(√−3)
     g) the L-group (dual group) of GL_n, SL₂, PGL₂, Sp₄, SO₅
     h) degree of L(s, Symⁿf), L(s, f × g), L(s, Ad f) for GL₂ forms f, g
     i) who proved functoriality of Sym², Sym³, Sym⁴, all Symⁿ (hol.)
     j) Sato-Tate: averages of (a_p/√p)², (a_p/√p)⁴; P(a_p > 0)
     k) local Langlands type of π_p for good, multiplicative, additive
        potentially good reduction (p ≥ 5)
     l) bound for |a_p| of a weight-1 newform
     Answers:
       a) 1/2; 1/6; 1/3              b) 0, −1, 2
       c) 108 = 2²·3³; weight 1      d) −6, −2, −10, 2
       e) 0, 0, 0 (CM by ℤ[ζ₃], p ≡ 2 mod 3)
       f) (5/·) of conductor 5; χ₋₄; χ₋₃
       g) GL_n(C), PGL₂(C), SL₂(C), SO₅(C), Sp₄(C)
       h) n + 1, 4, 3
       i) Gelbart-Jacquet; Kim-Shahidi; Kim; Newton-Thorne
       j) 1, 2 (Catalan numbers); 1/2
       k) unramified principal series; special (twist of Steinberg);
          ramified principal series or supercuspidal
       l) |a_p| ≤ 2 (sum of two roots of unity)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) An elliptic curve over Q has good reduction at almost all p.
     b) Some elliptic curve over Q has good reduction everywhere.
     c) Curves over Q with the same j are isomorphic over Q.
     d) E(Q)_tors ≅ ℤ/11 is possible.
     e) #E(F_p) = p is possible.
     f) For p ≥ 5, a_p = 0 iff E is supersingular at p.
     g) E[3] ⊆ E(Q) for some E over Q.
     h) Isogenous curves over Q have the same a_p for all good p.
     i) Two curves over Q with equal a_p for almost all p are isogenous.
     j) Every 2-dimensional complex torus is an abelian surface.
     k) Every principally polarized abelian 4-fold is a Jacobian.
     l) dim S₂(Γ₀(N)) = g(X₀(N)).
     m) τ(mn) = τ(m)τ(n) for all m, n.
     n) |τ(p)| ≤ 2p^{11/2} for all primes p.
     o) Every elliptic curve over Q is modular.
     p) ρ_{E,ℓ} is unramified at every p ≠ ℓ.
     q) det ρ_{E,ℓ} is the trivial character.
     r) ρ̄_{E,ℓ} is irreducible for every E and ℓ.
     s) If ord_{s=1}L(E, s) = 1 then rank E(Q) = 1.
     t) 1 is a congruent number.
     u) Sato-Tate equidistribution holds for CM curves.
     v) A weight-1 newform can have unbounded coefficients a_p.
     w) S₂(Γ₀(2)) = 0.
     Answers:
       a) T         b) F: every E/Q has conductor ≥ 11 (Tate)
       c) F: y² = x³ + 1 and y² = x³ + 2
       d) F: Mazur's list          e) T: y² = x³ − 2 over F₇
       f) T         g) F: Q(E[3]) ⊇ Q(ζ₃) ≠ Q
       h) T         i) T (Faltings)
       j) F: a generic C²/Λ has no Riemann form
       k) F: dim A₄ = 10 > 9 = dim M₄
       l) T         m) F: τ(4) = −1472 ≠ 576 = τ(2)²
       n) T (Deligne)              o) T (BCDT)
       p) F: 11a at p = 11 (v₁₁(j) < 0)
       q) F: it is χ_ℓ            r) F: 11a, ℓ = 5
       s) T (Gross-Zagier-Kolyvagin)          t) F (M6 b)
       u) F: a_p = 0 for half the primes (y² = x³ − x)
       v) F: |a_p| ≤ 2             w) T (g(X₀(2)) = 0)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep one "running example" on the board all term — the
  curve 11a, y² + y = x³ − x² − 10x − 20 — and attach every new notion to
  it: its discriminant −11⁵ and j (D1), its 5-torsion point (D2), its
  point counts a₂ = −2, a₃ = −1, a₅ = 1 (D3), its split multiplicative
  reduction at 11 (D5), its BSD quotient 1/5 (D7), its modular form
  η(τ)²η(11τ)² on X₀(11) (D10) and its reducible mod-5 representation
  (D11). Students then see that "elliptic curve", "modular form" and
  "Galois representation" are three descriptions of the same list of
  numbers a_p. Drill point counting by hand for p ≤ 13 until Hasse's
  bound and the recursion for #E(F_{p^k}) are automatic, and always
  check a model is minimal before reading off reduction types. Harder:
  prove Mordell-Weil for E/Q via 2-descent and heights; derive the
  Eichler-Shimura relation T_p = F + V on J₀(N) and deduce a_p(f) = a_p(E)
  for X₀(11); prove the valence formula and the dimension formula for
  Γ₀(N) via Riemann-Hurwitz; compute Q(E[3]) and the image of ρ̄_{E,3}
  for several curves; read Ribet's level-lowering statement and check
  that the Frey curve satisfies its hypotheses; and verify numerically
  the Sato-Tate moments 1, 2, 5 (Catalan numbers) for 37a with the
  primes up to 10⁴.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.3 — AUTOMORPHIC FORMS AND REPRESENTATIONS; p-ADIC HODGE THEORY;
MOTIVES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - CLASSICAL INPUT: M_k(SL₂(ℤ)) = ℂ[E₄, E₆] in weight k; dim M_k =
    ⌊k/12⌋ + 1 (k ≢ 2 mod 12), ⌊k/12⌋ (k ≡ 2 mod 12), k ≥ 0 even.
    Δ = q∏(1 − qⁿ)²⁴ = ∑τ(n)qⁿ spans S₁₂. HECKE OPERATORS T_n commute,
    are self-adjoint for the Petersson product on S_k(Γ₀(N)) (n prime to
    N); a normalized eigenform has a_mn = a_m a_n ((m, n) = 1) and
    a_{p^{r+1}} = a_p a_{p^r} − p^{k−1}a_{p^{r−1}}, so
    L(f, s) = ∏_p (1 − a_p p⁻ˢ + p^{k−1−2s})⁻¹ (p ∤ N).
  - LEVEL STRUCTURE: [SL₂(ℤ) : Γ₀(N)] = μ(N) = N∏_{p|N}(1 + 1/p);
    genus g(X₀(N)) = 1 + μ/12 − ν₂/4 − ν₃/3 − c/2 = dim S₂(Γ₀(N)).
    NEWFORMS (Atkin-Lehner): S_k(Γ₀(N)) = ⊕ S_k^new(M)^{⊕σ₀(N/M)}.
  - ADELES: 𝔸 = ℝ × ∏′ℚ_p (restricted product w.r.t. ℤ_p); ℚ ⊂ 𝔸
    discrete, 𝔸/ℚ compact; ℚ^×\𝔸^× ≅ ℝ_{>0} × ℤ̂^× (class number 1).
    STRONG APPROXIMATION for SL₂ gives GL₂(𝔸) = GL₂(ℚ)GL₂(ℝ)⁺K₀(N), so
    GL₂(ℚ)\GL₂(𝔸)/K₀(N) ≅ Γ₀(N)\GL₂(ℝ)⁺ and f ↦ φ_f(g) = f(g_∞i)
    j(g_∞, i)^{−k}(det g_∞)^{k/2} turns modular forms into AUTOMORPHIC
    FORMS (smooth, K-finite, 𝔷-finite, moderate growth); f cuspidal ⇔
    ∫_{ℚ\𝔸} φ(n(x)g) dx = 0 for all g.
  - AUTOMORPHIC REPRESENTATIONS: L²_cusp(GL₂(ℚ)\GL₂(𝔸), ω) is a discrete
    Hilbert sum of irreducibles with MULTIPLICITY ONE; FLATH: π ≅ ⊗′π_v
    (almost all π_p unramified). STRONG MULTIPLICITY ONE: π is fixed by
    π_p for almost all p. A newform f ↔ π_f with π_∞ = discrete series
    D_k; Maass forms ↔ π_∞ principal series (Δ-eigenvalue ¼ + r²);
    SELBERG: λ₁ ≥ 3/16 for congruence groups (conjecture ¼).
  - LOCAL GL₂(ℚ_p): principal series π(χ₁, χ₂) = normalized Ind_B(χ₁ ⊗
    χ₂) is irreducible iff χ₁χ₂⁻¹ ≠ |·|^{±1}; otherwise its constituents
    are a character χ∘det and a twist STEINBERG St ⊗ χ. The rest are
    SUPERCUSPIDAL. Central character χ₁χ₂. Unramified ⇔ χᵢ unramified ⇔
    π^{GL₂(ℤ_p)} ≠ 0; SATAKE PARAMETERS αₚ = χ₁(p), βₚ = χ₂(p) satisfy
    αₚ + βₚ = aₚ/p^{(k−1)/2}, αₚβₚ = 1 for a newform of trivial character.
  - CONDUCTOR (CASSELMAN): dim π^{K₁(pⁿ)} = max(0, n − a(π) + 1);
    a(π(χ₁, χ₂)) = a(χ₁) + a(χ₂), a(St ⊗ χ) = 1 (χ unramified), 2a(χ)
    (χ ramified), a(supercuspidal) ≥ 2. Level N of f = ∏p^{a(π_p)}.
    Elliptic curve over ℚ_p: good ↔ unramified, split/non-split
    multiplicative ↔ St / St ⊗ (unramified quadratic), aₚ = +1 / −1.
  - L-FUNCTIONS: L(s, π) = ∏L(s, π_v), L(s, π_p) = ((1 − αₚp⁻ˢ)(1 −
    βₚp⁻ˢ))⁻¹, so L(s, π_f) = L(f, s + (k−1)/2); completed Λ(s, π) =
    ε(s, π)Λ(1 − s, π̃) (Hecke, Jacquet-Langlands, Godement-Jacquet for
    GL_n). For f ∈ S_k(Γ₀(N)): Λ(s) = N^{s/2}(2π)⁻ˢΓ(s)L(f, s) = ±Λ(k −
    s); level 1: sign i^k. RANKIN-SELBERG L(s, π × π′) of degree nm, with
    a simple pole at s = 1 iff π′ ≅ π̃.
  - DUAL GROUPS: Ĝ has the dual root datum. GL_n ↔ GL_n(ℂ), SL_n ↔
    PGL_n(ℂ), PGL_n ↔ SL_n(ℂ), Sp_2n ↔ SO_{2n+1}(ℂ), SO_{2n+1} ↔ Sp_2n(ℂ),
    SO_2n ↔ SO_2n(ℂ), GSp₄ ↔ GSp₄(ℂ), G₂ ↔ G₂(ℂ), tori ↔ dual tori.
    SATAKE: ℋ(G(ℚ_p)//G(ℤ_p)) ≅ ℂ[X*(T̂)]^W, so unramified π_p ↔
    semisimple conjugacy classes t_p ∈ Ĝ; L(s, π_p, r) = det(1 −
    r(t_p)p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ for any representation r of Ĝ.
  - FUNCTORIALITY: a map ᴸH → ᴸG should transfer automorphic
    representations. Known cases: Sym² GL₂ → GL₃ (Gelbart-Jacquet;
    cuspidal unless π is dihedral/CM), Sym³, Sym⁴ (Kim-Shahidi, Kim),
    all Symⁿ for holomorphic newforms (Newton-Thorne), cyclic base change
    (Langlands, Arthur-Clozel), JACQUET-LANGLANDS (D^× ↔ discrete series
    of GL₂), GL₂ × GL₂ → GL₄ (Ramakrishnan), GL₂ × GL₃ → GL₆ (Kim-Shahidi).
  - LOCAL LANGLANDS FOR GL_n (Harris-Taylor, Henniart, Scholze): a
    bijection Irr GL_n(F) ↔ n-dimensional Frobenius-semisimple WEIL-
    DELIGNE representations (r, N), preserving L- and ε-factors; n = 1 is
    local class field theory; supercuspidal ↔ irreducible r, N = 0;
    Steinberg ↔ sp(2) (N ≠ 0).
  - GALOIS REPRESENTATIONS (Eichler-Shimura, Deligne): a newform f ∈
    S_k(Γ₀(N)) gives ρ_{f,ℓ}: G_ℚ → GL₂(ℚ̄_ℓ), irreducible,
    unramified outside Nℓ, char poly of Frob_p = X² − aₚX + p^{k−1},
    det = χ_ℓ^{k−1} (χ_ℓ = cyclotomic). RAMANUJAN-PETERSSON |aₚ| ≤
    2p^{(k−1)/2} (Deligne, from the Weil conjectures). CONGRUENCES:
    τ(n) ≡ σ₁₁(n) mod 691, i.e. ρ̄_{Δ,691} ≅ 1 ⊕ χ¹¹.
  - MODULARITY (Wiles, Taylor-Wiles, BCDT): every E/ℚ of conductor N has
    aₚ(E) = p + 1 − #Ẽ(F_p) = aₚ(f_E) for a newform f_E ∈ S₂(Γ₀(N));
    L(E, s) = L(f_E, s). Serre's conjecture (Khare-Wintenberger),
    Sato-Tate (Barnet-Lamb, Geraghty, Harris, Taylor).
  - ℂ_p = completion of ℚ̄_p: algebraically closed, complete, residue
    field F̄_p, value group ℚ. ℤ_p(1) = lim μ_{pⁿ}, ℚ_p(n) = χⁿ.
    TATE: H⁰(G_K, ℂ_p(i)) = H¹(G_K, ℂ_p(i)) = K if i = 0 and 0 otherwise
    (no "2πi" in ℂ_p). HODGE-TATE: V ⊗ ℂ_p ≅ ⊕ℂ_p(−i)^{mᵢ}.
    CONVENTION HERE: the HT weight of ℚ_p(1) is −1, so HT weights are the
    jumps of the Hodge filtration and Hᵐ_ét has weights in {0, ..., m}.
  - FALTINGS: for X/K smooth proper, Hᵐ_ét(X_{K̄}, ℚ_p) ⊗ ℂ_p ≅
    ⊕_j H^{m−j}(X, Ω^j) ⊗_K ℂ_p(−j): weight j with multiplicity h^{j,m−j}.
    SEN: V ⊗ ℂ_p has a Sen operator Θ; V is HT iff Θ is semisimple with
    integer eigenvalues.
  - PERIOD RINGS (FONTAINE): B_dR = Frac B_dR⁺, a complete DVR field with
    residue field ℂ_p, uniformizer t = log[ε], Fil^i = t^iB_dR⁺, gr =
    B_HT = ⊕ℂ_p(i); B_crys ⊂ B_st ⊂ B_dR with Frobenius φ (φ(t) = pt)
    and monodromy N on B_st = B_crys[log[p̃]]. B_dR^{G_K} = K,
    B_crys^{G_K} = B_st^{G_K} = K₀. D_B(V) = (B ⊗ V)^{G_K}; always
    dim D_B(V) ≤ dim V; V is B-ADMISSIBLE if equality.
  - HIERARCHY: crystalline ⊂ semistable ⊂ de Rham ⊂ Hodge-Tate, all
    strict; p-ADIC MONODROMY (Berger): de Rham ⇔ potentially semistable.
    Examples: unramified and ℚ_p(n) crystalline; Tate curve V_pE
    semistable not crystalline; ramified finite-order characters de Rham
    not semistable; a non-split extension 0 → ℚ_p(−1) → V → ℚ_p → 0 is
    HT but not de Rham; the extension of ℚ_p by ℚ_p via log χ is not HT.
  - FILTERED φ-MODULES: D over K₀ with bijective semilinear φ and a
    filtration on D_K. t_H(D) = ∑ i·dim gr^i, t_N(D) = v_p(det φ) (K =
    ℚ_p). WEAKLY ADMISSIBLE: t_H(D) = t_N(D) and t_H(D′) ≤ t_N(D′) for
    all φ-stable D′ ("Newton polygon above Hodge polygon").
    COLMEZ-FONTAINE: D_crys is an equivalence {crystalline reps} ≃
    {weakly admissible filtered φ-modules}; similarly (φ, N) with
    Nφ = pφN for semistable reps.
  - COMPARISON THEOREMS: C_dR (Faltings), C_crys for good reduction
    (Fontaine-Messing, Faltings): D_crys(Hᵐ_ét) ≅ Hᵐ_crys, char poly of φ
    = char poly of Frobenius; C_st for semistable reduction (Tsuji).
    E/ℚ_p good: φ on H¹_crys has char poly X² − aₚX + p; ORDINARY (p ∤ aₚ,
    slopes 0, 1) or SUPERSINGULAR (slopes ½, ½). A/K has good reduction ⇔
    V_pA crystalline (p-adic Néron-Ogg-Shafarevich).
  - GALOIS COHOMOLOGY: local Euler characteristic h⁰ − h¹ + h² = −[K:ℚ_p]
    dim V, h²(V) = h⁰(V*(1)). BLOCH-KATO H¹_f = ker(H¹ → H¹(B_crys ⊗ V)),
    dim H¹_f = h⁰(V) + dim D_dR(V)/Fil⁰ (V de Rham). KUMMER: H¹(K,
    ℚ_p(1)) = (K^×)^∧ ⊗ ℚ_p, H¹_f = (𝒪_K^×)^∧ ⊗ ℚ_p.
  - PURE MOTIVES: Corr^r(X, Y) = CH^{dim X + r}(X × Y)_ℚ, composition
    g∘f = p₁₃*(p₁₂*f · p₂₃*g). CHOW MOTIVES (X, p, m) with p² = p;
    h(X) = (X, Δ, 0); 𝟙 = h(pt), LEFSCHETZ 𝕃 with h(ℙ¹) = 𝟙 ⊕ 𝕃, TATE
    ℚ(1) = 𝕃⁻¹. h(ℙⁿ) = 𝟙 ⊕ 𝕃 ⊕ ... ⊕ 𝕃ⁿ; projective bundle h(ℙ(E)) =
    ⊕_{i<r} h(X)𝕃^i; BLOW-UP h(Bl_Z X) = h(X) ⊕ ⊕_{i=1}^{c−1} h(Z)𝕃^i
    (c = codim Z); h(C) = 𝟙 ⊕ h¹(C) ⊕ 𝕃 with h¹(C) ↔ Jac(C) ⊗ ℚ.
  - EQUIVALENCES rat ⇒ alg ⇒ hom ⇒ num. JANNSEN: numerical motives form a
    semisimple abelian category (and only num gives this). STANDARD
    CONJECTURES: B (Lefschetz), C (Künneth), D (hom = num), Hodge type;
    Grothendieck: they imply the Riemann hypothesis part of Weil. Known:
    C over finite fields (Katz-Messing), B and C for abelian varieties.
  - REALIZATIONS: Betti (ℚ-Hodge structure), de Rham (filtered), ℓ-adic
    (G_ℚ-module), crystalline; comparison isomorphisms give PERIODS
    (ℚ(−1) = h²(ℙ¹) = h¹(𝔾_m): 2πi = ∮dz/z). Tannakian formalism ⇒
    MOTIVIC GALOIS GROUP; Artin motives ↔ G_ℚ, ℚ(1) ↔ 𝔾_m.
  - WEIL CONJECTURES: #X(F_{qⁿ}) = ∑(−1)^i tr(Frobⁿ | H^i); eigenvalues on
    H^i have |α| = q^{i/2}. For a curve Z(C, T) = ∏(1 − αⱼT)/((1 − T)(1 −
    qT)). Cellular X (ℙⁿ, Grassmannians, flag varieties): h(X) = ⊕𝕃^{⊕bᵢ},
    #X(F_q) = ∑b_{2i}qⁱ; [X] ∈ K₀(Var) is the same polynomial in 𝕃.
  - MOTIVIC L-FUNCTIONS AND CONJECTURES: L(M, s) = ∏det(1 − Frob_p p⁻ˢ |
    M_ℓ^{I_p})⁻¹; L(ℚ(n), s) = ζ(s + n); L(h¹(E), s) = L(E, s). DELIGNE:
    critical values are rational multiples of periods (ζ(2m) ∈ ℚπ^{2m},
    ζ(1 − 2m) ∈ ℚ); BEILINSON / BLOCH-KATO for non-critical values
    (ζ(3) and Ext¹(ℚ(0), ℚ(3)) of mixed Tate motives over ℤ, which is
    ℚ for n = 3, 5, 7, ... and 0 for even n ≥ 2); BSD is a special case.
  - FONTAINE-MAZUR: an irreducible ℓ-adic representation of G_ℚ comes
    from geometry iff it is unramified almost everywhere and de Rham at
    ℓ; representations from geometry are pure (Weil numbers) and de Rham.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Using strong approximation for SL₂, show GL₂(𝔸) = GL₂(ℚ)·
    GL₂(ℝ)⁺·K₀(N), where K₀(N) = ∏_p{(a b; c d) ∈ GL₂(ℤ_p) : c ≡ 0 mod
    N}, and explain how f ∈ S_k(Γ₀(N)) becomes a cuspidal automorphic
    form φ_f. (b) Compute μ(N) = [SL₂(ℤ) : Γ₀(N)] for N = 11, 12, p².
    (c) Why is ℚ^×\𝔸_f^×/ℤ̂^× a single point, and what replaces it for a
    number field?
    Answer: (a) proof below; GL₂(ℚ) ∩ GL₂(ℝ)⁺K₀(N) = Γ₀(N). (b) 12, 24,
    p(p + 1). (c) ℤ is a PID; in general it is the class group Cl(F),
    and the adelic quotient is a disjoint union of #Cl⁺(F) locally symmetric
    spaces, indexed via det by the narrow class group.
    Teacher explains: (a) Take g = (g_∞, g_f). Every idele of 𝔸_f is
    r·u with r ∈ ℚ^×, u ∈ ℤ̂^× (factor det g_f = ∏p^{e_p}u_p, put r =
    ±∏p^{e_p}, the sign chosen equal to the sign of det g_∞). Replace g
    by γ₀⁻¹g with γ₀ = diag(r, 1) ∈ GL₂(ℚ) and right-multiply by
    diag(u′⁻¹, 1) ∈ K₀(N) (u′ = r⁻¹det g_f ∈ ℤ̂^×): now det g_f = 1,
    g_f ∈ SL₂(𝔸_f) and det g_∞ > 0. STRONG APPROXIMATION: SL₂(ℚ) is dense
    in SL₂(𝔸_f); the open set g_f(K₀(N) ∩ SL₂(𝔸_f)) contains some γ ∈
    SL₂(ℚ): g_f = γκ. Hence g = γ·(γ⁻¹g_∞)·κ with γ⁻¹g_∞ ∈ GL₂(ℝ)⁺
    (γ ∈ GL₂(ℚ) is embedded diagonally at all places). Intersecting
    GL₂(ℚ) with GL₂(ℝ)⁺K₀(N): an integral-at-all-p matrix with det in
    ℚ_{>0} ∩ ℤ̂^× = {1} and c ≡ 0 mod N, i.e. Γ₀(N). Define φ_f(γg_∞κ) =
    f(g_∞i)j(g_∞, i)^{−k}(det g_∞)^{k/2}, j((a b; c d), z) = cz + d; the
    cocycle rule j(γg, i) = j(γ, g i)j(g, i) and f|_kγ = f make it well
    defined. It is left GL₂(ℚ)-invariant, right K₀(N)-invariant, has
    weight k under SO(2), is killed by the lowering operator (f
    holomorphic) and the constant term ∫_{ℚ\𝔸}φ(n(x)g)dx is the 0-th
    Fourier coefficient of f at the cusp g_∞⁻¹·∞, which vanishes.
    (b) μ(N) = #ℙ¹(ℤ/N) = N∏(1 + 1/p): μ(11) = 12, μ(12) = 12·(3/2)·(4/3)
    = 24 (= #ℙ¹(ℤ/4)·#ℙ¹(ℤ/3) = 6·4), μ(p²) = p²(1 + 1/p) = p(p + 1).
    (c) 𝔸_f^× = ℚ^×_{>0}ℤ̂^× is exactly unique factorization. For F a
    number field, F^×\𝔸_{F,f}^×/𝒪̂_F^× = Cl(F), and GL₂(F)\GL₂(𝔸_F)/
    K is a union of #Cl⁺(F) components (narrow class group, via det).
    Common mistakes: applying strong approximation to GL₂ itself (false:
    det is the obstruction); forgetting (det g_∞)^{k/2} so that the
    centre does not act trivially. Now you try: μ(36). (36·(3/2)·(4/3) =
    72)

D2. (a) Show that for a normalized Hecke eigenform f = ∑aₙqⁿ ∈ S_k(SL₂(ℤ))
    the relations a_mn = a_m a_n ((m, n) = 1) and a_{p^{r+1}} = aₚa_{p^r}
    − p^{k−1}a_{p^{r−1}} give the Euler product L(f, s) = ∏(1 − aₚp⁻ˢ +
    p^{k−1−2s})⁻¹. (b) From τ(2) = −24, τ(3) = 252 compute τ(4), τ(6),
    τ(8), τ(9), τ(12). (c) Check Ramanujan-Petersson at p = 2, 3, 5
    (τ(5) = 4830) and find the Satake angle θ₂ (cos θ₂ = τ(2)/2·2^{11/2}).
    Answer: (a) below. (b) −1472, −6048, 84480, −113643, −370944.
    (c) bounds 90.51, 841.8, 13975.4; cos θ₂ ≈ −0.2652, θ₂ ≈ 0.585π.
    Teacher explains: (a) T_n f = a_n f and T_1 = id give a₁ = 1 and the
    eigenvalue of T_n is the coefficient a_n. Multiplicativity lets us
    write ∑aₙn⁻ˢ = ∏_p ∑_r a_{p^r}p^{−rs}. Put X = p⁻ˢ and A(X) = ∑a_{p^r}
    X^r. The recursion multiplied by X^{r+1} and summed over r ≥ 1 gives
    A(X) − 1 − aₚX = aₚX(A(X) − 1) − p^{k−1}X²A(X), so A(X)(1 − aₚX +
    p^{k−1}X²) = 1. (b) τ(4) = τ(2)² − 2¹¹ = 576 − 2048 = −1472.
    τ(6) = τ(2)τ(3) = −24·252 = −6048. τ(8) = τ(2)τ(4) − 2¹¹τ(2) =
    35328 + 49152 = 84480. τ(9) = 252² − 3¹¹ = 63504 − 177147 = −113643.
    τ(12) = τ(4)τ(3) = −1472·252 = −370944 (all agree with the product
    expansion q∏(1 − qⁿ)²⁴). (c) The bound is 2p^{11/2}: 2·2^{5.5} =
    2·45.255 = 90.51 ≥ 24; 2·3^{5.5} = 2·420.89 = 841.8 ≥ 252; 2·5^{5.5} =
    13975.4 ≥ 4830. Normalizing, αₚ + βₚ = τ(p)/p^{11/2} = 2cos θₚ with
    αₚβₚ = 1, so αₚ = e^{iθₚ} lies on the unit circle: π_p is TEMPERED.
    cos θ₂ = −24/90.51 = −0.2652, θ₂ = 0.5854π. Equivalently the roots of
    X² + 24X + 2048 are −12 ± i√1904, of absolute value √2048 = 2^{5.5}.
    Common mistakes: using p^{k−1} = p¹² instead of p¹¹; applying
    multiplicativity to τ(4) = τ(2)²; forgetting the unitary
    normalization when speaking of Satake parameters. Now you try: τ(16)
    = τ(2)τ(8) − 2¹¹τ(4). (−24·84480 + 2048·1472 = 987136)

D3. (a) State Deligne's theorem on ρ_{f,ℓ} for f = Δ and explain why
    τ(n) ≡ σ₁₁(n) mod 691 means ρ̄_{Δ,691} has semisimplification 1 ⊕ χ¹¹.
    (b) Prove the congruence from G₁₂ = 691/65520 + ∑σ₁₁(n)qⁿ, E₄³ and
    dim M₁₂ = 2. (c) Verify it for n = 2, 3, 5 and give the
    characteristic polynomial of ρ_{Δ,ℓ}(Frob₂) for ℓ ≠ 2.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) G₁₂ − Δ = 691(E₄³/65520 − Δ/91). (c) 2049 −
    (−24) = 2073 = 3·691; 177148 − 252 = 256·691; 48828126 − 4830 =
    70656·691; X² + 24X + 2048.
    Teacher explains: (a) DELIGNE: for each prime ℓ there is a continuous
    irreducible ρ = ρ_{Δ,ℓ}: G_ℚ → GL₂(ℚ_ℓ), unramified at p ≠ ℓ, with
    tr ρ(Frob_p) = τ(p) and det ρ = χ_ℓ¹¹ (so det ρ(Frob_p) = p¹¹); it is
    realized in H¹¹_ét of the Kuga-Sato variety (10-fold fibre power of
    the universal elliptic curve). Reduce a stable lattice mod ℓ: ρ̄ is
    determined up to semisimplification by its traces (Brauer-Nesbitt +
    Chebotarev). If τ(p) ≡ 1 + p¹¹ = tr(1 ⊕ χ¹¹)(Frob_p) and det ≡ p¹¹ for
    all p ≠ 691, then ρ̄^{ss} ≅ 1 ⊕ χ̄¹¹. (b) M₁₂ has basis E₄³, Δ, with
    E₄³ = 1 + 720q + ... . Write G₁₂ − Δ = aE₄³ + bΔ. Constant terms: a =
    691/65520. q¹-terms: 1 − 1 = 720a + b, so b = −691·720/65520 = −691/91.
    Hence G₁₂ − Δ = 691(E₄³/65520 − Δ/91). As 65520 = 2⁴·3²·5·7·13 and 91
    are prime to 691 and E₄³, Δ ∈ ℤ[[q]], every coefficient on the right
    is ≡ 0 mod 691: σ₁₁(n) ≡ τ(n). (c) n = 2: σ₁₁(2) = 2049, 2049 + 24 =
    2073 = 3·691. n = 3: 177148 − 252 = 176896 = 256·691. n = 5:
    48828126 − 4830 = 48823296 = 70656·691. Frob₂: X² − τ(2)X + 2¹¹ =
    X² + 24X + 2048. Common mistakes: expecting ρ̄ itself to split (only
    the semisimplification does; Ribet's method uses a NON-split lattice
    to build a 691-part of a class group, cf. Herbrand-Ribet); mixing
    arithmetic and geometric Frobenius (for the latter the determinant
    is p⁻¹¹ after dualizing). Now you try: check n = 7. (σ₁₁(7) = 1977326744,
    minus τ(7) = −16744: 1977343488 = 2861568·691)

D4. (a) Classify the irreducible admissible representations of
    GL₂(ℚ_p) with a GL₂(ℤ_p)- or Iwahori-fixed vector, and describe the
    newform theorem. (b) Let E = 11a: y² + y = x³ − x² − 10x − 20
    (conductor 11, a₂ = −2, a₁₁ = +1). Identify π₂ and π₁₁ and give the
    Satake parameters at 2. (c) For π of conductor exponent a, tabulate
    dim π^{K₁(pⁿ)} for n = 0, ..., 3 when a = 0, 1, 2.
    Answer: (a) unramified principal series and the characters χ∘det
    (χ unramified) / unramified twists of Steinberg. (b) π₂ =
    π(χ₁, χ₂) unramified with α₂, β₂ = e^{±3πi/4}; π₁₁ = St. (c) a = 0:
    1, 2, 3, 4; a = 1: 0, 1, 2, 3; a = 2: 0, 0, 1, 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let B = upper triangular. For characters χ₁,
    χ₂ of ℚ_p^×, π(χ₁, χ₂) = {f : f((a b; 0 d)g) = χ₁(a)χ₂(d)|a/d|^{1/2}
    f(g)} (normalized induction). It is irreducible unless χ₁χ₂⁻¹ =
    |·|^{±1}; then it has a one-dimensional constituent χ∘det and an
    infinite-dimensional one St ⊗ χ. By Casselman / Borel, π has an
    Iwahori-fixed vector iff it is a constituent of an unramified
    principal series, and a GL₂(ℤ_p)-fixed vector iff it is an
    irreducible unramified principal series or χ∘det with χ unramified
    (the latter is not generic, never a local component of a cusp
    form). Newform theorem: for generic π there is a least a = a(π) with
    π^{K₁(p^a)} ≠ 0, it is one-dimensional, and dim π^{K₁(pⁿ)} = n − a +
    1 for n ≥ a. (b) 2 ∤ 11: π₂ is unramified. With k = 2: α₂ + β₂ =
    a₂/√2 = −√2, α₂β₂ = 1, so α₂, β₂ = (−√2 ± i√2)/2 = e^{±3πi/4}.
    (Unnormalized: roots −1 ± i of X² + 2X + 2.) At 11 the reduction is
    multiplicative since 11 ∥ N; a₁₁ = +1 means SPLIT multiplicative,
    so π₁₁ = St (the twist is trivial); a₁₁ = −1 would give St ⊗ η with η
    the unramified quadratic character. a(St) = 1 matches 11¹ ∥ N.
    (c) directly from max(0, n − a + 1). Common mistakes: forgetting
    the |a/d|^{1/2} normalization, which moves the reducibility points;
    thinking a conductor-1 π with trivial central character could be a
    ramified principal series (π(χ, χ⁻¹) with χ ramified has a = 2a(χ)
    ≥ 2); confusing St with the trivial representation. Now you try: E with
    conductor 2⁵ = 32 (y² = x³ − x): dim π₂^{K₁(2⁷)}. (7 − 5 + 1 = 3)

D5. (a) Compute the dual groups of GL₂, SL₂, PGL₂, Sp₄, SO₅ from root
    data, and describe the Satake parameter of an unramified π_p of
    GL₂(ℚ_p). (b) For Δ, write the local factor L₂(s, Sym²Δ) (unitary
    normalization, X = 2⁻ˢ). (c) Show L(s, π_p × π̃_p) = ζ_p(s)L(s, π_p,
    Sym²) when ω_π = 1, and explain why L(s, π × π̃) has a simple pole at
    s = 1.
    Answer: (a) GL₂(ℂ), PGL₂(ℂ), SL₂(ℂ), SO₅(ℂ), Sp₄(ℂ); t_p =
    diag(αₚ, βₚ). (b) L₂⁻¹ = 1 + (23/32)X − (23/32)X² − X³. (c) below.
    Teacher explains: (a) A root datum (X*, Φ, X_*, Φ^∨) is dualized by
    swapping characters with cocharacters and roots with coroots. GL₂:
    X* = ℤ², roots ±(1, −1), coroots ±(1, −1): self-dual, Ĝ = GL₂(ℂ).
    SL₂: T = {diag(t, t⁻¹)}, X* = ℤ, the root is 2 and the coroot is 1.
    After swapping, the character lattice is ℤ with root 1: that is the
    torus of PGL₂. So SL₂^ = PGL₂(ℂ) and PGL₂^ = SL₂(ℂ). In general
    simply connected ↔ adjoint: Sp₄ (type C₂, simply connected) ↔ SO₅(ℂ)
    (type B₂, adjoint), SO₅ ↔ Sp₄(ℂ). SATAKE: the spherical Hecke algebra
    ℋ(GL₂(ℚ_p)//GL₂(ℤ_p)) ≅ ℂ[x^{±1}, y^{±1}]^{S₂}; on the spherical line
    of π(χ₁, χ₂), T_p = char fn of GL₂(ℤ_p)diag(p, 1)GL₂(ℤ_p) acts by
    p^{1/2}(αₚ + βₚ) and diag(p, p) by αₚβₚ. So π_p ↔ the conjugacy
    class of diag(αₚ, βₚ) in GL₂(ℂ). (b) For Δ at 2: αₚ + βₚ = s₂ :=
    −24/2^{11/2}, s₂² = 576/2048 = 9/32, αβ = 1. Sym² has eigenvalues α²,
    αβ = 1, β², and α² + β² = s₂² − 2 = −55/32. So det(1 − Sym²(t)X) =
    (1 − X)(1 + (55/32)X + X²) = 1 + (23/32)X − (23/32)X² − X³.
    Check: the trace α² + 1 + β² = −23/32 equals τ(4)/2¹¹ = −1472/2048.
    (c) L(s, π_p × π̃_p)⁻¹ = ∏_{i,j}(1 − αᵢαⱼ⁻¹X); with β = α⁻¹ the four
    numbers αᵢ/αⱼ are 1, 1, α², β² = {1} ∪ {α², αβ, β²}. Globally
    L(s, π × π̃) = ζ(s)L(s, Sym²π) = ζ(s)L(s, Ad π). ζ has a simple pole
    at s = 1, and L(s, Ad π) is holomorphic and non-zero at s = 1
    (Gelbart-Jacquet: Ad π is cuspidal on GL₃ for non-CM π, and cuspidal
    L-functions do not vanish on Re s = 1, Jacquet-Shalika); Rankin's
    unfolding identifies the residue with a multiple of ⟨f, f⟩. Common
    mistakes: "SL₂^ = SL₂(ℂ)"; forgetting that the Satake parameter of
    SL₂ lives in PGL₂(ℂ), i.e. only up to scalars; calling Sym²π cuspidal
    when π is dihedral (y² = x³ − x). Now you try: the trace of Sym²(t₃)
    for Δ. (τ(9)/3¹¹ = −113643/177147 = −1403/2187)

D6. (a) State the modularity theorem for E/ℚ and explain aₚ(E) =
    p + 1 − #Ẽ(F_p) as a trace of Frobenius on V_ℓE. (b) For E = 11a:
    y² + y = x³ − x² − 10x − 20 count #Ẽ(F_p) for p = 2, 3 by hand; with
    #Ẽ(F_p) = 5, 10, 10 for p = 5, 7, 13, compare with f = q∏(1 − qⁿ)²
    (1 − q^{11n})². (c) Explain aₚ ≡ 1 + p mod 5 and give the completed
    L-function and its sign.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) 5, 5; a₂, a₃, a₅, a₇, a₁₃ = −2, −1, 1, −2, 4
    = coefficients of f. (c) E(ℚ) ⊇ ℤ/5; Λ(s) = 11^{s/2}(2π)⁻ˢΓ(s)
    L(E, s) = +Λ(2 − s).
    Teacher explains: (a) For p ∤ Nℓ, Frob_p acts on V_ℓE = T_ℓE ⊗ ℚ_ℓ
    with det = p (Weil pairing: Λ²V_ℓE ≅ ℚ_ℓ(1)) and Ẽ(F_p) = ker(1 −
    Frob) on Ẽ(F̄_p), so #Ẽ(F_p) = det(1 − Frob) = 1 − tr + p, tr = aₚ.
    MODULARITY: there is a newform f ∈ S₂(Γ₀(N)) with aₚ(f) = aₚ(E) for
    all p ∤ N (N the conductor), equivalently ρ_{E,ℓ} ≅ ρ_{f,ℓ}, and
    L(E, s) = L(f, s) including bad factors. (b) Mod 2: the equation is
    y² + y = x³ + x². For y ∈ F₂, y² + y = 0; the right side is 0 at x = 0
    and at x = 1. So 4 affine points + ∞: #Ẽ(F₂) = 5, a₂ = 3 − 5 = −2.
    Mod 3: y² + y = x³ + 2x² + 2x + 1. Values of y² + y: y = 0 ↦ 0, 1 ↦ 2,
    2 ↦ 0. The right side is 1, 0, 0 at x = 0, 1, 2; value 0 has two y's,
    value 1 none: 2 + 2 = 4 affine points, #Ẽ(F₃) = 5, a₃ = −1. Then
    a₅ = 6 − 5 = 1, a₇ = 8 − 10 = −2, a₁₃ = 14 − 10 = 4. Expanding,
    ∏(1 − qⁿ)² = 1 − 2q − q² + 2q³ + q⁴ + 2q⁵ − 2q⁶ + ..., so f = q −
    2q² − q³ + 2q⁴ + q⁵ + 2q⁶ − 2q⁷ + ... and the prime coefficients
    match; also a₄ = a₂² − 2 = 2 and a₆ = a₂a₃ = 2 agree (Hecke). (c) E has
    the rational 5-torsion point (5, 5); prime-to-p torsion injects into
    Ẽ(F_p), so 5 | #Ẽ(F_p) = p + 1 − aₚ. In Galois terms ρ̄_{E,5}^{ss} ≅
    1 ⊕ χ₅, an Eisenstein congruence in weight 2. The sign ε = +1 (from
    the Atkin-Lehner eigenvalue −1 on f: ε = −w_N), so the analytic rank
    is even; in fact L(E, 1) ≠ 0 and E(ℚ) = ℤ/5. Common mistakes:
    forgetting the point at infinity; using the Hecke recursion at p = 11
    (bad: a_{11ⁿ} = a₁₁ⁿ = 1). Now you try: 37a: y² + y = x³ − x has
    #Ẽ(F₂) = 5, #Ẽ(F₃) = 7; find a₂, a₃ and check Hasse. (−2, −3;
    |−3| ≤ 2√3 ≈ 3.46)

D7. (a) Show that ℂ_p(1)^{G_K} = 0 implies that the multiplicities in a
    Hodge-Tate decomposition V ⊗ ℂ_p ≅ ⊕ℂ_p(−i)^{mᵢ} are determined by V.
    (b) Using Faltings' decomposition find the HT weights (with
    multiplicity) of H¹ and H² of an abelian surface, H² of a K3 surface,
    H² of a smooth quintic surface in ℙ³ and H³ of a quintic threefold.
    (c) Give the HT weights of ℚ_p(n), V_pE, Sym²V_pE, ρ_{Δ,p}|G_{ℚ_p}
    (det χ¹¹) and its dual.
    Answer: (a) mᵢ = dim_K(V ⊗ ℂ_p(i))^{G_K}. (b) {0², 1²}; {0, 1⁴, 2};
    {0, 1²⁰, 2}; {0⁴, 1⁴⁵, 2⁴}; {0, 1¹⁰¹, 2¹⁰¹, 3}. (c) −n; {−1, 0};
    {−2, −1, 0}; {−11, 0}; {0, 11}.
    Teacher explains: (a) Tensor with ℂ_p(i) and take invariants:
    (V ⊗ ℂ_p(i))^{G_K} = ⊕_j (ℂ_p(i − j)^{G_K})^{m_j}. By TATE,
    ℂ_p(r)^{G_K} = K for r = 0 and 0 for r ≠ 0 (proof: pass to the
    cyclotomic tower K_∞; normalized traces reduce to a finite level
    K_n, where γ ∈ Γ acts with finite order on a fixed element but
    multiplies by χ(γ)^r of infinite order unless r = 0). So only j = i
    survives: dim_K = mᵢ, intrinsic to V. In particular ℂ_p has no
    element t with g t = χ(g)t: there is no "2πi" in ℂ_p (it lives in
    B_dR). (b) FALTINGS: Hᵐ ⊗ ℂ_p ≅ ⊕ H^{m−j}(Ω^j) ⊗ ℂ_p(−j), so weight j
    has multiplicity h^{j,m−j}. Abelian surface: h^{1,0} = h^{0,1} = 2;
    H² = Λ²H¹ has h^{2,0} = 1, h^{1,1} = 2·2 = 4, h^{0,2} = 1 (dimension
    6 = C(4, 2)). K3: 1, 20, 1 (b₂ = 22). Quintic surface: p_g = C(4, 3)
    = 4, b₂ = d³ − 4d² + 6d − 2 = 53, so h^{1,1} = 53 − 8 = 45. Quintic
    threefold: h^{3,0} = 1, h^{2,1} = 101, b₃ = 204. (c) ℚ_p(n) ⊗ ℂ_p =
    ℂ_p(n) = ℂ_p(−i) with i = −n. V_pE = H¹(E)^∨ ≅ H¹(E)(1) shifts {0, 1}
    by −1: {−1, 0}; the sum −1 is the weight of det = ℚ_p(1). Sym² of
    {a, b} is {2a, a + b, 2b} = {−2, −1, 0}. ρ_Δ has weights {0, −11}
    (sum = weight of χ¹¹); the dual, which sits in H¹¹_ét of the
    Kuga-Sato variety, has {0, 11}: only h^{11,0} = h^{0,11} = 1 in the
    Δ-part. Common mistakes: mixing the two sign conventions in one
    computation; giving Betti numbers instead of Hodge numbers; thinking
    every representation is HT (see D8). Now you try: HT weights of H² of
    ℙ¹ × ℙ¹. ({1, 1}: h^{1,1} = 2, h^{2,0} = 0)

D8. (a) Sketch the construction of B_dR⁺ and t = log[ε], and show that
    g(t) = χ(g)t. (b) Compute D_dR(ℚ_p(n)) and D_crys(ℚ_p(n)) with φ.
    (c) Place each representation of G_{ℚ_p} (p odd) in the hierarchy
    crystalline ⊂ semistable ⊂ de Rham ⊂ HT: (i) an unramified character;
    (ii) the quadratic character η of ℚ_p(√p); (iii) V_pE for a Tate
    curve E_q, v(q) > 0; (iv) V = ℚ_p² with g ↦ (1 log χ(g); 0 1);
    (v) a non-split extension 0 → ℚ_p(−1) → V → ℚ_p → 0.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) ℚ_p·t⁻ⁿ ⊗ e, Fil jump at −n, φ = p⁻ⁿ.
    (c) (i) crystalline; (ii) de Rham, not semistable; (iii) semistable,
    not crystalline; (iv) not HT; (v) HT, not de Rham.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let 𝒪^♭ = lim_{x↦x^p} 𝒪_{ℂ_p}/p (the tilt),
    A_inf = W(𝒪^♭) and θ: A_inf → 𝒪_{ℂ_p}, θ(∑pⁿ[xₙ]) = ∑pⁿxₙ^♯, a
    surjection with principal kernel (generated by [p̃] − p, p̃ = (p, p^{1/p},
    ...)). B_dR⁺ = the ker θ-adic completion of A_inf[1/p]: a complete
    DVR with residue field ℂ_p. With ε = (1, ζ_p, ζ_{p²}, ...) ∈ 𝒪^♭,
    θ([ε] − 1) = 0, so t = log[ε] = ∑(−1)^{n+1}([ε] − 1)ⁿ/n converges; one
    checks t ∈ ker θ ∖ (ker θ)², so t is a uniformizer. Since g(ε) = ε^{χ(g)},
    g[ε] = [ε]^{χ(g)} and g(t) = χ(g)t: ℚ_p t ≅ ℚ_p(1) inside B_dR.
    (b) An element b ⊗ e of B_dR ⊗ ℚ_p(n) is invariant iff g(b) =
    χ(g)⁻ⁿb, i.e. b = c t⁻ⁿ with c ∈ B_dR^{G} = ℚ_p. t⁻ⁿ lies in Fil⁻ⁿ
    but not Fil^{−n+1}: HT weight −n. t ∈ B_crys and φ(t) = pt, so
    φ(t⁻ⁿ ⊗ e) = p⁻ⁿ t⁻ⁿ ⊗ e. (c) (i) D_crys is 1-dimensional by Lang's
    theorem over Ŵ(F̄_p)[1/p] ⊂ B_crys. (ii) η is trivial on G_L, L =
    ℚ_p(√p), so η is potentially crystalline hence de Rham (Hilbert 90
    descends D_dR). A semistable character is crystalline (N = 0 on a
    line, since N lowers slope by 1), and a crystalline character of HT
    weight 0 has φ-slope 0, hence is unramified; η is ramified.
    (iii) V_pE_q is the extension of ℚ_p by ℚ_p(1) with Kummer class of
    q in H¹(ℚ_p(1)) = (ℚ_p^×)^∧ ⊗ ℚ_p. H¹_f = units, and q is not a unit,
    so not crystalline; the element log[p̃] ∈ B_st provides the missing
    period, N ≠ 0. (iv) V ⊗ ℂ_p ≅ ℂ_p² would need c ∈ ℂ_p with g(c) − c =
    log χ(g); but log χ spans H¹(G, ℂ_p) ≅ ℚ_p ≠ 0 (Tate). The Sen
    operator is (0 1; 0 0) ≠ 0: not HT. (v) After ⊗ ℂ_p the extension
    class lies in H¹(G, ℂ_p(−1)) = 0, so V ⊗ ℂ_p = ℂ_p(−1) ⊕ ℂ_p: HT with
    weights {0, 1}. But dim H¹_g(ℚ_p(−1)) = h⁰ + dim D_dR/Fil⁰ +
    dim D_crys(ℚ_p(2))^{φ=1} = 0 + 0 + 0 while dim H¹ = 1 (D10), so no
    non-split extension is de Rham. Common mistakes: believing "potentially
    crystalline ⇒ semistable"; forgetting that HT is about ℂ_p-semilinear
    splitting only. Now you try: is V_pE crystalline for E with good
    reduction at p? (yes, by C_crys; conversely crystalline ⇒ good)

D9. (a) Define weakly admissible filtered φ-modules over ℚ_p and show a
    rank-1 module D = ℚ_p e, φe = λe, gr^i ≠ 0, is weakly admissible
    iff v_p(λ) = i. (b) Let D = ℚ_p e₁ ⊕ ℚ_p e₂, φ = diag(α, β), v(α) = 0,
    v(β) = 1, Fil⁰ = D ⊃ Fil¹ = L (a line) ⊃ Fil² = 0. Which L are weakly
    admissible? What if instead φ has no eigenvalue in ℚ_p? (c) Apply to
    D_crys(H¹) of E: y² = x³ − x at p = 5 (a₅ = −2) and p = 3 (a₃ = 0);
    find the unit root mod 25 at p = 5; compare the Newton and Hodge
    polygons of Δ at p = 2 (char poly X² + 24X + 2048, weights 0, 11).
    Answer: (a) below. (b) every L ≠ ℚ_p e₁; if φ has no rational
    eigenvalue, every L. (c) p = 5 ordinary, slopes 0, 1, unit root ≡ 13
    mod 25; p = 3 supersingular, slopes ½, ½; Δ: Newton slopes 3, 8 lie
    above Hodge slopes 0, 11, common endpoint (2, 11).
    Teacher explains: (a) For a filtered φ-module put t_H(D) = ∑ i·dim
    gr^i D and t_N(D) = v_p(det φ). D is WEAKLY ADMISSIBLE if t_H(D) =
    t_N(D) and t_H(D′) ≤ t_N(D′) for every φ-stable subspace D′ with the
    induced filtration. In rank 1 the only subobjects are 0 and D, and
    t_H = i, t_N = v(λ). Check: D_crys(ℚ_p(n)) has λ = p⁻ⁿ and jump −n.
    (b) Since α ≠ β, the φ-stable lines are ℚ_p e₁ (t_N = 0) and ℚ_p e₂
    (t_N = 1); t_H(line) = 1 if it equals L, else 0. Globally t_H = 1 =
    0 + 1 = t_N. The condition fails only for the line ℚ_p e₁ when L =
    ℚ_p e₁ (t_H = 1 > 0). L = ℚ_p e₂ gives a split module (a direct sum of
    two rank-1 admissible pieces); any other L can be moved to ⟨e₁ + e₂⟩
    by the automorphisms diag(a, b) that commute with φ, giving one
    non-split isomorphism class. If φ has no eigenvalue in ℚ_p there
    are no φ-stable lines, so every L works; and since ℚ_p[φ]^× acts
    transitively on lines, all choices are isomorphic. (c) p = 5: char
    poly X² + 2X + 5, 5 ∤ 2: ORDINARY, Newton polygon (0,0), (1,0), (2,1).
    Unit root: α ≡ −2 mod 5; put α = −2 + 5c: α² + 2α + 5 = 5 − 10c +
    25c² ≡ 0 mod 25 ⇔ 1 − 2c ≡ 0 mod 5 ⇔ c ≡ 3; α ≡ 13 mod 25 (check
    169 + 26 + 5 = 200 = 8·25). p = 3: X² + 3, v(roots) = ½: SUPERSINGULAR,
    Newton polygon the segment to (2, 1), strictly above the Hodge polygon
    (0,0), (1,0), (2,1), and −3 is not a square in ℚ₃, so no φ-stable
    line. Δ at 2: v₂(24) = 3, v₂(2048) = 11, so the roots have
    valuations 3 and 8: vertices (0,0), (1,3), (2,11); Hodge: (0,0),
    (1,0), (2,11). Newton above Hodge, as Mazur's inequality demands.
    Common mistakes: forgetting t_H(D) = t_N(D) for D itself; taking
    Newton slopes from aₚ alone without the constant term. Now you try:
    11a at p = 2 (X² + 2X + 2): ordinary or supersingular? (supersingular:
    roots −1 ± i have valuation ½)

D10. (a) State local Tate duality and the local Euler characteristic
    formula, and compute h¹(K, V) for V = ℚ_p, ℚ_p(1), ℚ_p(n) (n ≠ 0, 1),
    V_pE, with d = [K:ℚ_p]. (b) Using dim H¹_f = h⁰(V) + dim D_dR/Fil⁰,
    compute dim H¹_f for the same V and check with Kummer theory and
    E(K). (c) Give all numbers for K = ℚ₅(ζ₅).
    Answer: (a) h¹ = d + 1, d + 1, d, 2d. (b) H¹_f: 1, d, d (n ≥ 2) or
    0 (n ≤ −1), d. (c) d = 4: (5, 1), (5, 4), (4, 4) for ℚ₅(2),
    (4, 0) for ℚ₅(−1), (8, 4) for V₅E.
    Teacher explains: (a) TATE DUALITY: Hⁱ(K, V) × H^{2−i}(K, V*(1)) →
    H²(K, ℚ_p(1)) = ℚ_p is perfect; EULER: h⁰ − h¹ + h² = −d·dim V. For
    V = ℚ_p: h⁰ = 1, h² = h⁰(ℚ_p(1)) = 0 (χ has infinite image on G_K), so
    h¹ = d + 1. For ℚ_p(1): h⁰ = 0, h² = h⁰(ℚ_p) = 1, h¹ = d + 1. For
    n ≠ 0, 1: h⁰ = h² = 0, h¹ = d. For V_pE: V*(1) ≅ V by the Weil
    pairing and h⁰(V_pE) = 0 (E(K) has finite torsion), so h¹ = 2d.
    (b) D_dR(ℚ_p) = K in Fil⁰: quotient 0, dim H¹_f = 1 + 0 = 1 — the
    unramified homomorphisms G_K → ℚ_p. D_dR(ℚ_p(1)) = K t⁻¹, jump −1, so
    Fil⁰ = 0 and dim H¹_f = 0 + d. KUMMER: K^× ≅ ℤ × μ(K) × ℤ_p^d, so
    H¹(ℚ_p(1)) = (K^×)^∧ ⊗ ℚ_p has dimension d + 1 and H¹_f = units has
    dimension d. ℚ_p(n): Fil⁰D_dR = 0 for n ≥ 1, = D_dR for n ≤ −1; so
    H¹_f = H¹ (dim d) for n ≥ 2 and 0 for n ≤ −1. V_pE: HT weights
    {−1, 0}, so D_dR/Fil⁰ has K-dimension 1 and dim H¹_f = d. KUMMER for
    E: E(K) ⊗ ℚ_p ↪ H¹(K, V_pE) lands in H¹_f, and E(K) ≅ 𝒪_K^d × finite
    via the formal logarithm: dimension d, equal. (c) ℚ₅(ζ₅) has degree
    d = 4, giving (h¹, h¹_f) = (5, 1), (5, 4), (4, 4), (4, 0), (8, 4).
    Common mistakes: h⁰(ℚ_p(1)) = 1 when μ_p ⊂ K (no: with ℚ_p-coefficients
    one needs all of μ_{p^∞}); forgetting the h² term. Now you try: dim
    H¹_f(ℚ_p, ℚ_p(1) ⊕ V_pE). (1 + 1 = 2)

D11. (a) Define Chow motives and prove h(ℙ¹) ≅ 𝟙 ⊕ 𝕃 using the
    projectors π₀ = [x₀ × ℙ¹], π₂ = [ℙ¹ × x₀]. State the blow-up formula,
    the standard conjectures and Jannsen's theorem. (b) Give h(X) and
    #X(F_q) for ℙ¹ × ℙ¹, ℙ² blown up in 3 rational points, Gr(2, 4), the
    flag variety Fl₃ and a split cubic surface. (c) Evaluate at q = 2
    and q = 3 and read off Betti numbers and χ_top.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) (q + 1)², q² + 4q + 1, q⁴ + q³ + 2q² + q + 1,
    q³ + 2q² + 2q + 1, q² + 7q + 1. (c) q = 2: 9, 13, 35, 21, 19; q = 3:
    16, 22, 130, 52, 31; χ_top = 4, 6, 6, 6, 9.
    Teacher explains: (a) Objects (X, p, m): X smooth projective, p ∈
    CH^{dim X}(X × X)_ℚ with p∘p = p, m ∈ ℤ; Hom((X, p, m), (Y, q, n)) =
    q∘CH^{dim X + n − m}(X × Y)_ℚ∘p. For product cycles, (c × d)∘(a × b) =
    deg(b·c)(a × d) when b·c is a 0-cycle, and 0 otherwise. π₀∘π₀:
    b·c = ℙ¹·x₀ = x₀, degree 1, so π₀² = π₀; π₂² = π₂ likewise. π₀∘π₂:
    b·c = x₀·x₀ = 0 in CH¹(ℙ¹)·CH¹(ℙ¹) = CH²(ℙ¹) = 0; π₂∘π₀: b·c = ℙ¹ is not
    a 0-cycle, so 0. In CH¹(ℙ¹ × ℙ¹) = ℤh₁ ⊕ ℤh₂ (h₁ = x₀ × ℙ¹, h₂ = ℙ¹ ×
    x₀, h₁h₂ = 1, hᵢ² = 0) the diagonal satisfies Δ·h₁ = Δ·h₂ = 1, so Δ =
    h₁ + h₂ = π₀ + π₂. (ℙ¹, π₀) ≅ h(pt) = 𝟙 and (ℙ¹, π₂) =: 𝕃. BLOW-UP:
    h(Bl_Z X) = h(X) ⊕ ⊕_{i=1}^{c−1} h(Z)𝕃^i. STANDARD CONJECTURES:
    Künneth projectors are algebraic (C), the inverse of Lefschetz is
    algebraic (B), homological = numerical (D), Hodge index positivity.
    JANNSEN: numerical motives form a semisimple abelian category.
    (b) Point counts are traces of Frobenius on the realization, and 𝕃
    contributes q. ℙ¹ × ℙ¹: (𝟙 ⊕ 𝕃)² = 𝟙 ⊕ 2𝕃 ⊕ 𝕃². Blow-up of a point
    (c = 2) adds one 𝕃: Bl₃ℙ² = 𝟙 ⊕ 4𝕃 ⊕ 𝕃². Gr(2, 4): Schubert cells of
    dimensions 0, 1, 2, 2, 3, 4, or the Gaussian binomial (q⁴ − 1)(q³ −
    1)/((q² − 1)(q − 1)) = (q² + 1)(q² + q + 1). Fl₃ = ℙ¹-bundle over ℙ²:
    (1 + q)(1 + q + q²). Split cubic = Bl₆ℙ²: 1 + 7q + q². (c) Plug in;
    check Fl₃(F₃) = #GL₃(F₃)/#B = 11232/216 = 52. The Betti numbers are
    the coefficients (b_{2i} = coefficient of qⁱ, odd ones 0) and
    χ_top = value at q = 1. Common mistakes: forgetting that the
    exceptional curves must be defined over F_q for the count; adding 𝕃
    twice per blown-up point. Now you try: #Gr(2, 5)(F₂). (155)

D12. (a) Describe the Betti, de Rham, ℓ-adic and crystalline
    realizations of ℚ(1) and of h¹(E), and the period 2πi. (b) For E:
    y² = x³ − x over F₅ (a₅ = −2) give Z(E/F₅, T), the Weil numbers and
    #E(F₂₅); explain why the same polynomial appears in every
    realization. (c) Show L(ℚ(n), s) = ζ(s + n), find the critical
    integers of ζ(s), and give ζ(2), ζ(4), ζ(−1), ζ(−3); where does ζ(3)
    fit?
    Answer: (a) below. (b) Z = (1 + 2T + 5T²)/((1 − T)(1 − 5T)); α, β =
    −1 ± 2i, |α| = √5; #E(F₂₅) = 32. (c) critical: 2, 4, 6, ... and −1,
    −3, −5, ...; π²/6, π⁴/90, −1/12, 1/120; ζ(3) is a period of a mixed
    Tate motive, Ext¹(ℚ(0), ℚ(3)) = ℚ.
    Teacher explains: (a) ℚ(−1) = h²(ℙ¹) = h¹(𝔾_m). Betti: H¹(ℂ^×, ℚ) =
    ℚ, dual to the loop γ; de Rham: H¹_dR(𝔾_m/ℚ) = ℚ·dz/z, jump at 1;
    ℓ-adic: H¹_ét = ℚ_ℓ(−1), Frob_p acts by p; crystalline: φ = p. The
    comparison H_dR ⊗ ℂ ≅ H_B ⊗ ℂ is the pairing ∮_γ dz/z = 2πi: the
    period of ℚ(−1) is 2πi, of ℚ(n) it is (2πi)⁻ⁿ. For h¹(E): Betti rank
    2 with Hodge types (1,0), (0,1); de Rham spanned by ω = dx/y and
    x dx/y, Fil¹ = ⟨ω⟩; ℓ-adic V_ℓE^∨; periods Ω₁, Ω₂ = ∫_{γᵢ}ω.
    (b) Z(E/F_q, T) = (1 − aT + qT²)/((1 − T)(1 − qT)) =
    (1 + 2T + 5T²)/((1 − T)(1 − 5T)). Roots of X² + 2X + 5: −1 ± 2i,
    modulus √5 (RIEMANN HYPOTHESIS, weight 1); E has CM by ℤ[i] and
    −1 + 2i is a Gaussian prime of norm 5. #E(F₂₅) = 25 + 1 − (α² + β²),
    α² + β² = (α + β)² − 2αβ = 4 − 10 = −6, so #E(F₂₅) = 32. The
    characteristic polynomial of Frobenius is the same in H¹_ét for all
    ℓ ≠ 5 (it is determined by point counts) and on H¹_crys (Katz-
    Messing): "independence of ℓ", the shadow of one motive h¹(E).
    (c) ℚ(n)_ℓ = ℚ_ℓ(n) and Frob_p acts on it by p⁻ⁿ (geometric Frobenius),
    so the Euler factor is (1 − p⁻ⁿp⁻ˢ)⁻¹: L = ζ(s + n). With Γ_ℝ(s) =
    π^{−s/2}Γ(s/2), Λ(s) = Γ_ℝ(s)ζ(s) = Λ(1 − s); s = m is critical when
    neither Γ_ℝ(s) nor Γ_ℝ(1 − s) has a pole there: exclude 0, −2, −4, ...
    and 1, 3, 5, ...; left: 2, 4, 6, ... and −1, −3, .... DELIGNE:
    ζ(2m) ∈ ℚ·(2πi)^{2m}: ζ(2) = π²/6, ζ(4) = π⁴/90; ζ(−1) = −1/12,
    ζ(−3) = 1/120 (= −B_{2m}/2m). ζ(3) is not critical; Beilinson
    relates it to a regulator on K₅(ℤ) ⊗ ℚ ≅ Ext¹_{MT(ℤ)}(ℚ(0), ℚ(3)) = ℚ.
    Common mistakes: using arithmetic Frobenius (giving ζ(s − n));
    calling ζ(3) a rational multiple of π³. Now you try: for E = 11a,
    BSD predicts L(E, 1)/Ω = #Ш·c₁₁/#E(ℚ)²; with Ш = 1, c₁₁ = 5, #E(ℚ) =
    5, compute it. (5/25 = 1/5, which is the known value)

    h) a(St)          i) a(St ⊗ η)    j) a(St ⊗ χ), a(χ) = 2

    o) π_p for E with non-split multiplicative reduction

       ℚ₂(√2), ℚ₂(√3), ℚ₂(√−3), and of ℚ_p(√p) for p odd

M5. Dual groups and functoriality.
    a) GL₃^      b) SL₃^      c) PGL₃^     d) Sp₆^      e) SO₇^

    Answers:
