M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) The Langlands dual group of SL₂ is SL₂(ℂ).
     b) Strong approximation holds for GL₂ over ℚ.
     c) |τ(p)| ≤ 2p^{11/2} for every prime p.
     d) Every principal series π(χ₁, χ₂) of GL₂(ℚ_p) is irreducible.
     e) The Sym² lift of every cuspidal π on GL₂ is cuspidal.
     f) L(s, π × π′) has a pole at s = 1 for all cuspidal π, π′ on GL₂.
     g) Local Langlands for GL₁(F) is local class field theory.
     h) For p ≥ 5, E/F_p is supersingular iff aₚ = 0.
     i) Every 2-dimensional ℓ-adic representation of G_ℚ unramified
        outside finitely many primes is ρ_{f,ℓ} for a newform f.
     j) ℂ_p contains t ≠ 0 with g(t) = χ(g)t for all g ∈ G_{ℚ_p}.
     k) crystalline ⇒ semistable ⇒ de Rham ⇒ Hodge-Tate.
     l) Every de Rham representation of G_{ℚ_p} is semistable.
     m) Every Hodge-Tate representation is de Rham.
     n) Weakly admissible filtered φ-modules all come from crystalline
        representations.
     o) V_pE is crystalline for every E/ℚ_p with good reduction.
     p) dim H¹(ℚ_p, ℚ_p) = 1.
     q) Chow motives with ℚ-coefficients form a semisimple abelian
        category.
     r) h(ℙⁿ) ≅ 𝟙 ⊕ 𝕃 ⊕ ... ⊕ 𝕃ⁿ.
     s) #Gr(k, n)(F_q) is a polynomial in q with non-negative integer
        coefficients.
     t) L(ℚ(1), s) = ζ(s − 1).
     u) s = 3 is a critical point of ζ(s).
     v) Frobenius eigenvalues on H^i of a smooth projective variety over
        F_q have absolute value q^{i/2}.
     Answers:
       a) F: PGL₂(ℂ)         b) F: det; GL₂(ℚ) is not dense in GL₂(𝔸_f)
       c) T (Deligne)
       d) F: π(|·|^{1/2}, |·|^{−1/2}) (constituents 1∘det and St)
       e) F: dihedral π, e.g. from y² = x³ − x (CM by ℚ(i))
       f) F: only when π′ ≅ π̃ (11a × 37a is entire)
       g) T          h) T (|aₚ| ≤ 2√p < p)
       i) F: 1 ⊕ 1 (even, reducible; ρ_f is odd and irreducible)
       j) F (Tate; t lives in B_dR)       k) T
       l) F: the quadratic character of ℚ_p(√p)
       m) F: a non-split extension of ℚ_p by ℚ_p(−1)
       n) T (Colmez-Fontaine)             o) T (C_crys)
       p) F: 2 (unramified and log χ)
       q) F: only numerical motives are (Jannsen); rat ≠ num already
          for zero-cycles on E × E
       r) T          s) T (Schubert cells)
       t) F: ζ(s + 1)
       u) F: the critical integers are 2, 4, 6, ... and −1, −3, ...
       v) T (Deligne, Weil I)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: run one example through every language of the topic
  before any theory — the curve 11a. Count points mod 2 and 3 by hand,
  match them with q∏(1 − qⁿ)²(1 − q^{11n})², read off the Satake angle
  3π/4 at 2 and the Steinberg component at 11, write its Frobenius
  polynomial X² + 2X + 2, see its Newton polygon (supersingular at 2),
  its Hodge-Tate weights {0, 1} on H¹, and its motive h¹(E) with
  Z(T) = (1 + 2T + 2T²)/((1 − T)(1 − 2T)). Keep a single sign convention
  on the board (here ℚ_p(1) has HT weight −1, geometric Frobenius on
  ℚ_ℓ(n) is p⁻ⁿ), write the unitary normalization αβ = 1 next to every
  Satake computation, and always check Ramanujan (|α| = 1) and
  t_H = t_N as a free sanity test. For period rings, memorize the four
  test cases (ℚ_p(n), ramified quadratic character, Tate curve, the two
  bad extensions) and place every new example between them. For motives
  do the arithmetic of 𝕃 = q first; the categorical language then only
  names what the point counts already show. Harder: prove the Euler
  product and functional equation for L(f, s) via the Mellin transform
  and adelically via Jacquet-Langlands local zeta integrals; derive the
  Satake isomorphism for GL₂ and the formula L(s, π × π̃) = ζ(s)L(s, Ad π);
  prove Tate's theorem H¹(G_K, ℂ_p) = K; show that weak admissibility
  in rank 2 is exactly "Newton above Hodge" and classify all 2-dimensional
  crystalline representations with HT weights {0, k − 1} (the
  Breuil / Berger-Li-Zhu picture); compute H¹_f via the Bloch-Kato
  exponential; prove Manin's identity principle and the blow-up formula
  for Chow motives; and verify the Bloch-Kato conjecture numerically for
  ζ(−1) = −1/12 or for L(11a, 1)/Ω = 1/5.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.4 — ALGEBRAIC TOPOLOGY (ADVANCED): SPECTRAL SEQUENCES, K-THEORY,
STABLE HOMOTOPY THEORY, CHARACTERISTIC CLASSES, COBORDISM
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - SPECTRAL SEQUENCES: pages (Eʳ, dʳ) with dʳ ∘ dʳ = 0 and Eʳ⁺¹ =
    H(Eʳ, dʳ). Homological: dʳ : Eʳ_{p,q} → Eʳ_{p−r,q+r−1}; cohomological:
    dʳ : E_r^{p,q} → E_r^{p+r,q−r+1}. A filtered complex gives one with
    E¹ = homology of the graded pieces and, for a bounded filtration,
    E^∞ = the associated graded of H_*. EXTENSION PROBLEM: E^∞ gives only
    gr H (Z/4 and Z/2 ⊕ Z/2 both have gr = Z/2, Z/2).
  - SERRE SPECTRAL SEQUENCE: F → E → B a fibration, B path-connected with
    π₁(B) acting trivially on H_*(F) (e.g. B simply connected):
    E²_{p,q} = H_p(B; H_q(F)) ⇒ H_{p+q}(E). The cohomology version E₂^{p,q}
    = H^p(B; H^q(F)) is MULTIPLICATIVE (dʳ are derivations). Edge maps are
    H_*(E) → H_*(B) and H_*(F) → H_*(E); TRANSGRESSION dⁿ : E^{0,n−1} →
    E^{n,0}. Consequences: Gysin sequence (sphere bundles, Euler class),
    Wang sequence (fibrations over spheres), χ(E) = χ(B)χ(F).
  - STANDARD SERRE COMPUTATIONS: S¹ → S^{2n+1} → CPⁿ gives H*(CPⁿ) =
    Z[x]/(x^{n+1}); ΩSⁿ → PSⁿ → Sⁿ gives H_k(ΩSⁿ) = Z for (n−1) | k, else
    0; H*(SU(n)) = Λ(x₃, x₅, ..., x_{2n−1}), H*(U(n)) = Λ(x₁, ..., x_{2n−1}),
    H*(Sp(n)) = Λ(x₃, x₇, ..., x_{4n−1}); H*(K(Z, 2)) = Z[x₂]; rationally
    H*(K(Z, n); Q) = Q[x] (n even) or Λ(x) (n odd).
  - SERRE CLASSES AND HOMOTOPY OF SPHERES: mod-𝒞 Hurewicz; π_i(Sⁿ) is
    finite except π_n(Sⁿ) = Z and, for n even, π_{2n−1}(Sⁿ) = Z ⊕ finite.
    The 3-connected cover S³⟨3⟩ (fibre CP^∞) has H_{2k} = Z/k, whence
    π₄(S³) = Z/2 and the first p-torsion of π_*(S³) is Z/p in π_{2p}(S³).
  - ATIYAH-HIRZEBRUCH SPECTRAL SEQUENCE for a generalized cohomology h*:
    E₂^{p,q} = H^p(X; h^q(pt)) ⇒ h^{p+q}(X). For K-theory the rows are
    H^p(X; Z) in even q, 0 in odd q; d₃ = Sq³_Z (integral lift of Sq³),
    the first possible differential. It COLLAPSES if H*(X; Z) is torsion
    free or concentrated in even degrees; rationally always.
  - TOPOLOGICAL K-THEORY: K(X) = Grothendieck group of complex vector
    bundles on compact X (a λ-ring under ⊕, ⊗); K̃(X) = ker(rank);
    K^{−n}(X) = K̃(Σⁿ(X₊)), extended to all n by BOTT PERIODICITY
    K̃(Σ²X) ≅ K̃(X):
    K̃⁰(S^{2n}) = Z, K̃⁰(S^{2n+1}) = 0; K(S²) = Z[H]/(H − 1)² (clutching).
    K(CPⁿ) = Z[H]/(H − 1)^{n+1}; K̃⁰(RP^{2n}) = Z/2ⁿ, K¹(RP^{2n}) = 0.
    REAL KO has period 8: KO^{−n}(pt) = Z, Z/2, Z/2, 0, Z, 0, 0, 0
    (n = 0, ..., 7); K̃O(Sⁿ) = π_{n−1}(O).
  - CHERN CHARACTER ch : K(X) → H^{ev}(X; Q), a ring map with ch(L) =
    e^{c₁(L)}; ch ⊗ Q is an ISOMORPHISM for finite CW X. On S^{2n} the
    image of ch is exactly H^{2n}(S^{2n}; Z) and ch_n = ±c_n/(n − 1)!, so
    c_n of a bundle on S^{2n} is divisible by (n − 1)! (Bott).
  - ADAMS OPERATIONS ψᵏ: ring maps with ψᵏ(L) = Lᵏ for line bundles,
    ψᵏψˡ = ψᵏˡ, ψᵖ(x) ≡ xᵖ (mod p), ψᵏ = kⁿ on K̃(S^{2n}). HOPF INVARIANT
    ONE (Adams, Adams-Atiyah): f : S^{4n−1} → S^{2n} with H(f) odd exists
    only for 2n = 2, 4, 8; hence Rⁿ is a division algebra, S^{n−1} is an
    H-space, or S^{n−1} is parallelizable only for n = 1, 2, 4, 8.
    VECTOR FIELDS: S^{n−1} has exactly ρ(n) − 1 independent vector fields,
    n = 2^{4a+b}·odd (0 ≤ b ≤ 3), ρ(n) = 2ᵇ + 8a (Radon-Hurwitz, Adams).
  - STABLE HOMOTOPY: FREUDENTHAL: π_{n+k}(Sⁿ) → π_{n+k+1}(S^{n+1}) is
    iso for n ≥ k + 2; stable stems π_k^s = Z, Z/2, Z/2, Z/24, 0, 0, Z/2,
    Z/240, (Z/2)², (Z/2)³, Z/6, Z/504 (k = 0, ..., 11), generated in low
    degrees by η ∈ π₁^s, ν ∈ π₃^s, σ ∈ π₇^s (Hopf maps), η² ≠ 0, η³ =
    12ν, ην = 0. SPECTRA: sequences Eₙ with ΣEₙ → Eₙ₊₁; suspension
    spectra Σ^∞X, Eilenberg-MacLane HA, KU (π_* = Z[β^{±1}], |β| = 2),
    KO, Thom spectra MO, MSO, MU. BROWN: cohomology theories are
    represented by spectra.
  - J-HOMOMORPHISM J : π_k(SO) → π_k^s; for k = 4s − 1 its image is
    cyclic of order the denominator of B_{2s}/4s: 24, 240, 504, 480, 264.
  - STEENROD SQUARES Sqⁱ : Hⁿ(−; Z/2) → H^{n+i}: natural, stable, Sq⁰ =
    1, Sqⁿx = x² (|x| = n), Sqⁱx = 0 (i > |x|), CARTAN Sq(xy) = Sq(x)Sq(y),
    ADEM Sqᵃ Sqᵇ = ∑ⱼ C(b−1−j, a−2j) Sq^{a+b−j} Sqʲ (a < 2b). On RP^∞:
    Sqⁱ(xⁿ) = C(n, i) x^{n+i}. Sqⁱ is indecomposable iff i = 2ᵏ.
    ADAMS SPECTRAL SEQUENCE E₂ = Ext_𝒜(H*(X; F₂), F₂) ⇒ π_*(X)^∧₂; for
    the sphere h₀ ↔ 2, h₁ ↔ η, h₂ ↔ ν, h₃ ↔ σ, d₂(h_j) = h₀h_{j−1}² (j ≥ 4).
  - CHARACTERISTIC CLASSES: STIEFEL-WHITNEY wᵢ(E) ∈ Hⁱ(B; Z/2) (w₀ = 1,
    wᵢ = 0 for i > rank, WHITNEY w(E ⊕ F) = w(E)w(F), w₁(γ¹) ≠ 0 on RP^∞);
    CHERN cᵢ ∈ H^{2i}(B; Z) (c(E ⊕ F) = c(E)c(F), c(E*) = ∑(−1)ⁱcᵢ);
    PONTRYAGIN pᵢ(E) = (−1)ⁱc_{2i}(E ⊗ C) ∈ H^{4i}(B; Z); EULER class
    e(E) ∈ Hⁿ(B; Z) of an oriented rank-n bundle, e mod 2 = wₙ, c_top = e,
    e(TM)[M] = χ(M). H*(BO(n); Z/2) = Z/2[w₁, ..., wₙ], H*(BU(n)) =
    Z[c₁, ..., cₙ]. SPLITTING PRINCIPLE: compute with Chern roots.
  - STANDARD VALUES: w(RPⁿ) = (1 + a)^{n+1}; c(CPⁿ) = (1 + x)^{n+1};
    p(CPⁿ) = (1 + x²)^{n+1}; p(HP²) = 1 + 2u + 7u²; w(Sⁿ) = 1 but
    e(TS^{2n}) = 2. M orientable ⇔ w₁ = 0; spin ⇔ w₁ = w₂ = 0. WU:
    w = Sq(v) with ⟨vᵢ ∪ x, [M]⟩ = ⟨Sqⁱx, [M]⟩.
  - APPLICATIONS: dual classes w̄ = w⁻¹; if Mⁿ immerses in R^{n+k} then
    w̄ᵢ = 0 for i > k (and w̄ₖ = 0 if it embeds). RP^{2ʳ} does not immerse in
    R^{2^{r+1}−2}, nor embed in R^{2^{r+1}−1} (Whitney's bounds are sharp).
    S^{2n} is almost complex only for n = 1, 3.
  - GENERA: HIRZEBRUCH SIGNATURE THEOREM σ(M⁴ᵏ) = ⟨L_k(p), [M]⟩, L₁ =
    p₁/3, L₂ = (7p₂ − p₁²)/45; Â₁ = −p₁/24, Â₂ = (−4p₂ + 7p₁²)/5760 (an
    INTEGER on spin manifolds); TODD Td₁ = c₁/2, Td₂ = (c₁² + c₂)/12 (=
    χ(𝒪) on a complex surface). ROKHLIN: a closed smooth spin 4-manifold
    has σ ≡ 0 (mod 16) (K3: σ = −16, Â = 2).
  - COBORDISM: closed Mⁿ, Nⁿ are cobordant if M ⊔ N = ∂W. PONTRYAGIN-THOM:
    Ωₙ^{fr} ≅ π_n^s, Ωₙ^O ≅ πₙ(MO), Ωₙ^{SO} ≅ πₙ(MSO), Ωₙ^U ≅ πₙ(MU).
    Characteristic NUMBERS are cobordism invariants (Pontryagin); THOM:
    M bounds (unoriented) ⇔ all SW numbers vanish, and Ω^O_* = Z/2[xᵢ : i
    ≠ 2ʲ − 1] (dims 1, 0, 1, 0, 2, 1, 3, 1, 5 in n = 0, ..., 8; x_{2k} =
    RP^{2k}). Ω^{SO}_* ⊗ Q = Q[CP², CP⁴, ...]; Ω^{SO}ₙ = Z, 0, 0, 0, Z, Z/2,
    0, 0, Z² (n ≤ 8; Z/2 from the Wu manifold SU(3)/SO(3)); Ω^{Spin}ₙ = Z,
    Z/2, Z/2, 0, Z, 0, 0, 0, Z²; MU_* = Z[x₁, x₂, ...], |xᵢ| = 2i (Milnor,
    Quillen: the universal formal group law).
  - EXOTIC SPHERES: the signature theorem shows the boundary of the E₈
    plumbing is an exotic 7-sphere (p₂ = 45·8/7 ∉ Z); Θ₇ = Z/28
    (Milnor, Kervaire-Milnor).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Use the Serre spectral sequence of S¹ → S^{2n+1} → CPⁿ to prove
    H*(CPⁿ; Z) = Z[x]/(x^{n+1}), |x| = 2. (b) Use the path-loop fibration
    ΩSᵐ → PSᵐ → Sᵐ (m ≥ 2) to compute H_*(ΩSᵐ; Z). (c) Write down the
    Poincaré series of ΩS³ and ΩS⁴ and the rank of H₁₂ for each.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) H_k(ΩSᵐ) = Z if (m − 1) | k, 0 else.
    (c) 1/(1 − t²) and 1/(1 − t³); H₁₂ = Z in both cases (rank 1).
    Teacher explains: (a) CPⁿ is simply connected, so E₂^{p,q} = H^p(CPⁿ)
    ⊗ H^q(S¹): two rows q = 0, 1, and columns in 0 ≤ p ≤ 2n. The only
    possible differential is d₂ : E₂^{p,1} → E₂^{p+2,0}; after that the
    SS has collapsed. The total space S^{2n+1} has H^k = 0 for 0 < k <
    2n + 1. Let u ∈ E₂^{0,1} generate H¹(S¹). Since H¹(S^{2n+1}) = 0 and
    H²(S^{2n+1}) = 0 (n ≥ 1), both E^{0,1} and E^{2,0} must die, so d₂u =
    x generates H²(CPⁿ) (and in particular H²(CPⁿ) = Z; H¹ = 0 similarly).
    Inductively, E₂^{p,1} = H^p(CPⁿ)·u, and by the Leibniz rule d₂(y·u) =
    ±y·x. For 0 ≤ p < 2n − 1 total degree p + 1 ≤ 2n so everything must
    die: multiplication by x : H^p → H^{p+2} is an ISOMORPHISM for 0 ≤ p ≤
    2n − 2. Starting from H⁰ = Z: H^{2k} = Z·xᵏ, H^{odd} = 0, and x^{n+1} =
    0 as H^{2n+2} = 0. The surviving class H^{2n}·u in total degree 2n+1
    is the fundamental class of S^{2n+1}. (b) PSᵐ is contractible and Sᵐ
    is simply connected. E²_{p,q} = H_p(Sᵐ) ⊗ H_q(ΩSᵐ), nonzero only in
    columns p = 0, m. The only differential is dᵐ : E_{m,q} → E_{0,q+m−1},
    and since everything except E_{0,0} must die, dᵐ : H_q(ΩSᵐ) →
    H_{q+m−1}(ΩSᵐ) is an isomorphism for all q ≥ 0 and H_k(ΩSᵐ) = 0 for 0
    < k < m − 1. Hence H_k = Z exactly for k = 0, m−1, 2(m−1), ... (c)
    m = 3: classes in degrees 0, 2, 4, ...: 1/(1 − t²); m = 4: degrees 0,
    3, 6, ...: 1/(1 − t³). 12 is divisible by 2 and by 3: rank 1 each.
    Common mistakes: forgetting that the Serre SS needs trivial π₁(B)
    action (fine here); using the homological SS with a cup product (the
    product lives in cohomology); concluding H*(ΩS³) is polynomial: it is
    a DIVIDED POWER algebra Γ[x] (xᵏ = k!·γₖ, γₖ generating H_{2k}).
    Now you try: compute H*(ΩS³; Q) as a ring and H_k(ΩS⁵) for k ≤ 13.
    (Q[x], |x| = 2; Z in degrees 0, 4, 8, 12, zero otherwise)

D2. (a) Derive the Gysin sequence of an oriented S^{k}-bundle S^k → E →
    B with Euler class e ∈ H^{k+1}(B) from the Serre SS. (b) Compute
    H*(E; Z) for the S¹-bundle over S² with Euler class m (m ≥ 1), and
    identify m = 1, 2. (c) Show H*(SU(3); Z) = Λ(x₃, x₅) and give its
    Poincaré polynomial.
    Answer: (a) ... → H^i(B) →(∪e) H^{i+k+1}(B) → H^{i+k+1}(E) → H^{i+1}(B)
    → ... (b) H⁰ = Z, H¹ = 0, H² = Z/m, H³ = Z; m = 1 gives S³, m = 2
    gives RP³ = SO(3) = unit tangent bundle of S². (c) 1 + t³ + t⁵ + t⁸.
    Teacher explains: (a) With trivial π₁-action (orientable bundle),
    E₂^{p,q} = H^p(B) ⊗ H^q(S^k) has two rows q = 0, k. The only possible
    differential is d_{k+1} : E^{p,k} → E^{p+k+1,0}; on the row q = k it
    is cup product with the transgression of the fibre class, which is
    by definition the Euler class e. So E_∞ has rows coker(∪e) (q = 0)
    and ker(∪e) (q = k), and the short exact sequences 0 → E_∞^{n,0} →
    Hⁿ(E) → E_∞^{n−k,k} → 0 splice into the long exact GYSIN SEQUENCE.
    (b) B = S², k = 1, e = m·[S²]. The sequence gives
       0 → H¹(E) → H⁰(S²) = Z →(·m) H²(S²) = Z → H²(E) → H¹(S²) = 0,
    so H¹(E) = ker(·m) = 0, H²(E) = Z/m. In degree 3: 0 = H³(S²) → H³(E)
    → H²(S²) = Z → H⁴(S²) = 0, so H³(E) = Z. These are the lens spaces
    L(m, 1). For m = 1 we get the Hopf fibration S³ → S²; for m = 2,
    e(TS²) = χ(S²) = 2, and the unit tangent bundle is SO(3) ≅ RP³, with
    H² = Z/2. (c) SU(2) = S³ → SU(3) → S⁵ (SU(3) acts transitively on
    S⁵ ⊂ C³). E₂ = H*(S⁵) ⊗ H*(S³), nonzero at (0,0), (0,3), (5,0), (5,3).
    A differential dʳ from (0, 3) would land in (r, 4 − r); the only
    nonzero column with q = 0 is p = 5 and r = 5 needs 4 − r = 0: no. So
    E₂ = E_∞: H* is free on 1, x₃, x₅, x₃x₅, with x₃² = 0 (odd degree,
    H⁶ = 0 anyway). Poincaré polynomial (1 + t³)(1 + t⁵) = 1 + t³ + t⁵ +
    t⁸. Common mistakes: forgetting the orientability hypothesis in the
    Gysin sequence; getting H¹ and H² of the lens space swapped (torsion
    appears in cohomology one degree above homology: H₁ = Z/m). Now you
    try: the Poincaré polynomial of SU(4) and of U(3). ((1 + t³)(1 + t⁵)
    (1 + t⁷); (1 + t)(1 + t³)(1 + t⁵) = 1+t+t³+t⁴+t⁵+t⁶+t⁸+t⁹)

D3. (a) Let X = S³⟨3⟩ be the 3-connected cover of S³, sitting in a
    fibration K(Z, 2) → X → S³. Compute H*(X; Z) by the Serre SS. (b)
    Deduce π₄(S³) = Z/2 and π₄(S²) = Z/2. (c) Explain why the first
    p-torsion in π_*(S³) occurs in π_{2p}(S³), and find it for p = 3.
    Answer: (a) H^{2k+1}(X) = Z/k for k ≥ 2, all other positive degrees
    0; equivalently H_{2k}(X) = Z/k. (b) Hurewicz on X. (c) via Serre
    classes; π₆(S³) ⊇ Z/3 (in fact π₆(S³) = Z/12).
    Teacher explains: (a) K(Z, 2) = CP^∞, so E₂ = H*(S³) ⊗ Z[u] = Λ[ι] ⊗
    Z[u], |ι| = 3, |u| = 2, with entries at p = 0, 3 and q even. The only
    differential is d₃ : E^{0,2k} → E^{3,2k−2}. Since X is 3-connected,
    H²(X) = H³(X) = 0, forcing d₃u = ι (up to sign). By the Leibniz rule
    d₃(uᵏ) = k·u^{k−1}ι. So d₃ : Z·uᵏ → Z·u^{k−1}ι is multiplication by
    k: injective (kernel 0 for k ≥ 1) with cokernel Z/k. E_∞ has only
    Z/k at (3, 2k − 2), total degree 2k + 1: H^{2k+1}(X) = Z/k (k ≥ 2),
    H^{even > 0} = 0. By universal coefficients H_{2k}(X) = Z/k. (b) X
    is 3-connected, so Hurewicz gives π₄(X) ≅ H₄(X) = Z/2 (k = 2); and
    X → S³ induces isomorphisms on πᵢ for i ≥ 4. So π₄(S³) = Z/2. The
    Hopf fibration S¹ → S³ → S² gives πᵢ(S³) ≅ πᵢ(S²) for i ≥ 3, so
    π₄(S²) = Z/2. (c) Let 𝒞 be the class of finite abelian groups with no
    p-torsion. H_{2k}(X) = Z/k lies in 𝒞 while k < p, so by the mod-𝒞
    Hurewicz theorem πᵢ(X) ∈ 𝒞 for i < 2p and π_{2p}(X) → H_{2p}(X) = Z/p
    is a 𝒞-isomorphism: the p-part of π_{2p}(S³) is Z/p. For
    p = 3: π₆(S³) contains Z/3 (together with Z/4 it is Z/12). Common
    mistakes: writing H^{2k}(X) = Z/k (torsion shifts up one degree in
    cohomology); forgetting the Leibniz rule and taking d₃(uᵏ) = ι u^{k−1}
    (coefficient 1). Now you try: find the 5-primary part of πᵢ(S³) for
    the smallest i where it is nonzero. (i = 10, Z/5; indeed π₁₀(S³) =
    Z/15)

D4. (a) State the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence for complex
    K-theory and explain why it collapses for CPⁿ and for tori. (b) Use
    it to find the ORDER of K̃⁰(RP^{2n}), and use the complexified
    tautological line bundle to determine the group. (c) Compute K⁰, K¹
    of CP³, T³, RP⁴, RP⁵.
    Answer: (a) E₂^{p,q} = H^p(X; K^q(pt)) ⇒ K^{p+q}(X). (b) order 2ⁿ,
    K̃⁰(RP^{2n}) = Z/2ⁿ generated by λ = L_C − 1. (c) CP³: Z⁴, 0; T³: Z⁴,
    Z⁴; RP⁴: Z ⊕ Z/4, 0; RP⁵: Z ⊕ Z/4, Z.
    Teacher explains: (a) K^q(pt) = Z for q even and 0 for q odd (Bott),
    so E₂ is a checkerboard of copies of H^p(X; Z). The differentials d₂
    vanish (they change q by an odd amount), d₃ = Sq³_Z, and all
    differentials are torsion-valued (they vanish after ⊗Q because ch is
    a rational iso). For CPⁿ everything is in even total degree, so no
    differential can hit anything: collapse, K⁰ = Z^{n+1}, K¹ = 0. For
    tori H* is torsion free, so torsion-valued differentials are 0.
    (b) H^p(RP^{2n}; Z) = Z (p = 0), Z/2 (p even, 2 ≤ p ≤ 2n), 0 (p odd).
    All positive-degree classes are in even p and hence even total degree
    so again no differential is possible: E_∞ in total degree 0 has
    Z/2 (p = 2, 4, ..., 2n), n copies, so #K̃⁰ = 2ⁿ, and K¹ = 0. The SS
    cannot decide between Z/2ⁿ and (Z/2)ⁿ: EXTENSION PROBLEM. Let L be
    the real tautological line, λ = L_C − 1. As L ⊗ L is trivial, (λ +
    1)² = 1, so λ² = −2λ, and λᵏ = (−2)^{k−1}λ. λ is detected in filtration
    2 (c₁(L_C) ≠ 0) and λᵏ in filtration 2k, and λⁿ⁺¹ = 0 (filtration >
    2n). So λ generates and 2ⁿλ = ±λ^{n+1} = 0 while 2^{n−1}λ = ±λⁿ ≠ 0:
    the group is cyclic Z/2ⁿ. (c) CP³: H^{ev} = Z⁴, H^{odd} = 0. T³:
    H^{ev} = Z ⊕ Z³, H^{odd} = Z³ ⊕ Z, torsion free. RP⁴ (n = 2): K⁰ = Z ⊕
    Z/4, K¹ = 0. RP⁵: H⁵(RP⁵) = Z (orientable) sits in odd degree, K̃⁰ =
    Z/4 still, K¹ = Z. Common mistakes: forgetting that the AHSS computes
    only an associated graded; claiming K̃⁰(RP²ⁿ) = (Z/2)ⁿ. Now you try:
    K*(S² × S³) and K*(RP²). (K⁰ = Z², K¹ = Z²; K̃⁰(RP²) = Z/2, K¹ = 0)

D5. (a) State Bott periodicity and compute K(S²) using the clutching
    construction. (b) Show that the Chern character of a bundle E on
    S^{2n} with vanishing lower Chern classes is ch(E) = rank + (−1)^{n−1}
    cₙ(E)/(n − 1)!, and deduce that c₃ of a bundle on S⁶ is even and c₄ of
    one on S⁸ is divisible by 6. (c) Show that S⁴ and S⁸ are not almost
    complex.
    Answer: (a) K̃(Σ²X) ≅ K̃(X); K(S²) = Z[H]/(H − 1)². (b) Newton
    identities; Bott integrality. (c) S⁴: p₁ contradiction; S⁸: c₄ = e
    = 2 is not divisible by 3! = 6.
    Teacher explains: (a) Bott: external product with the reduced
    Hopf class β = H − 1 ∈ K̃(S²) gives K̃(X) ≅ K̃(S² ∧ X) = K̃(Σ²X).
    Bundles on S² = D₊ ∪ D₋ are classified by clutching maps S¹ →
    GLₙ(C), i.e. by [S¹, U(n)] = π₁(U(n)) = Z (the degree of det). The
    Hopf bundle H has clutching z, H² has z², and the Laurent polynomial
    argument shows every bundle is H^{a} ⊕ trivial. The relation: the
    clutching diag(z, z) is homotopic to diag(z², 1) (rotate in U(2)),
    so H ⊕ H ≅ H² ⊕ 1, i.e. (H − 1)² = 0. So K(S²) = Z ⊕ Z·β, β² = 0.
    (b) With Chern roots xᵢ: ch = ∑ e^{xᵢ} = r + ∑ₖ sₖ/k!, sₖ = ∑xᵢᵏ.
    Newton: sₙ − c₁s_{n−1} + ... + (−1)^{n−1}n cₙ = 0. On S^{2n} all cᵢ
    with 0 < i < n vanish (H^{2i} = 0), and s_k = 0 for k < n, so sₙ =
    (−1)^{n−1} n cₙ and chₙ = (−1)^{n−1}cₙ/(n−1)!. Bott's theorem says
    ch(K̃(S^{2n})) = H^{2n}(S^{2n}; Z) (it is an iso onto the integral
    lattice, by induction using ch(β) = x and multiplicativity of ch
    under external products). So cₙ ∈ (n − 1)!·Z: n = 3: 2 | c₃; n = 4:
    6 | c₄. (c) An almost complex structure makes TS^{2n} complex with
    cₙ = e(TS^{2n}) = χ(S^{2n}) = 2. S⁸ (n = 4): 2 is not divisible by 6.
    S⁴ (n = 2): here (n − 1)! = 1, so use p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂ = 0 − 4 = −4
    ≠ 0, whereas TS⁴ ⊕ R = trivial gives p₁(S⁴) = 0. Contradiction. Only
    S² and S⁶ survive (both ARE almost complex). Common mistakes: using
    c₁ ≠ 0 on S^{2n}, n ≥ 2 (H² = 0); forgetting the sign convention
    p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂. Now you try: which even-dimensional spheres S^{2n},
    n ≥ 4, can be almost complex? (none: (n − 1)! ∤ 2 for n ≥ 4)

D6. (a) Define the Hopf invariant H(f) of f : S^{4n−1} → S^{2n} and
    compute it for the Hopf map S³ → S² and for the Whitehead product
    [ι, ι] ∈ π₃(S²). (b) Using the Adams operations ψ² and ψ³ in K-theory,
    prove that H(f) odd forces n ∈ {1, 2, 4}. (c) Verify numerically which
    n ≤ 8 satisfy 2ⁿ | 3ⁿ − 1.
    Answer: (a) a² = H(f)·b in H*(C_f); H = 1 and H = 2. (b) proof below.
    (c) n = 1, 2, 4 only.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let C_f = S^{2n} ∪_f e^{4n}, a ∈ H^{2n}, b ∈
    H^{4n} generators; a² = H(f)b. For the Hopf map C_f = CP², a² = b: H
    = 1. For [ι, ι] = 2η (in π₃(S²) = Z the Whitehead square of ι₂ is
    twice the Hopf map) H is additive on π₃(S²), so H = 2; in general
    H([ι₂ₙ, ι₂ₙ]) = 2. (b) In K̃(C_f) = Z{α, β} with α restricting to the
    generator of K̃(S^{2n}) and β coming from K̃(S^{4n}); ch shows α² =
    H(f)β. ψᵏβ = k^{2n}β and ψᵏα = kⁿα + μₖβ. ψ²α ≡ α² (mod 2) gives μ₂
    ≡ H(f) (mod 2), so μ₂ is odd. Now ψ²ψ³ = ψ⁶ = ψ³ψ²:
       ψ²ψ³α = 6ⁿα + (3ⁿμ₂ + 2^{2n}μ₃)β,
       ψ³ψ²α = 6ⁿα + (2ⁿμ₃ + 3^{2n}μ₂)β.
    Equating: 2ⁿ(2ⁿ − 1)μ₃ = 3ⁿ(3ⁿ − 1)μ₂. With μ₂ and 3ⁿ odd, 2ⁿ must
    divide 3ⁿ − 1. The 2-adic valuation v₂(3ⁿ − 1) is 1 for n odd and
    v₂(n) + 2 for n even (lifting the exponent). n odd: need n ≤ 1; n =
    2ʲm: need n ≤ j + 2, true only for n = 2, 4. (c) 3 − 1 = 2 ✓, 9 − 1
    = 8, 4 | 8 ✓, 27 − 1 = 26, 8 ∤ 26, 81 − 1 = 80, 16 | 80 ✓, 242 (n =
    5): 32 ∤, 728 (n = 6): 64 ∤ (728 = 8·91), 2186 (n = 7) no, 6560 (n =
    8): v₂ = 5 < 8 no. So maps of Hopf invariant one exist only for S³ →
    S², S⁷ → S⁴, S¹⁵ → S⁸, and R², R⁴, R⁸ (plus R) are the only division
    algebras. Common mistakes: forgetting that ψ² ≡ squaring holds only
    mod 2; claiming Steenrod squares alone give n = 1, 2, 4 (they give
    only "2n is a power of 2", since Sq^{2n} must be indecomposable;
    ruling out 2n = 16, 32, ... needs secondary operations or K-theory).
    Now you try: H(g ∘ ν) and H(ν ∘ h) for ν : S⁷ → S⁴ the Hopf map and
    g, h maps of degree 3 on S⁴, S⁷. (H(g∘ν) = 9, H(ν∘h) = 3)

D7. (a) State the Freudenthal suspension theorem and use it to show
    that π_{n+1}(Sⁿ) = Z/2 for n ≥ 3. (b) Tabulate π_{n+3}(Sⁿ) for n = 2,
    ..., 6 and say where it becomes stable. (c) Explain the Pontryagin-
    Thom isomorphism Ωₖ^{fr} ≅ π_k^s, identify η and ν with framed
    manifolds, and compute the orders of im J in stems 3, 7, 11, 15.
    Answer: (a) Σ : π_{n+k}(Sⁿ) → π_{n+k+1}(S^{n+1}) is iso for k ≤ n − 2,
    onto for k = n − 1. (b) Z/2, Z/12, Z ⊕ Z/12, Z/24, Z/24: stable from
    n = 5. (c) η = S¹ and ν = S³ with Lie framings; 24, 240, 504, 480.
    Teacher explains: (a) Freudenthal: Sⁿ is (n−1)-connected, so the
    suspension map πᵢ(Sⁿ) → π_{i+1}(S^{n+1}) is an isomorphism for i <
    2n − 1 and onto for i = 2n − 1. With i = n + k: iso for k ≤ n − 2.
    For k = 1: π₃(S²) = Z (Hopf η) → π₄(S³) is ONTO (i = 3 = 2n − 1),
    and π₄(S³) → π₅(S⁴) → ... are isos. From D3, π₄(S³) = Z/2; so π₃(S²)
    = Z maps onto Z/2 (kernel 2Z, generated by the Whitehead square
    [ι₂, ι₂] = 2η) and π_{n+1}(Sⁿ) = Z/2 for all n ≥ 3. (b) For k = 3
    stability starts at n = k + 2 = 5: π₅(S²) = Z/2, π₆(S³) = Z/12, π₇(S⁴)
    = Z ⊕ Z/12 (the Z from the Hopf map ν, of Hopf invariant 1), π₈(S⁵)
    = Z/24 = π₉(S⁶) = π₃^s. Note the unstable Z in π₇(S⁴), as predicted
    by Serre's theorem (n = 4 even, i = 2n − 1). (c) Given f : S^{n+k} →
    Sⁿ smooth and transverse to a point y, M = f⁻¹(y) is a closed
    k-manifold in R^{n+k} ⊂ S^{n+k} with a FRAMED normal bundle (pull
    back a frame at y). Conversely a framed M ⊂ R^{n+k} gives a collapse
    map S^{n+k} → Thom space of the trivial bundle = Σᵏ M₊ → Sⁿ.
    Homotopies ↔ framed cobordisms. Stably Ωₖ^{fr} ≅ π_k^s. k = 0:
    signed points, degree Z. k = 1: the circle with the framing induced
    by the Lie group S¹ = U(1) is η, generating Z/2. k = 3: S³ = SU(2)
    with its left-invariant framing gives ν, generating Z/24. Changing
    a framing on Sᵏ by a map Sᵏ → SO(N) realises J : π_k(SO) → π_k^s.
    im J in stem 4s − 1 is cyclic of order = denominator of B_{2s}/4s:
    s = 1: (1/6)/4 = 1/24; s = 2: (1/30)/8 = 1/240; s = 3: (1/42)/12 =
    1/504; s = 4: (1/30)/16 = 1/480. In stems 3, 7, 11 im J is all of
    π^s; π₁₅^s = Z/480 ⊕ Z/2 is bigger. Common mistakes: reading
    Freudenthal off by one (π₃(S²) → π₄(S³) is only surjective); forgetting
    |B₄| = 1/30 carries a sign; confusing Ω^{fr} with oriented cobordism
    (S³ bounds as an oriented manifold, but not as a framed one). Now you
    try: the order of im J in stem 19. (B₁₀ = 5/66, (5/66)/20 = 1/264:
    order 264)

D8. (a) Show that Sqⁱ(xⁿ) = C(n, i) x^{n+i} in H*(RP^∞; Z/2) and compute
    Sq(x³) and Sq⁴(x⁵). (b) Use Sq² on CP² to show that the stable class
    of η is nonzero in every π_{n+1}(Sⁿ). (c) Use Adem relations to write
    Sq³, Sq⁵, Sq⁶ as sums of composites of Sq^{2ʲ}'s; explain the link with
    the Hopf invariant one problem. (d) Explain how the Adams spectral
    sequence yields the 2-primary part Z/8 of π₃^s.
    Answer: (a) Sq(x³) = x³ + x⁴ + x⁵ + x⁶; Sq⁴(x⁵) = x⁹. (b) Sq² ≠ 0 on
    Σⁿ⁻²CP². (c) Sq³ = Sq¹Sq², Sq⁵ = Sq¹Sq⁴, Sq⁶ = Sq²Sq⁴ + Sq¹Sq⁴Sq¹.
    (d) the tower h₂, h₀h₂, h₀²h₂ in stem 3.
    Teacher explains: (a) |x| = 1, so Sq(x) = x + x² = x(1 + x). Sq is a
    ring map (Cartan), hence Sq(xⁿ) = xⁿ(1 + x)ⁿ = ∑ᵢ C(n, i)x^{n+i}. For
    n = 3: (1 + x)³ = 1 + 3x + 3x² + x³ ≡ 1 + x + x² + x³, so Sq(x³) = x³ +
    x⁴ + x⁵ + x⁶. Sq⁴(x⁵) = C(5, 4)x⁹ = 5x⁹ = x⁹. (b) CP² = S² ∪_η e⁴ and
    Sq²(y) = y² ≠ 0 for y ∈ H²(CP²). Steenrod squares commute with
    suspension, so Sq² : H^n → H^{n+2} is nonzero on Σ^{n−2}CP² = Sⁿ ∪_{Σⁿ⁻²η}
    e^{n+2}. If Σ^{n−2}η were null-homotopic, this complex would be Sⁿ ∨
    S^{n+2} and Sq² would vanish (it is zero on spheres for degree
    reasons, and natural w.r.t. the retractions). So η ≠ 0 in π_1^s. (c)
    Adem with (a, b) = (1, 2): Sq¹Sq² = C(1, 1)Sq³ = Sq³. (1, 4): Sq¹Sq⁴ =
    C(3, 1)Sq⁵ = Sq⁵. (2, 4): Sq²Sq⁴ = C(3, 2)Sq⁶ + C(2, 0)Sq⁵Sq¹ = Sq⁶ +
    Sq⁵Sq¹, so Sq⁶ = Sq²Sq⁴ + Sq¹Sq⁴Sq¹. In general Sqⁱ is decomposable
    unless i = 2ʲ (and Sq^{2ʲ} is indecomposable, as Sq^{2ʲ}(x^{2ʲ}) =
    x^{2^{j+1}} on RP^∞ while decomposables kill x^{2ʲ}). If f : S^{2m−1} →
    Sᵐ has odd Hopf invariant, then Sqᵐ : Hᵐ(C_f) → H^{2m}(C_f) is a² ≠ 0,
    but all lower-degree operations vanish on C_f for degree reasons, so
    Sqᵐ must be indecomposable: m is a power of 2. (d) E₂^{s,t} =
    Ext_𝒜^{s,t}(F₂, F₂), stem t − s. h_j ∈ Ext^{1,2ʲ} corresponds to Sq^{2ʲ}.
    In stem 3 the E₂-term is F₂ in filtrations s = 1, 2, 3 (h₂, h₀h₂,
    h₀²h₂), nothing else; no differentials can hit or leave them in this
    range. Multiplication by h₀ detects multiplication by 2, so the
    three classes form one tower: Z/8. The 3-primary Adams SS gives Z/3,
    and π₃^s = Z/8 ⊕ Z/3 = Z/24. Common mistakes: computing C(n, i) over
    Z instead of mod 2 (C(5, 2) = 10 ≡ 0); applying Adem relations with a
    ≥ 2b (they are only for a < 2b). Now you try: Sq²(x⁶), Sq⁴(x¹²) and
    the Adem expansion of Sq³Sq³. (C(6, 2) = 15 is odd, so Sq²(x⁶) = x⁸;
    Sq⁴(x¹²) = C(12, 4)x¹⁶ = 495x¹⁶ = x¹⁶; Sq³Sq³ = Sq⁵Sq¹)

D9. (a) Prove w(TRPⁿ) = (1 + a)^{n+1}, a ∈ H¹(RPⁿ; Z/2). (b) Decide for
    which n RPⁿ is orientable, spin, and has w(TRPⁿ) = 1. (c) Compute the
    dual class w̄(RPⁿ) and show that RP^{2ʳ} does not immerse in
    R^{2^{r+1}−2}. (d) Apply to RP⁸ and RP⁶.
    Answer: (a) TRPⁿ ⊕ 1 ≅ (n + 1)γ. (b) orientable ⇔ n odd; spin ⇔ n ≡ 3
    (mod 4); w = 1 ⇔ n + 1 = 2ʳ. (c) w̄ = (1 + a)^{−(n+1)}; for n = 2ʳ,
    w̄ = 1 + a + ... + a^{n−1}. (d) RP⁸ does not immerse in R¹⁴; RP⁶ does
    not immerse in R⁹.
    Teacher explains: (a) Lines ℓ ∈ RPⁿ: T_ℓRPⁿ = Hom(ℓ, ℓ^⊥), so TRPⁿ ⊕
    Hom(ℓ, ℓ) = Hom(ℓ, R^{n+1}) = (n + 1)Hom(γ, R) ≅ (n + 1)γ (γ has a
    metric so γ* ≅ γ), and Hom(γ, γ) is trivial. Whitney: w(TRPⁿ) =
    w(γ)^{n+1} = (1 + a)^{n+1}. (b) w₁ = (n + 1)a: zero iff n odd. For n
    odd, w₂ = C(n + 1, 2)a² = ((n + 1)n/2)a², zero iff 4 | n + 1. So RP³,
    RP⁷, RP¹¹ are spin, RP⁵ is orientable but not spin. w = 1 iff all
    C(n + 1, i) are even for 0 < i ≤ n, iff n + 1 is a power of 2 (Lucas):
    RP¹, RP³, RP⁷, RP¹⁵, ... (only RP¹, RP³, RP⁷ are parallelizable). (c)
    If M immerses in R^{n+k} with normal bundle ν then TM ⊕ ν is trivial,
    so w(ν) = w(M)⁻¹ = w̄(M) and w̄ᵢ = 0 for i > k. For n = 2ʳ, (1 + a)ⁿ =
    1 + aⁿ, and in Z/2[a]/(a^{n+1}):
       w̄ = (1 + a)⁻¹(1 + aⁿ)⁻¹ = (1 + a + ... + aⁿ)(1 + aⁿ)
         = 1 + a + a² + ... + a^{n−1}.
    So w̄_{n−1} ≠ 0: the normal bundle of an immersion has rank ≥ n − 1,
    i.e. RPⁿ does not immerse in R^{2n−2}. (Whitney: every Mⁿ immerses in
    R^{2n−1}, so this is sharp.) Since an embedding in R^{2n−1} would have
    a rank n − 1 normal bundle with Euler class, hence w̄_{n−1}, equal to
    0 (the normal bundle of an embedding has vanishing Euler class),
    RP^{2ʳ} does not embed in R^{2n−1} either. (d) RP⁸: w̄₇ ≠ 0, no
    immersion in R¹⁴. RP⁶: w̄ = (1 + a)^{−7} = (1 + a)⁻⁸(1 + a) = (1 +
    a⁸)⁻¹(1 + a) = 1 + a (a⁷ = 0), so w̄ gives only the trivial bound k
    ≥ 1, although w(RP⁶) = (1 + a)⁷ = 1 + a + ... + a⁶ is "large". In
    fact RP⁶ immerses in R⁷: TRP⁶ ⊕ γ ⊕ 1 = 8γ is trivial, because
    8(γ − 1) = 0 in K̃O(RP⁶) = Z/8, and Hirsch's theorem applies. Common
    mistakes: forgetting that (1 + a)^{2ʳ} = 1 + a^{2ʳ} mod 2; computing w̄
    without truncating at a^{n+1}; reading a bound off w instead of w̄.
    Now you try: compute w̄(RP⁵) and w̄(RP⁹). ((1 + a)⁻⁶ = (1 + a²)⁻³ =
    (1 + a²)(1 + a⁸)⁻¹ = 1 + a²; (1 + a)⁻¹⁰ = (1 + a²)⁻⁵ = (1 + a²)³ = 1 +
    a² + a⁴ + a⁶ (truncated at a⁹), so w̄₆ ≠ 0: RP⁹ does not immerse in
    R¹⁴)

D10. (a) For a smooth surface X_d ⊂ CP³ of degree d, compute c(TX_d),
    χ(X_d), the signature σ(X_d) and Td(X_d). (b) Tabulate d = 1, ..., 5
    and check d = 1, 2, 3 against known surfaces. (c) Show the quartic
    (K3) is spin with σ = −16 and relate to Rokhlin's theorem.
    Answer: (a) c = 1 + (4 − d)h + (d² − 4d + 6)h², χ = d(d² − 4d + 6), σ
    = d(4 − d²)/3, Td = χ(𝒪) = (c₁² + c₂)/12. (b) χ = 3, 4, 9, 24, 55;
    σ = 1, 0, −5, −16, −35; Td = 1, 1, 1, 2, 5. (c) c₁ = 0 ⇒ w₂ = 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) TCP³|X = TX ⊕ N, N = 𝒪(d)|X, so c(TX) =
    (1 + h)⁴/(1 + dh) with h the hyperplane class, ⟨h², [X]⟩ = d:
       c(TX) = (1 + 4h + 6h²)(1 − dh + d²h²)
             = 1 + (4 − d)h + (6 − 4d + d²)h².
    χ = ⟨c₂, [X]⟩ = d(d² − 4d + 6). p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂ = [(4 − d)² − 2(d² − 4d +
    6)]h² = (4 − d²)h², and σ = p₁/3 = d(4 − d²)/3. Td₂ = (c₁² + c₂)/12 =
    d[(4 − d)² + d² − 4d + 6]/12 = d(2d² − 12d + 22)/12. (b) d = 1: χ = 3,
    σ = 1: CP² ✓. d = 2: χ = 2·2 = 4, σ = 2·0/3 = 0: CP¹ × CP¹ ✓ (χ = 4,
    form [[0,1],[1,0]]). d = 3: χ = 9, σ = −5: CP² blown up at 6 points,
    χ = 3 + 6, σ = 1 − 6 ✓. d = 4: χ = 24, σ = 4·(−12)/3 = −16. d = 5: χ =
    55, σ = −35. Td: d = 1: (9 + 3)/12 = 1; d = 2: (8 + 4)/12 = 1; d = 3:
    (3 + 9)/12 = 1; d = 4: (0 + 24)/12 = 2; d = 5: (5 + 55)/12 = 5 (=
    1 + p_g, p_g = C(d−1, 3)). Integrality of Td is a check (Riemann-Roch).
    (c) d = 4: c₁ = 0 so w₂ = c₁ mod 2 = 0 and X is simply connected
    (Lefschetz): spin. ROKHLIN: σ ≡ 0 mod 16 for smooth spin closed
    4-manifolds: σ(K3) = −16 ✓, and Â(K3) = −p₁/24 = 48/24 = 2. By
    contrast the E₈ form (even, σ = 8) cannot be the form of a smooth
    closed spin 4-manifold; for simply connected ones even form ⇔ spin,
    so Freedman's topological E₈-manifold is not smoothable. Also d = 3
    is not spin (c₁ = h odd), consistent with σ = −5 ≢ 0 (mod 16), and
    d = 2 is spin with σ = 0. Common mistakes: forgetting the factor
    ⟨h², [X]⟩ = d; using p₁ = c₁² + 2c₂. Now you try: d = 6. (χ = 108,
    σ = −64, Td = 11; spin? c₁ = −2h, even: yes, and 16 | 64 ✓)

D11. (a) Compute the Pontryagin classes of CP², CP⁴ and HP² and verify
    the Hirzebruch signature theorem σ = ⟨L, [M]⟩ for each. (b) Prove that
    the Pontryagin numbers of a closed oriented 8-manifold satisfy 45σ =
    7p₂ − p₁², and hence that 7 divides p₁² + 45σ.
    (c) Explain Milnor's argument that ∂W⁸ of the E₈ plumbing is an exotic
    7-sphere.
    Answer: (a) p(CP²) = 1 + 3x², p(CP⁴) = 1 + 5x² + 10x⁴, p(HP²) = 1 + 2u
    + 7u²; σ = 1 in all cases. (b) L₂ = (7p₂ − p₁²)/45. (c) a smooth
    closing would have p₂ = 360/7.
    Teacher explains: (a) p(CPⁿ) = (1 + x²)^{n+1}. CP²: p₁ = 3x², σ = p₁/3
    = 1 ✓ (form (1)). CP⁴: (1 + x²)⁵ = 1 + 5x² + 10x⁴ (x⁶ = 0 in degree
    12 > 8): p₁² = 25, p₂ = 10 (as numbers), L₂ = (70 − 25)/45 = 1 ✓.
    HP²: p = (1 + u)⁶/(1 + 4u) = (1 + 6u + 15u²)(1 − 4u + 16u²) = 1 + 2u
    + (15 − 24 + 16)u² = 1 + 2u + 7u²; p₁² = 4, p₂ = 7, (49 − 4)/45 = 1 ✓.
    (b) The L-genus is multiplicative with L(x) = x/tanh x; the degree-8
    part in Pontryagin classes is (7p₂ − p₁²)/45. Both sides are
    cobordism invariants and Ω^{SO}₈ ⊗ Q has basis CP⁴, CP² × CP², so it
    suffices to check these: CP² × CP²: p = (1 + 3a²)(1 + 3b²), p₁ =
    3(a² + b²), p₁² = 9·2a²b² → 18, p₂ = 9a²b² → 9, (63 − 18)/45 = 1 = σ
    (σ is multiplicative) ✓. So 7p₂ = 45σ + p₁², i.e. 7 | 45σ + p₁². (c)
    Plumb eight copies of the disc bundle of TS⁴ according to the E₈
    diagram: W⁸ is parallelizable, its intersection form is E₈ (even,
    unimodular, σ = 8), so ∂W = Σ⁷ is a homotopy sphere. If Σ were
    diffeomorphic to S⁷, glue in D⁸: M = W ∪ D⁸ is a closed smooth
    8-manifold with σ = 8 and p₁ = 0 (H⁴(M) → H⁴(W) is an isomorphism,
    and p₁(W) = 0 as W is parallelizable). Then 7p₂ = 45·8 + 0 = 360,
    p₂ = 360/7 ∉ Z: contradiction. So Σ is an exotic sphere;
    Kervaire-Milnor: Θ₇ = Z/28, and Σ generates it. Common mistakes:
    forgetting the sign convention of pᵢ; computing (1 + x²)⁵ without
    truncation; thinking the signature theorem needs M to be complex.
    Now you try: check the signature theorem for S⁴ × S⁴ and for CP² #
    CP². (all pᵢ numbers 0, σ = 0 ✓; p₁ = 3x₁² + 3x₂² → 6 = 3σ with
    σ = 2 ✓)

D12. (a) Prove that the Stiefel-Whitney numbers of a closed manifold
    that bounds are all zero. (b) State Thom's theorem on Ω^O_* and list
    dim_{Z/2} Ωₙ^O for n ≤ 8. (c) Decide which of RP², RP³, RP⁴, CP², CP³,
    S² × S², RP² × RP² bound, and which are cobordant to each other.
    (d) Describe Ω^{SO}₄ and Ω^{SO}₅.
    Answer: (a) Stokes-type argument below. (b) Z/2[xᵢ : i ≠ 2ʲ − 1];
    1, 0, 1, 0, 2, 1, 3, 1, 5. (c) bound: RP³, CP³, S² × S²; CP² ~
    RP² × RP² in Ω₄^O; RP² and RP⁴ do not bound. (d) Z (signature, CP²)
    and Z/2 (w₂w₃, Wu manifold SU(3)/SO(3)).
    Teacher explains: (a) Let M = ∂W, i : M → W. The inward normal gives
    TW|M = TM ⊕ 1, so wᵢ(M) = i*wᵢ(W), and any monomial w_I(M) = i*w_I(W).
    With coefficients Z/2, [M] = ∂[W, M] and ⟨i*y, ∂[W, M]⟩ = ⟨δi*y,
    [W, M]⟩ = 0 as δ ∘ i* = 0 (exactness of H*(W) → H*(M) → H*⁺¹(W, M)).
    (b) THOM: MO splits as a wedge of Eilenberg-MacLane spectra, Ω^O_* =
    π_*(MO) = Z/2[x₂, x₄, x₅, x₆, x₈, x₉, ...] (one generator in each degree
    not of the form 2ʲ − 1), and M bounds iff all SW numbers vanish. So
    dim Ωₙ^O = number of partitions of n into parts not in {1, 3, 7, 15,
    ...}: n = 4: {4}, {2,2}; n = 6: {6}, {4,2}, {2,2,2}; n = 8: {8}, {6,2},
    {4,4}, {4,2,2}, {2,2,2,2}. Answer 1, 0, 1, 0, 2, 1, 3, 1, 5. (c)
    RP²: w = 1 + a + a², w₂ = a² ≠ 0 (also w₁² ≠ 0): does not bound. RP³:
    w = (1 + a)⁴ = 1: bounds. RP⁴: w = (1 + a)⁵ = 1 + a + a⁴: w₄[RP⁴] = 1
    (= χ mod 2): does not bound. CP²: w = 1 + x + x², numbers w₄ = w₂² =
    1: does not bound. CP³: w = (1 + x)⁴ = 1: bounds. S² × S²: w = 1:
    bounds. RP² × RP²: w = (1 + a + a²)(1 + b + b²) gives w₄ = a²b², w₂² =
    (a² + ab + b²)² = a²b², w₁⁴ = a⁴ + b⁴ = 0, w₁²w₂ = 0, w₁w₃ = 0; so the
    numbers (w₄, w₂², w₁⁴, ...) = (1, 1, 0, 0, 0), exactly those of CP²:
    CP² ~ RP² × RP². RP⁴ has w₁⁴ = 1 and differs. (d) Ω^{SO}₄ = Z via
    the signature (a cobordism invariant) with generator CP²; Ω^{SO}₅ =
    Z/2 detected by the de Rham invariant/number w₂w₃, generator the Wu
    manifold SU(3)/SO(3). Common mistakes: expecting the SW numbers of
    an odd-dimensional manifold to vanish automatically (Ω₅^O ≠ 0);
    thinking "χ even ⇒ bounds" (only "χ odd ⇒ does not bound" is valid:
    RP² × RP² ⊔ RP⁴ has χ = 2 and does not bound); forgetting that a
    manifold may bound unoriented but not oriented (CP² # CP²: cobordant
    to 2CP² = 0 in Ω^O, but σ = 2 ≠ 0).
    Now you try: does CP⁴ bound in Ω^O? in Ω^{SO}? (No: χ = 5 is odd so
    w₈ ≠ 0; no: σ = 1)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Spectral sequence bookkeeping and Serre computations.
    a) Bidegree of dʳ in a homological spectral sequence.
    b) Bidegree of d_r in a cohomological spectral sequence.
    c) In a first-quadrant homological SS, for which r is E^r_{p,q}
       certainly equal to E^∞_{p,q}?
    d) E^∞ in total degree 2 is Z/2 at (2, 0) and Z/2 at (0, 2): the
       possible H₂.
    e) E^∞ in total degree 1 is Z at (1, 0) and Z/3 at (0, 1): H₁?
    f) Serre SS of S¹ → S³ → S²: which differential is nonzero?
    g) H*(CP²) from S¹ → S⁵ → CP²: the ring.
    h) H_k(ΩS³) for k = 0, ..., 6.
    i) H_k(ΩS⁵) for k ≤ 12.
    j) H_k(ΩS²) for all k.
    k) χ of the total space of an S²-bundle over a genus-2 surface.
    l) χ(U(3)), χ(SU(3)) and χ(U(3)/T) (T a maximal torus).
    m) Poincaré polynomial of U(2).
    n) Poincaré polynomial of Sp(2); check the top degree = dim Sp(2).
    o) Poincaré polynomial of G₂ = Λ(x₃, x₁₁); dim G₂?
    p) H*(K(Z, 3); Q) and H*(K(Z, 4); Q).
    q) Gysin: H*(E) for the S¹-bundle over S² with Euler class 5.
    r) Gysin: H*(E) for the S³-bundle over S⁴ with Euler class k ≠ 0.
    Answers:
      a) (−r, r − 1)            b) (r, 1 − r)
      c) r > max(p, q + 1)      d) Z/4 or Z/2 ⊕ Z/2 (extension problem)
      e) Z ⊕ Z/3 (the quotient Z is free, so the extension splits)
      f) d² : E²_{2,0} = Z → E²_{0,1} = Z, an isomorphism
      g) Z[x]/(x³), |x| = 2     h) Z, 0, Z, 0, Z, 0, Z
      i) Z in degrees 0, 4, 8, 12; 0 otherwise
      j) Z for every k ≥ 0 (ΩS² ≃ S¹ × ΩS³)
      k) χ(S²)χ(Σ₂) = 2·(−2) = −4
      l) 0, 0, 3! = 6            m) (1 + t)(1 + t³) = 1 + t + t³ + t⁴
      n) (1 + t³)(1 + t⁷) = 1 + t³ + t⁷ + t¹⁰; dim Sp(2) = 10 ✓
      o) 1 + t³ + t¹¹ + t¹⁴; dim G₂ = 14
      p) Λ_Q(x₃) and Q[x₄]
      q) H⁰ = Z, H¹ = 0, H² = Z/5, H³ = Z (lens space L(5, 1))
      r) H⁰ = Z, H³ = 0, H⁴ = Z/|k|, H⁷ = Z, others 0

M2. Homotopy groups from fibrations, Serre classes and Freudenthal.
    a) π₃(S²)        b) π₄(S²)        c) π₅(S²)        d) π₆(S²)
    e) π₄(S³)        f) π₅(S³)        g) π₆(S³)        h) π₇(S³)
    i) π₆(S⁴)        j) π₇(S⁴)        k) π₈(S⁵)        l) π₉(S⁶)
    m) rank of π₁₁(S⁶), π₁₃(S⁷), π₁₅(S⁸)
    n) π₅(CP²) and π₆(CP²)
    o) H₆ and H₈ of the 3-connected cover S³⟨3⟩
    p) smallest i with 5-torsion in π_i(S³); with 7-torsion
    q) π_{n+1}(Sⁿ) for n = 2 and n ≥ 3
    r) for which n is π_{n+4}(Sⁿ) → π_{n+5}(S^{n+1}) an isomorphism?
    Answers:
      a) Z          b) Z/2        c) Z/2        d) Z/12
      e) Z/2        f) Z/2        g) Z/12       h) Z/2
      i) Z/2        j) Z ⊕ Z/12   k) Z/24       l) Z/24
      m) 1, 0, 1 (only π_{2n−1}(Sⁿ), n even, has a Z)
      n) Z and Z/2 (S¹ → S⁵ → CP² gives πᵢ(CP²) = πᵢ(S⁵), i ≥ 3)
      o) Z/3 and Z/4
      p) i = 10 (Z/5 ⊂ π₁₀(S³)); i = 14 (Z/7 ⊂ π₁₄(S³))
      q) Z; Z/2       r) n ≥ 6 (k = 4 ≤ n − 2)

M3. K-groups: give K⁰ and K¹ (unreduced) unless told otherwise.
    a) a point      b) S¹       c) S²       d) S³       e) S^{2n}, S^{2n+1}
    f) T²           g) T³       h) CPⁿ      i) S² × S⁴  j) S¹ × S²

M4. K-theory rings and the Chern character. In K(CPⁿ), H is the
    hyperplane bundle, h = H − 1, x = c₁(H).
    a) ch(H) on CP²          b) ch(h) on CP²        c) ch(h²) on CP²

       on CP²

M10. Genera and the signature theorem.
    a) σ(CP²)          b) σ(CP² # C̄P²)       c) σ(CP² × CP²)

    k) Td of a surface with c₁² = 5, c₂ = 55

M11. Cobordism.
    a) dim Ωₙ^O for n = 0, ..., 10.
    b) degrees of the polynomial generators of Ω^O_* up to 10.
    c) Ωₙ^{SO} for n = 0, ..., 8.

    g) Does it bound (unoriented)? RP⁵, RP⁶, CP⁵, CP², RP² × S¹,
       S² × RP², the Klein bottle, RP² # T².
    h) SW numbers w₁⁴, w₁²w₂, w₁w₃, w₂², w₄ of RP⁴.
    i) The same for RP² × RP² and for CP².
    j) Pontryagin numbers (p₁², p₂) of CP⁴ and of CP² × CP².
    k) Is CP² # CP² an oriented boundary? an unoriented one?
    l) Signature of an oriented boundary.

    n) Generator of Ω₄^{Spin} and its signature.
    Answers:
