M11. Khovanov homology (Poincaré polynomials over Q in t, q).
    a) Kh(O)        b) Kh(2-component unlink)      c) Kh(H₊)
    d) Kh(H₋)       e) Kh(right trefoil)           f) Kh(left trefoil)
    g) Kh(4₁)       h) Kh(T(2, 5))                 i) Kh(T(2, 7))
    j) given Kh(8₁₉) = q⁵ + q⁷ + t²q⁹ + t³q¹³ + t⁴q¹¹ + t⁴q¹³ + t⁵q¹⁵ +
       t⁵q¹⁷, compute χ_q and divide by q + q⁻¹; is 8₁₉ Kh-thin?
    k) chain-group ranks of the standard diagrams of H₊ and of 3₁
    l) chain group of the crossingless diagram of the 3-component unlink
    m) rank Kh(K; Q) for thin knots of determinant 3, 5, 7, 9
    n) Rasmussen s of: right trefoil, left trefoil, 4₁, T(2, 5), T(2, 7),
       T(3, 4), T(3, 5), T(4, 5)
    o) slice genus of T(3, 5), T(4, 5), T(5, 6)
    p) reduced Khovanov homology of the right trefoil, and its rank
    q) the torsion in Kh(right trefoil; Z)
    Answers:
      a) q + q⁻¹                 b) q² + 2 + q⁻²
      c) 1 + q² + t²q⁴ + t²q⁶     d) 1 + q⁻² + t⁻²q⁻⁴ + t⁻²q⁻⁶
      e) q + q³ + t²q⁵ + t³q⁹      f) q⁻¹ + q⁻³ + t⁻²q⁻⁵ + t⁻³q⁻⁹
      g) t⁻²q⁻⁵ + t⁻¹q⁻¹ + q⁻¹ + q + tq + t²q⁵
      h) q³ + q⁵ + t²q⁷ + t³q¹¹ + t⁴q¹¹ + t⁵q¹⁵
      i) q⁵ + q⁷ + t²q⁹ + t³q¹³ + t⁴q¹³ + t⁵q¹⁷ + t⁶q¹⁷ + t⁷q²¹
      j) χ = q⁵ + q⁷ + q⁹ + q¹¹ − q¹⁵ − q¹⁷ = (q + q⁻¹)(q⁶ + q¹⁰ − q¹⁶), i.e.
         V = t³ + t⁵ − t⁸; not thin (rank 8 ≠ det + 1 = 4; three
         diagonals)
      k) 4, 4, 4; 4, 6, 12, 8     l) V^{⊗3}, rank 8
      m) 4, 6, 8, 10 (= det + 1)
      n) 2, −2, 0, 4, 6, 6, 8, 12 (s(T(p, q)) = (p − 1)(q − 1))
      o) 4, 6, 10
      p) q² + t²q⁶ + t³q⁸; 3 = det
      q) Z/2 in bidegree (3, 7)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) The Kauffman bracket is invariant under all Reidemeister moves.
     b) The Jones polynomial distinguishes the two trefoils.
     c) V_K(t) = V_K(t⁻¹) for every knot K.
     d) V(K₁ # K₂) = V(K₁)V(K₂).
     e) span V_K = c(K) for every knot K.
     f) Mutant knots have the same Jones polynomial.
     g) There is a nontrivial link with the Jones polynomial of an
        unlink.
     h) dim TL_n = n! for generic d.
     i) Every invertible solution of the YBE gives representations of
        all braid groups B_n.
     j) U_q(sl₂) is cocommutative.
     k) For generic q, V₁ ⊗ V₁ ≅ V₂ ⊕ V₀ as U_q(sl₂)-modules.
     l) In SU(2)_k the quantum dimension [k + 2] vanishes.
     m) In a 2D TQFT, Z(T²) = dim Z(S¹).
     n) Every commutative Frobenius algebra is semisimple.
     o) 2D Dijkgraaf-Witten theory distinguishes D₄ from Q₈.
     p) 3D Dijkgraaf-Witten theory distinguishes D₄ from Q₈.
     q) In a 3D TQFT with Z(S²) = C, Z(M # N) = Z(M)Z(N).
     r) Verlinde dimensions dim V(Σ_g) are nonnegative integers.
     s) For SU(2)_k, TV(M) = |RT(M)|².
     t) Khovanov homology determines the Jones polynomial.
     u) The Jones polynomial determines Khovanov homology.
     v) Khovanov homology detects the unknot.
     w) The Rasmussen invariant gives g₄(T(p, q)) = (p − 1)(q − 1)/2.
     x) Every surgery presentation of S³ is the empty link.
     Answers:
       a) F: R1 multiplies it by −A^{±3}
       b) T: t + t³ − t⁴ vs t⁻¹ + t⁻³ − t⁻⁴
       c) F: the trefoil
       d) T
       e) F: 8₁₉ (span 5, crossing number 8)
       f) T (e.g. Kinoshita-Terasaka and Conway knots)
       g) T (Eliahou-Kauffman-Thistlethwaite; for knots it is open)
       h) F: dim TL₃ = 5 < 6 = 3! (Catalan numbers)
       i) T
       j) F: Δ(E) = E ⊗ K + 1 ⊗ E ≠ K ⊗ E + E ⊗ 1
       k) T         l) T ([k + 2] = sin π/sin(π/(k + 2)) = 0)
       m) T         n) F: C[x]/(x²), ε(x) = 1
       o) F: both give 4·8^{2g−2} + 4^{2g−2} (same character degrees)
       p) T: Z(RP³) = 3/4 vs 1/4
       q) F: DW, Z/2: Z(RP³ # RP³) = 2 ≠ 1·1 (need division by Z(S³))
       r) T         s) T (Turaev-Walker, Roberts)
       t) T (graded Euler characteristic)
       u) F: 5₁ and 10₁₃₂ have equal V (up to mirror) but different Kh
       v) T (Kronheimer-Mrowka)
       w) T
       x) F: the ±1-framed unknot also gives S³ (Kirby blow-up)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep one worked example alive through the whole topic —
  the trefoil. Compute its bracket by hand from the eight states, then
  recompute it from the braid σ₁³ in TL₂, then from the R-matrix trace
  [3]q⁻³ − q⁻⁹, then read it off Khovanov's cube as the Euler
  characteristic of q + q³ + t²q⁵ + t³q⁹; seeing the SAME polynomial four
  times is what makes "quantum invariant" concrete. Always write down the
  conventions first (A versus t, which smoothing is A, sign of the
  writhe, t^{1/2} = −q or −q⁻¹) and check them on the Hopf link before any
  long computation. For TQFT, draw the surfaces: every 2D identity is a
  picture of pants and caps, Z(Σ_g) = ε(ω^g) is "cap, g handles, cup",
  and Z(Σ × S¹) = dim is "bend and close". For Verlinde and DW, test
  every formula at g = 0 (must give ε(1), e.g. 1/|G| or 1) and g = 1
  (must give dim Z(S¹)). Harder: prove the Kauffman-Murasugi-Thistlethwaite
  theorem via adequacy; derive the universal R-matrix of U_h(sl₂) and
  show it gives Ř on V₁ ⊗ V₁; prove the Frobenius-algebra classification
  of 2D TQFTs from the normal form of surfaces; show TV_{Vec_G} = DW and
  that Rep D(G) is modular; prove the Verlinde formula from the modular
  S-matrix and compute the SU(2)_k genus-2 dimension C(k + 3, 3) in
  closed form; prove invariance of Khovanov homology under R1 and R2 by
  Gaussian elimination, and use Lee's spectral sequence to prove Milnor's
  conjecture g₄(T(p, q)) = (p − 1)(q − 1)/2.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.7 — MIRROR SYMMETRY AND ENUMERATIVE GEOMETRY: CALABI-YAU
MANIFOLDS, GROMOV-WITTEN INVARIANTS, QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY, PERIODS, SYZ,
HOMOLOGICAL MIRROR SYMMETRY, TORIC GEOMETRY AND BATYREV
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - CALABI-YAU n-FOLD: a compact Kähler manifold X with K_X ≅ 𝒪_X (a
    nowhere-vanishing holomorphic n-form Ω) and, in the strict sense,
    h^{p,0} = 0 for 0 < p < n. YAU (Calabi conjecture): each Kähler class
    contains a unique RICCI-FLAT Kähler metric; holonomy ⊆ SU(n). n = 1:
    elliptic curves; n = 2: K3 surfaces (and abelian surfaces if one drops
    h^{1,0} = 0); n = 3: the quintic and friends.
  - ADJUNCTION: K_X = (K_P + X)|_X. A smooth hypersurface of degree d in
    Pⁿ is CY iff d = n + 1 (cubic curve, quartic K3, QUINTIC threefold);
    a complete intersection of degrees d₁, ..., d_k in Pⁿ iff ∑dᵢ = n + 1;
    in weighted P(w₀, ..., w_n) iff d = ∑wᵢ (sextic in P(1,1,1,1,2),
    octic in P(1,1,1,1,4), dectic in P(1,1,1,2,5)).
  - HODGE DIAMOND of a CY 3-fold: h^{0,0} = h^{3,0} = 1, h^{1,0} = h^{2,0}
    = 0, free numbers h^{1,1} (KÄHLER moduli) and h^{2,1} = dim H¹(T_X)
    (COMPLEX moduli, unobstructed by BOGOMOLOV-TIAN-TODOROV); b₂ = h^{1,1},
    b₃ = 2h^{2,1} + 2, χ = 2(h^{1,1} − h^{2,1}). K3: h^{1,1} = 20, χ = 24;
    elliptic curve: all four h^{p,q} = 1, χ = 0.
  - EULER NUMBERS by Chern classes: c(X) = c(TP)/c(N_X), e.g. quintic
    c(X) = (1 + H)⁵/(1 + 5H) = 1 + 10H² − 40H³, ∫_X H³ = 5, so χ = −200;
    LEFSCHETZ gives h^{1,1} = 1, hence h^{2,1} = 101 (= 126 quintic
    monomials − 25 = dim GL₅). Table (h^{1,1}, h^{2,1}, χ): quintic (1,
    101, −200); sextic (1, 103, −204); octic (1, 149, −296); dectic (1,
    145, −288); (3,3) ⊂ P⁵ (1, 73, −144); (2,4) ⊂ P⁵ (1, 89, −176);
    (2,2,3) ⊂ P⁶ (1, 73, −144); (2,2,2,2) ⊂ P⁷ (1, 65, −128); bicubic in
    P²×P² (2, 83, −162); (2,2,2,2) in (P¹)⁴ (4, 68, −128).
  - MIRROR SYMMETRY (Hodge-theoretic): CY 3-folds X, X^∨ with h^{p,q}(X)
    = h^{3−p,q}(X^∨); so h^{1,1} ↔ h^{2,1}, χ(X^∨) = −χ(X), and the Kähler
    moduli of X ↔ complex moduli of X^∨. GREENE-PLESSER: the MIRROR
    QUINTIC is the resolution of {∑xᵢ⁵ − 5ψ∏xᵢ = 0}/G, G ≅ (Z/5)³ of
    order 125; h^{1,1} = 1 + 40 + 60 = 101, h^{2,1} = 1, χ = 200.
    Rigid CY (h^{2,1} = 0) has no naive mirror.
  - LINES ON HYPERSURFACES: the lines on X_d ⊂ Pⁿ are the zeros of a
    section of Sym^d S* on G(2, n+1) (S tautological, rank d + 1);
    expected dimension 2n − 3 − d. SCHUBERT CALCULUS with c₁(S*) = σ₁,
    c₂(S*) = σ₁₁ and the SPLITTING PRINCIPLE: 27 lines on a cubic
    surface, 2875 LINES ON THE QUINTIC, 698005 on a septic in P⁵; lines
    meeting 4 general lines in P³: ∫σ₁⁴ = 2. CLEMENS: the general quintic
    has finitely many smooth rational curves of each degree; conics
    609250 (Katz), twisted cubics 317206375 (Ellingsrud-Strømme).
  - STABLE MAPS: M̄_{g,n}(X, β) (Kontsevich) of VIRTUAL DIMENSION (1 − g)
    (dim X − 3) + ∫_β c₁(X) + n; for M̄_{0,n}(Pʳ, d) it is smooth (as a
    stack) of dimension rd + r + d + n − 3. GROMOV-WITTEN INVARIANTS
    ⟨γ₁, ..., γ_n⟩_{g,β} = ∫_[M̄]^vir ∏ evᵢ*γᵢ. AXIOMS: FUNDAMENTAL CLASS
    (⟨1, γ₂, ..., γ_n⟩_{0,β} = 0 for β ≠ 0), DIVISOR ⟨D, γ...⟩_β =
    (D·β)⟨γ...⟩_β, SPLITTING (⇒ WDVV), degree 0 = classical cup product
    ⟨a, b, c⟩₀ = ∫ a ∪ b ∪ c.
  - CY 3-FOLDS: virtual dimension 0 for every g, n = 0. N_{0,d} counts
    rational curves "virtually"; ASPINWALL-MORRISON: a rigid (−1,−1)
    curve of degree d contributes 1/k³ in degree kd, so N_d = ∑_{k|d}
    n_{d/k}/k³ with INSTANTON (Gopakumar-Vafa) numbers n_d ∈ Z. Quintic:
    n₁ = 2875, n₂ = 609250, n₃ = 317206375, n₄ = 242467530000.
  - KONTSEVICH'S FORMULA: N_d = # rational plane curves of degree d
    through 3d − 1 general points:
    N_d = ∑_{d_A+d_B=d} N_{d_A}N_{d_B} d_A²d_B [d_B C(3d−4, 3d_A−2) − d_A
    C(3d−4, 3d_A−1)], N₁ = 1, giving 1, 1, 12, 620, 87304, 26312976,
    14616808192. Proof: WDVV (associativity) for Γ = ∑N_d e^{dt₁}
    t₂^{3d−1}/(3d−1)!: Γ₂₂₂ = Γ₁₁₂² − Γ₁₁₁Γ₁₂₂. Check N₃ = 12 by the
    pencil of cubics through 8 points: χ(Bl₉P²) = 12 = # nodal members.
  - QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY: a ∘ b = ∑_β ∑_i ⟨a, b, Tᵢ⟩_{0,β} qᵝ Tⁱ ({Tⁱ} the
    Poincaré dual basis): a deformation of ∪, associative BY WDVV (the
    splitting axiom), graded with deg q = c₁·β. QH*(Pⁿ) = Z[H, q]/(H^{n+1}
    − q); QH*(P¹ × P¹): H₁² = q₁, H₂² = q₂; QH*(G(2,4)): σ₁∘σ₂₁ = σ₂₂ + q,
    σ₂∘σ₁₁ = q, σ₁^{∘4} = 2σ₂₂ + 2q. For the quintic: H∘H = (Y(q)/5)H²,
    Y = ⟨H,H,H⟩ = 5 + ∑ n_d d³ q^d/(1 − q^d) = 5 + 2875q + 4876875q² + ...
  - LANDAU-GINZBURG MIRROR of a Fano toric variety: W = x₁ + ... + x_n +
    q/(x₁⋯x_n) for Pⁿ; Jac(W) ≅ QH*(Pⁿ), n + 1 critical points = χ(Pⁿ),
    critical values (n + 1)ζq^{1/(n+1)} = eigenvalues of c₁∘.
  - LOCALIZATION (Atiyah-Bott, BOTT RESIDUE): for a torus T acting on
    compact M with isolated fixed points, ∫_M α = ∑_p α|_p / e_T(T_pM);
    χ(M) = #M^T. On Pⁿ: ∑ᵢ λᵢᵏ/∏_{j≠i}(λᵢ − λⱼ) = 0 (k < n), 1 (k = n),
    h_{k−n}(λ) (k > n). Applied on G(2,4), G(2,5) (and by Kontsevich on
    M̄_{0,0}(P⁴, d) with graph sums) it computes 27, 2875, 609250, ...
  - PERIODS AND PICARD-FUCHS: ϖ(z) = ∫_γ Ω_z satisfies a linear ODE in z
    (Griffiths-Dwork reduction). Mirror quintic: θ⁴ − 5z(5θ + 1)(5θ +
    2)(5θ + 3)(5θ + 4), θ = z d/dz, z = (5ψ)⁻⁵; ϖ₀ = ∑(5n)!/(n!)⁵zⁿ = 1 +
    120z + 113400z² + ...; singular points z = 0 (MAXIMALLY UNIPOTENT
    MONODROMY: exponents 0,0,0,0, (T − 1)⁴ = 0 ≠ (T − 1)³), the CONIFOLD
    z = 5⁻⁵ (exponents 0,1,1,2), z = ∞ (exponents ⅕, ⅖, ⅗, ⅘). Analogues:
    θ² − 3z(3θ+1)(3θ+2) (cubic curve, 1/27), θ³ − 4z(4θ+1)(4θ+2)(4θ+3)
    (quartic K3, 1/256), Legendre λ(1−λ)f″ + (1−2λ)f′ − f/4 = 0.
  - MIRROR MAP AND YUKAWA: ϖ₁ = ϖ₀ log z + 5∑(5n)!/(n!)⁵(H_{5n} − H_n)zⁿ,
    t = ϖ₁/ϖ₀, q = eᵗ = z + 770z² + ..., z = q − 770q² + 171525q³ − ...
    Yukawa Y_{zzz} = 5/(z³(1 − 5⁵z)); in the flat coordinate Y(q) = 5/((1
    − 5⁵z)ϖ₀²)(θ log q)⁻³ reproduces 5 + ∑n_d d³q^d/(1 − q^d) (CANDELAS-DE
    LA OSSA-GREEN-PARKES 1991; proved by GIVENTAL, LIAN-LIU-YAU).
  - SYZ (Strominger-Yau-Zaslow): mirror CY's carry dual special
    Lagrangian torus fibrations X → B ← X^∨ over the same base; fibres
    Tⁿ ↔ dual tori H¹(T, R)/H¹(T, Z) (T-DUALITY, R ↔ 1/R). Elliptic curve:
    T-dualizing the fibre circle exchanges τ and the complexified Kähler
    parameter ρ = B + iA. K3: elliptic fibration with 24 I₁ fibres (χ = 24).
  - HOMOLOGICAL MIRROR SYMMETRY (Kontsevich 1994): D^b Coh(X) ≅ D^π
    Fuk(X^∨). Proved for elliptic curves (Polishchuk-Zaslow): a sheaf of
    (rank, degree) = (r, d) ↔ a Lagrangian line of class (r, d) ∈ H₁(T²);
    𝒪 ↔ horizontal circle, 𝒪_p ↔ vertical circle, 𝒪(dp) ↔ slope d.
    χ(E, F) = r_E d_F − r_F d_E = intersection number; for semistable E,
    F with μ(E) < μ(F), dim Hom = #(L_E ∩ L_F), Ext¹ = 0. Serre functor =
    [1]; Aut D^b(E) → SL(2, Z) (⊗𝒪(p): (r,d) ↦ (r, d+r); Fourier-Mukai:
    (r,d) ↦ (d, −r)) ↔ Dehn twists. Products m₂ = sums over holomorphic
    triangles = theta functions.
  - TORIC GEOMETRY: fans Σ ⊂ N_R ↔ toric varieties X_Σ; polytopes Δ ⊂ M_R
    ↔ polarized ones. Smooth complete: χ(X_Σ) = # maximal cones; a smooth
    toric surface with r rays has K² = 12 − r; (−K)ⁿ = n! vol(Δ_{−K}).
    Lattice points of Δ_{−K} = monomials in H⁰(−K).
  - BATYREV: Δ is REFLEXIVE if it is a lattice polytope containing 0 in
    its interior whose POLAR Δ° = {y : ⟨x, y⟩ ≥ −1 ∀x ∈ Δ} is a lattice
    polytope. Anticanonical hypersurfaces Z_Δ ⊂ P_Δ and Z_{Δ°} ⊂ P_{Δ°}
    are a mirror pair: for n = 4, h^{2,1}(Z_Δ) = l(Δ) − 5 − ∑_{facets Γ}
    l*(Γ) + ∑_{codim 2 Γ} l*(Γ)l*(Γ°), h^{1,1}(Z_Δ) = same with Δ ↔ Δ°.
    Counts (Kreuzer-Skarke): 16 reflexive polygons, 4319 in dim 3,
    473800776 in dim 4. In dim 2: b(Δ) + b(Δ°) = 12.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Let X be a Calabi-Yau 3-fold (K_X trivial, h^{1,0} = h^{2,0} =
    0). Derive its Hodge diamond and show χ(X) = 2(h^{1,1} − h^{2,1}).
    (b) For a smooth quintic X ⊂ P⁴ compute χ(X) with Chern classes, and
    deduce h^{1,1} and h^{2,1}. (c) Confirm h^{2,1} = 101 by counting
    deformations.
    Answer: (a) rows 1; 0 0; 0 h11 0; 1 h21 h21 1; 0 h11 0; 0 0; 1.
    (b) χ = −200, h^{1,1} = 1, h^{2,1} = 101. (c) 126 − 25 = 101.
    Teacher explains: (a) Hodge symmetry h^{p,q} = h^{q,p} and Serre
    duality h^{p,q} = h^{3−p,3−q} hold on any compact Kähler 3-fold. Since
    K_X = 𝒪, h^{3,0} = h⁰(K_X) = 1 = h^{0,0}; by assumption h^{1,0} = h^{2,0}
    = 0, so also h^{0,1}, h^{0,2}, h^{3,1}, h^{3,2}, h^{1,3}, h^{2,3} vanish.
    Moreover Ω²_X ≅ T_X ⊗ K_X ≅ T_X, so h^{2,1} = h¹(T_X). The only free
    numbers are h^{1,1} = h^{2,2} and h^{2,1} = h^{1,2}. Betti numbers:
    b₀ = b₆ = 1, b₁ = b₅ = 0, b₂ = b₄ = h^{1,1}, b₃ = 2 + 2h^{2,1}. Hence
       χ = 1 + 2h^{1,1} − (2 + 2h^{2,1}) + 1 = 2(h^{1,1} − h^{2,1}).
    (b) From 0 → T_X → T_{P⁴}|_X → 𝒪(5)|_X → 0 and c(T_{P⁴}) = (1 + H)⁵:
       c(X) = (1 + H)⁵(1 + 5H)⁻¹
            = (1 + 5H + 10H² + 10H³)(1 − 5H + 25H² − 125H³)
            = 1 + 0·H + (10 − 25 + 25)H² + (10 − 50 + 125 − 125)H³
            = 1 + 10H² − 40H³.
    So c₁ = 0 (CY, as adjunction predicted) and χ = ∫_X c₃ = −40·∫_X H³ =
    −40·5 = −200 (a line meets X in 5 points). By the LEFSCHETZ HYPERPLANE
    THEOREM H²(X) ≅ H²(P⁴) = Z, so h^{1,1} = 1 and by (a) −200 = 2(1 −
    h^{2,1}), h^{2,1} = 101. (c) Quintic forms: C(5 + 4, 4) = 126
    coefficients. Two quintics define isomorphic hypersurfaces iff they
    differ by GL₅ (dim 25, scalars included, which rescale the equation):
    the moduli has dimension 126 − 25 = 101 = h¹(T_X) (all deformations
    of a quintic are again quintics, and BTT says they are unobstructed).
    Common mistakes: forgetting the scalar matrices (126 − 24 is wrong);
    using ∫_{P⁴}H⁴ = 1 instead of ∫_X H³ = deg X = 5; writing b₃ = 2h^{2,1}.
    Now you try: the octic X ⊂ P(1,1,1,1,4) avoids the singular point;
    with c(X) = (1+H)⁴(1+4H)/(1+8H) and ∫_X H³ = 8/4 = 2 find χ and
    h^{2,1}. (c₃ = −148H³, χ = −296, h^{2,1} = 149)

D2. (a) State Hodge-theoretic mirror symmetry for CY 3-folds and explain
    which moduli are exchanged. (b) For each X give the Hodge numbers of
    a mirror and χ(X^∨): (i) the quintic; (ii) the complete intersection
    of two cubics in P⁵ (χ = −144); (iii) the bicubic in P² × P².
    (c) Explain how Greene-Plesser obtain h^{1,1} = 101 for the mirror
    quintic, and why a rigid CY cannot have a Kähler mirror.
    Answer: (a) h^{p,q}(X^∨) = h^{3−p,q}(X). (b) (i) (101, 1), χ = 200;
    (ii) (73, 1), 144; (iii) (83, 2), 162. (c) 1 + 4·10 + 6·10 = 101;
    h^{1,1}(X^∨) = h^{2,1}(X) = 0 contradicts Kähler.
    Teacher explains: (a) Mirror symmetry rotates the diamond by 90°:
    h^{1,1} ↔ h^{2,1}, and χ changes sign. Physically the type IIA string
    on X equals IIB on X^∨: the complexified Kähler moduli space of X
    (dimension h^{1,1}(X)) is identified with the complex structure
    moduli of X^∨ (dimension h^{2,1}(X^∨) = h^{1,1}(X)), near a large
    volume / maximally unipotent point. (b) (ii) h^{1,1} = 1 by Lefschetz,
    −144 = 2(1 − h^{2,1}) gives h^{2,1} = 73; mirror (73, 1), χ = +144.
    Check 73 by counting: a pencil of cubics has 2·56 = 112 coefficients,
    minus GL₂ acting on the pencil (4) and PGL₆ (35): 112 − 4 − 35 = 73.
    (iii) b₂ = 2 (Lefschetz: H² of P² × P²), monomials 10·10 = 100, minus
    2·dim PGL₃ = 16 and the scalar 1: h^{2,1} = 83; χ = 2(2 − 83) = −162,
    mirror (83, 2), χ = 162. (i) mirror (101, 1), χ = 200. (c) G =
    {(ζ^{a₁}, ..., ζ^{a₅}) : ζ⁵ = 1, ∑aᵢ ≡ 0 mod 5}/diagonal ≅ (Z/5)³
    preserves X_ψ = {∑xᵢ⁵ − 5ψ x₁x₂x₃x₄x₅ = 0} and Ω. X_ψ/G has 10 curves
    of A₄ singularities (each resolved by 4 divisors) meeting in 10 points
    of type C³/(Z/5)² (6 more divisors each: the 6 interior lattice points
    of a triangle of side 5): h^{1,1} = 1 + 40 + 60 = 101; the only
    deformation left is ψ, so h^{2,1} = 1. A rigid X has h^{1,1}(X^∨) =
    0, impossible for a Kähler X^∨ (the Kähler class is nonzero in H^{1,1}).
    Common mistakes: exchanging h^{1,1} with h^{2,2} instead of h^{2,1};
    forgetting that χ flips sign; thinking G has order 5⁴ (the diagonal
    acts trivially on P⁴).
    Now you try: give the mirror Hodge numbers of the (2,2,2,2) in (P¹)⁴
    (h^{1,1} = 4, χ = −128) and verify h^{2,1} by counting.
    ((68, 4); 3⁴ − 4·3 − 1 = 68)

D3. (a) Explain why the lines on a hypersurface X_d ⊂ Pⁿ are the zero
    locus of a section of Sym^d S* on G(2, n + 1) and compute the expected
    dimension. (b) Use Schubert calculus on G(2, 4) to show a smooth cubic
    surface contains 27 lines. (c) Show the general quintic 3-fold
    contains 2875 lines, given on G(2, 5): ∫σ₁⁴σ₁₁ = 2, ∫σ₁²σ₁₁² = 1,
    ∫σ₁₁³ = 1.
    Answer: (a) dimension 2(n − 1) − (d + 1) = 2n − 3 − d. (b) 18 + 9 =
    27. (c) 25(48 + 58 + 9) = 2875.
    Teacher explains: (a) A line ℓ = P(W), W ∈ G(2, n+1). A form F of
    degree d restricts to F|_W ∈ Sym^d W*; this is the fibre of Sym^d S*
    at W, so F gives a section s_F, and ℓ ⊂ X ⇔ s_F(W) = 0. The bundle has
    rank d + 1 and G(2, n + 1) has dimension 2(n − 1), so the expected
    dimension is 2n − 3 − d; when it is 0 (d = 2n − 3) and s_F is
    transverse, the number of lines is ∫ c_{d+1}(Sym^d S*). (b) Let x, y
    be the Chern roots of S*, c₁ = x + y = σ₁, c₂ = xy = σ₁₁. Sym³S* has
    roots 3x, 2x + y, x + 2y, 3y, so
       c₄ = 3x·3y·(2x + y)(x + 2y) = 9xy(2x² + 5xy + 2y²)
          = 9c₂(2c₁² + c₂) = 18σ₁²σ₁₁ + 9σ₁₁².
    On G(2,4): σ₁σ₁₁ = σ₂₁, σ₁σ₂₁ = σ₂₂ (the point class), so σ₁²σ₁₁ = 1;
    σ₁₁² = 1 (two general planes in P³ share one line). Total 18 + 9 = 27.
    (c) Sym⁵S* has roots 5x, 4x+y, 3x+2y, 2x+3y, x+4y, 5y. Pair them:
    (4x+y)(x+4y) = 4c₁² + 9c₂, (3x+2y)(2x+3y) = 6c₁² + c₂, so
       c₆ = 25c₂(4c₁² + 9c₂)(6c₁² + c₂) = 25c₂(24c₁⁴ + 58c₁²c₂ + 9c₂²)
          = 25(24σ₁⁴σ₁₁ + 58σ₁²σ₁₁² + 9σ₁₁³) = 25(48 + 58 + 9) = 2875.
    For a GENERAL quintic the section is transverse (all lines are
    isolated with normal bundle 𝒪(−1) ⊕ 𝒪(−1)); special quintics (Fermat)
    contain 1-parameter families of lines, and then 2875 is only a
    virtual count.
    Common mistakes: using S instead of S* (sections of 𝒪(d) are Sym^d of
    the DUAL); forgetting the factor 3x·3y = 9c₂; counting lines on the
    Fermat quintic and expecting 2875.
    Now you try: the lines of P³ meeting 4 general lines are ∫σ₁⁴ = ?
    and the expected dimension of lines on a cubic 3-fold? (2; 2)

D4. (a) Define genus-0 Gromov-Witten invariants and compute the virtual
    dimension of M̄_{0,n}(X, β) for X = P², a CY 3-fold, P¹ × P¹. (b) State
    the divisor axiom and the Aspinwall-Morrison multiple cover formula;
    compute N₂ and N₃ of the quintic. (c) Given the Yukawa coupling of the
    quintic Y = 5 + 2875q + 4876875q² + 8564575000q³ + ..., extract n₁,
    n₂, n₃.
    Answer: (a) 3d − 1 + n; n; 2a + 2b − 1 + n. (b) N₂ = 4876875/8,
    N₃ = 8564575000/27. (c) 2875, 609250, 317206375.
    Teacher explains: (a) A stable map is f : (C, p₁, ..., p_n) → X, C a
    nodal genus-g curve, finitely many automorphisms, f_*[C] = β. With a
    virtual fundamental class of dimension (1 − g)(dim X − 3) + c₁·β + n,
       ⟨γ₁, ..., γ_n⟩_{0,β} = ∫_{[M̄_{0,n}(X,β)]^vir} ev₁*γ₁ ⋯ ev_n*γ_n.
    P²: dim 2 − 3 + 3d + n = 3d − 1 + n; imposing n points (each costs 2)
    needs 2n = 3d − 1 + n, i.e. n = 3d − 1: N_d = ⟨pt^{3d−1}⟩_d. CY3:
    c₁ = 0, dim = 0 + n; with n = 0 the invariant N_{0,β} = deg[M̄]^vir is
    a rational number. P¹ × P¹, β = (a, b): c₁ = 2H₁ + 2H₂, c₁·β = 2a + 2b,
    dim 2a + 2b − 1 + n. (b) DIVISOR AXIOM: for β ≠ 0 and D ∈ H², ⟨D, γ₁,
    ...⟩_β = (∫_β D)⟨γ₁, ...⟩_β (choose where the extra point maps on
    D ∩ curve). On the quintic ⟨H, H, H⟩_d = d³N_d. MULTIPLE COVERS: a
    rigid rational curve C with N = 𝒪(−1)² contributes 1/k³ to N_{k[C]}
    (the obstruction bundle integral over degree-k covers of P¹). So
       N₂ = n₂ + n₁/8 = 609250 + 2875/8 = 4876875/8,
       N₃ = n₃ + n₁/27 = 317206375 + 2875/27 = 8564575000/27.
    (c) Y = 5 + ∑_d n_d d³ q^d/(1 − q^d) = 5 + ∑_m (∑_{d|m} d³n_d)qᵐ.
    q¹: n₁ = 2875. q²: n₁ + 8n₂ = 4876875 ⇒ n₂ = 4874000/8 = 609250.
    q³: n₁ + 27n₃ = 8564575000 ⇒ n₃ = 8564572125/27 = 317206375.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the degree-0 term 5 = ∫H³; dividing by d²
    or k² instead of k³; applying the divisor axiom for β = 0.
    Now you try: n₄ = 242467530000; find the q⁴ coefficient of Y.
    (64n₄ + 8n₂ + n₁ = 15517926796875)

D5. (a) Write the WDVV equation for the quantum potential of P² and
    deduce Kontsevich's recursion for N_d. (b) Compute N₃ and N₄. (c)
    Check N₃ = 12 independently with a pencil of cubics.
    Answer: (a) Γ₂₂₂ = Γ₁₁₂² − Γ₁₁₁Γ₁₂₂ ⇒ the formula in the checklist.
    (b) N₃ = 12, N₄ = 620. (c) χ(Bl₉P²) = 12 nodal fibres.
    Teacher explains: (a) Basis T₀ = 1, T₁ = H, T₂ = pt with coordinates
    t₀, t₁, t₂. The genus-0 potential is Φ = ½(t₀²t₂ + t₀t₁²) + Γ with
       Γ(t₁, t₂) = ∑_{d≥1} N_d e^{dt₁} t₂^{3d−1}/(3d − 1)!
    (divisor axiom turns H-insertions into e^{dt₁}). Associativity of
    (T₁ ∘ T₁) ∘ T₂ = T₁ ∘ (T₁ ∘ T₂) gives Γ₂₂₂ = Γ₁₁₂² − Γ₁₁₁Γ₁₂₂. The
    coefficient of t₂^{3d−4}/(3d − 4)! e^{dt₁} on the left is N_d; the
    right gives products N_{d_A}N_{d_B} with d_A + d_B = d weighted by
    d_A²d_B² C(3d−4, 3d_A−2) − d_A³d_B C(3d−4, 3d_A−1). (b) d = 3,
    C(5, ·): (d_A, d_B) = (1,2): 1·1·[1·4·5 − 1·2·10] = 0; (2,1):
    [4·1·C(5,4) − 8·1·C(5,5)] = 20 − 8 = 12. N₃ = 12. d = 4, C(8, ·):
       (1,3): 12[1·9·8 − 1·3·28] = 12(72 − 84) = −144
       (2,2): [4·4·70 − 8·2·56] = 1120 − 896 = 224
       (3,1): 12[9·1·8 − 27·1·1] = 12·45 = 540
    N₄ = −144 + 224 + 540 = 620. (c) Cubics through 8 general points form
    a pencil with a 9th base point. Blowing up 9 points gives an elliptic
    fibration Bl₉P² → P¹ with χ = 3 + 9 = 12. A smooth fibre is a torus
    (χ = 0), a nodal cubic has χ = 1, so χ = ∑ χ(singular fibres) = # nodal
    cubics = 12; nodal cubics are exactly the rational ones.
    Common mistakes: using 3d points (that over-determines); forgetting
    the reducible curves are excluded automatically by genericity;
    sign errors in the second binomial term.
    Now you try: compute N₅ from N₁, ..., N₄. (87304)

D6. (a) Define the small quantum product and show QH*(Pⁿ) = Q[H, q]/
    (H^{n+1} − q). (b) Write the multiplication table of QH*(P²) and check
    associativity for (H∘H)∘H² = H∘(H∘H²). (c) Show that the mirror
    Landau-Ginzburg model W = x + y + q/(xy) of P² has Jac(W) ≅ QH*(P²)
    and find its critical values.
    Answer: (a) ⟨H^a, H^b, H^c⟩_1 = 1 iff a + b + c = 2n + 1. (b) H∘H =
    H², H∘H² = q, H²∘H² = qH. (c) critical values 3ζq^{1/3}, ζ³ = 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) a ∘ b = ∑_{d≥0} qᵈ ∑_i ⟨a, b, Tᵢ⟩_d Tⁱ with Tⁱ
    dual to Tᵢ (∫TᵢTʲ = δᵢʲ). On Pⁿ, degree d 3-point invariants live on
    a space of dimension n + (n+1)d; three classes H^a, H^b, H^c (a,b,c ≤
    n) have degree a + b + c ≤ 3n, so d = 1 needs a + b + c = 2n + 1 and
    d ≥ 2 is impossible ((n+1)·2 + n > 3n). For d = 1, ⟨H^a, H^b, H^c⟩₁
    counts lines meeting general linear spaces of codimensions a, b, c:
    exactly 1. Hence H∘H^k = H^{k+1} for k < n and H∘Hⁿ = q·1, so
    H^{∘(n+1)} = q. (b) n = 2: H∘H = H² (classical, a+b+c = 5 forces
    c = 3); H∘H²: ⟨H, H², H²⟩₁ = 1 so the q-term is q·(dual of H²) =
    q·1; H²∘H²: ⟨H², H², H⟩₁ = 1 gives q·(dual of H) = qH. Associativity:
    (H∘H)∘H² = H²∘H² = qH and H∘(H∘H²) = H∘q = qH. ✓. (c) ∂_xW = 1 −
    q/(x²y) = 0, ∂_yW = 1 − q/(xy²) = 0 ⇒ x²y = xy² = q ⇒ x = y, x³ = q.
    Jac(W) = C[x^±, y^±]/(x²y − q, xy² − q) ≅ C[x]/(x³ − q) (x ↔ H), which
    is QH*(P²) ⊗ C. Three critical points (= χ(P²)), values W = 3x =
    3ζq^{1/3}: these are the eigenvalues of c₁∘ = 3H∘ on QH*.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the dual basis (H²∘H² is qH, not qH²);
    grading: deg q = c₁·ℓ = n + 1 (in complex units), so H^{n+1} = q is
    homogeneous.
    Now you try: in QH*(G(2,4)) with σ₁∘σ₂₁ = σ₂₂ + q, compute σ₁^{∘4}
    and read off the lines meeting 4 general lines in P³. (2σ₂₂ + 2q; 2)

D7. (a) State the Atiyah-Bott localization formula and derive
    ∑ᵢ λᵢⁿ/∏_{j≠i}(λᵢ − λⱼ) = 1 on Pⁿ. (b) Recompute the 27 lines by Bott's
    residue formula on G(2, 4) with weights w = (0, 1, 2, 3), listing the
    six contributions. (c) Explain how Kontsevich used localization for
    rational curves on the quintic.
    Answer: (a) ∫_M α = ∑_p α|_p/e(T_pM). (b) 0, 0, 0, 90, −315, 252;
    sum 27. (c) graph sums on M̄_{0,0}(P⁴, d) for c_top of E_d, rank 5d+1.
    Teacher explains: (a) For T = (C*)ᵏ acting on compact M with isolated
    fixed points and α ∈ H_T*(M), ∫_M α = ∑_{p∈M^T} α|_p / e_T(T_pM), an
    identity in the fraction field of H_T*(pt) = Q[λ]; a class of degree
    2 dim M gives a number, lower degrees give 0. On Pⁿ with weights λᵢ
    the fixed points are the coordinate points pᵢ; with H|_{pᵢ} = λᵢ and
    tangent weights λᵢ − λⱼ (j ≠ i), ∫Hⁿ = ∑λᵢⁿ/∏(λᵢ − λⱼ) = 1, while ∫Hᵏ
    = 0 for k < n. Taking α = 1 in equivariant Euler class form, χ(M) =
    #M^T: χ(Pⁿ) = n + 1, χ(G(2,4)) = 6. (b) Fixed points of G(2,4): the
    coordinate planes L_{ij}. At L_{ij}: S* has weights −wᵢ, −wⱼ;
    Sym³S* has weights −(3wᵢ), −(2wᵢ + wⱼ), −(wᵢ + 2wⱼ), −(3wⱼ);
    T = S* ⊗ Q has weights w_k − wᵢ, w_k − wⱼ (k ∉ {i, j}). With w =
    (0, 1, 2, 3):
       {0,1}: numerator 0 (weight −3w₀ = 0), denominator 12: 0
       {0,2}: 0/(−3) = 0     {0,3}: 0/4 = 0
       {1,2}: (−3)(−4)(−5)(−6) = 360, denom (−1)(−2)(1)(2) = 4: 90
       {1,3}: (−3)(−5)(−7)(−9) = 945, denom (−1)(−3)(1)(−1) = −3: −315
       {2,3}: (−6)(−7)(−8)(−9) = 3024, denom (−2)(−3)(−1)(−2) = 12: 252
    Sum 90 − 315 + 252 = 27. (c) M̄_{0,0}(P⁴, d) has dimension 5d + 1 and
    carries E_d with fibre H⁰(C, f*𝒪(5)) of rank 5d + 1; N_d = ∫ c_top(E_d)
    (the quintic's equation gives a section vanishing exactly on maps into
    X). The T-fixed stable maps are indexed by decorated graphs (vertices
    ↦ fixed points, edges ↦ multiple covers of coordinate lines), and the
    localization sum reproduced 2875 and 609250 + 2875/8.
    Common mistakes: inconsistent sign conventions between the numerator
    and tangent weights (fix them once and check with ∫σ₁⁴ = 2);
    choosing weights that make a DENOMINATOR vanish (numerators may vanish).
    Now you try: with the same weights compute ∫σ₁⁴ where σ₁ = c₁(S*) =
    −(wᵢ + wⱼ). (1/12 − 16/3 + 81/4 + 81/4 − 256/3 + 625/12 = 2)

D8. (a) Show that ϖ(λ) = ∫₀¹ dx/√(x(1 − x)(1 − λx)), a period of the
    Legendre curve y² = x(x − 1)(x − λ), equals π∑ₙ(C(2n,n)/4ⁿ)²λⁿ and
    satisfies λ(1 − λ)f″ + (1 − 2λ)f′ − f/4 = 0. (b) Rewrite the equation
    with θ = λ d/dλ, find its singular points and local exponents, and
    explain the log solution at λ = 0. (c) For the Hesse-Dwork family
    x³ + y³ + z³ − 3ψxyz with z = (3ψ)⁻³ the holomorphic period is ∑aₙzⁿ,
    aₙ = (3n)!/(n!)³. Find its Picard-Fuchs operator and radius of
    convergence.
    Answer: (a) see below. (b) θ²f = λ(θ + ½)²f; λ = 0, 1, ∞ with
    exponents (0,0), (0,0), (½,½). (c) θ² − 3z(3θ + 1)(3θ + 2); 1/27.
    Teacher explains: (a) Expand (1 − λx)^{−1/2} = ∑C(2n,n)(λx/4)ⁿ and
    integrate termwise with the Beta integral ∫₀¹ x^{n−½}(1 − x)^{−½}dx =
    B(n + ½, ½) = π·C(2n,n)/4ⁿ. Hence ϖ = π∑aₙλⁿ, aₙ = (C(2n,n)/4ⁿ)²:
    a₀, a₁, a₂, a₃ = 1, 1/4, 9/64, 25/256. The ratio aₙ/aₙ₋₁ = ((2n −
    1)/(2n))² gives n²aₙ = (n − ½)²aₙ₋₁, i.e. θ²ϖ = λ(θ + ½)²ϖ (θλⁿ =
    nλⁿ, and λ shifts n by one). (b) Expanding, θ² = λ²D² + λD and
    (θ + ½)² = λ²D² + 2λD + ¼, so θ² − λ(θ + ½)² = λ[λ(1 − λ)D² + (1 −
    2λ)D − ¼]: the hypergeometric equation with a = b = ½, c = 1. The
    singular points are the zeros of the leading coefficient λ(1 − λ)
    and ∞ — exactly where the curve degenerates (λ = 0, 1: nodal; ∞).
    At λ = 0 the indicial equation is r² = 0: a double root, so besides
    ϖ₀ = ₂F₁ there is ϖ₁ = ϖ₀ log λ + (power series); monodromy around 0
    is unipotent, [[1, 2], [0, 1]]-type up to conjugacy (maximally
    unipotent in dimension 2). Their ratio τ = ϖ₁/(πiϖ₀) + const inverts
    to the modular lambda function λ = 16q − 128q² + 704q³ − ..., q =
    e^{πiτ}: this is the MIRROR MAP of the elliptic curve. (c) aₙ/aₙ₋₁ =
    3n(3n − 1)(3n − 2)/n³ ⇒ n²aₙ = 3(3n − 1)(3n − 2)aₙ₋₁ = 3(3(n−1) +
    2)(3(n−1) + 1)aₙ₋₁, giving (θ² − 3z(3θ + 1)(3θ + 2))ϖ = 0. Since
    aₙ₊₁/aₙ → 27, the radius is 1/27: the singular fibre ψ³ = 1 (z =
    1/27) where the cubic acquires 3 nodes.
    Common mistakes: forgetting that θ does not commute with z (zθ = (θ −
    1)z); reading the radius from the ODE's apparent singularities
    instead of the discriminant; confusing λ with its mirror coordinate q.
    Now you try: aₙ = (4n)!/(n!)⁴ (quartic K3 mirror): give a₁, a₂, a₃,
    the operator and the conifold point. (24, 2520, 369600;
    θ³ − 4z(4θ+1)(4θ+2)(4θ+3); z = 1/256)

D9. (a) For the mirror quintic family let ϖ₀ = ∑(5n)!/(n!)⁵zⁿ. Check
    that it is annihilated by L = θ⁴ − 5z(5θ+1)(5θ+2)(5θ+3)(5θ+4) and
    compute the first three coefficients. (b) With ϖ₁ = ϖ₀ log z + ϖ̃₁,
    ϖ̃₁ = 5∑(5n)!/(n!)⁵(H_{5n} − H_n)zⁿ, compute q = exp(ϖ₁/ϖ₀) to order
    z³ and invert. (c) Explain how the Yukawa coupling in q gives the
    instanton numbers, and describe the singularities of L.
    Answer: (a) 1, 120, 113400. (b) q = z + 770z² + 1014275z³ + ...,
    z = q − 770q² + 171525q³ − ... (c) Y(q) = 5 + 2875q + 4876875q² + ...
    Teacher explains: (a) With aₙ = (5n)!/(n!)⁵: aₙ/aₙ₋₁ = 5n(5n−1)(5n−2)
    (5n−3)(5n−4)/n⁵, so n⁴aₙ = 5(5n−1)(5n−2)(5n−3)(5n−4)aₙ₋₁, which is the
    coefficient identity for θ⁴ϖ₀ = 5z(5θ+1)⋯(5θ+4)ϖ₀ (on z^{n−1} the
    right side gives 5(5n−4)(5n−3)(5n−2)(5n−1)). a₁ = 5!/1 = 120, a₂ =
    10!/2⁵ = 3628800/32 = 113400. (b) H₅ − H₁ = 137/60 − 1 = 77/60, so
    the z-term of ϖ̃₁ is 5·120·77/60 = 770. H₁₀ − H₂ = 7381/2520 − 3/2 =
    3601/2520, z² term 5·113400·3601/2520 = 225·3601 = 810225. Then
       ϖ̃₁/ϖ₀ = 770z + (810225 − 770·120)z² + ... = 770z + 717825z² + ...
       q/z = exp(ϖ̃₁/ϖ₀) = 1 + 770z + (717825 + 770²/2)z² + ...
           = 1 + 770z + 1014275z² + ...
    Inverting z = q + c₂q² + c₃q³: c₂ = −770, c₃ = 2·770² − 1014275 =
    1185800 − 1014275 = 171525. (c) The Griffiths-Dwork computation gives
    the Yukawa coupling ∫Ω ∧ θ³Ω = 5/(1 − 5⁵z) (normalized with ϖ₀ and
    the flat coordinate t = log q): Y(q) = 5/((1 − 5⁵z)ϖ₀²)·(θ log q)⁻³ =
    5 + 2875q + 4876875q² + 8564575000q³ + ..., and MIRROR SYMMETRY says
    Y(q) = ⟨H, H, H⟩ on the quintic, whence n₁ = 2875, n₂ = 609250, n₃ =
    317206375 (D4). Singularities: z = 0 (MUM point = large complex
    structure, mirror to large volume); z = 5⁻⁵ = 1/3125 (conifold: ψ⁵ =
    1, X_ψ has nodes, a vanishing S³, exponents 0, 1, 1, 2); z = ∞ (ψ =
    0, the Fermat point, exponents ⅕, ⅖, ⅗, ⅘, finite-order monodromy of
    order 5).
    Common mistakes: using H_{5n} alone (the −H_n comes from the Γ(1 +
    n + ε)⁵ in the Frobenius method); inverting the series with the wrong
    sign of c₃; forgetting ϖ₀² in Y (Y is a section of a line bundle).
    Now you try: the radius of convergence of ϖ₀ and the value of ψ at
    the conifold. (1/3125; ψ⁵ = 1)

D10. (a) State the SYZ conjecture and explain why the mirror should be
    the moduli space of special Lagrangian tori with flat U(1)
    connections. (b) Let E = S¹_{R₁} × S¹_{R₂} be a flat torus (radii R₁,
    R₂, B = 0), with τ = iR₂/R₁ and ρ = iR₁R₂. Viewing E → S¹_{R₂} as an
    SYZ fibration, show T-duality R₁ ↦ 1/R₁ exchanges τ and ρ; compute for
    R₁ = 2, R₂ = 3. (c) An elliptic K3 has singular fibres of Kodaira
    types I₁ (χ = 1) and I₂ (χ = 2). If there are 4 fibres of type I₂,
    how many I₁ fibres?
    Answer: (b) τ′ = iR₁R₂ = ρ, ρ′ = iR₂/R₁ = τ; (3i/2, 6i) ↦ (6i, 3i/2).
    (c) 24 − 8 = 16.
    Teacher explains: (a) SYZ (1996): a CY X near a large complex
    structure limit admits a special Lagrangian fibration π : X → B with
    generic fibre Tⁿ; the mirror X^∨ is a compactification of the space
    of pairs (L, ∇), L a smooth fibre, ∇ a flat U(1) connection on L. Flat
    connections on Tⁿ = Vⁿ/Λ are Hom(Λ, U(1)) = the DUAL TORUS, so X^∨ →
    B is the dual fibration. Motivation: a point of X^∨ is a 0-brane,
    whose moduli is X^∨ itself; under mirror symmetry it becomes a
    Lagrangian brane on X with a flat connection whose moduli must again
    be X^∨; McLean's theorem gives that special Lagrangian Tⁿ deform in
    an n-dimensional family (b₁(Tⁿ) = n), matching dim_R B = n. (b) The
    complex structure is the ratio of periods: τ = 2πiR₂/2πR₁ = iR₂/R₁.
    The complexified Kähler class is B + i·Area/(4π²) = iR₁R₂. T-duality
    on the fibre replaces R₁ by 1/R₁ (in string units): τ′ = iR₂/(1/R₁)
    = iR₁R₂ = ρ and ρ′ = i(1/R₁)R₂ = τ. For R₁ = 2, R₂ = 3: τ = 3i/2, ρ =
    6i, and after T-duality τ′ = 6i, ρ′ = 3i/2: the large-volume limit ρ
    → i∞ corresponds to the degenerating complex structure τ′ → i∞ (q =
    e^{2πiτ′} → 0, the MUM point). (c) For an elliptic fibration S → P¹,
    χ(S) = χ(P¹)χ(T²) + ∑(χ(F_sing) − 0) = ∑χ(F_sing). χ(K3) = 24, so 4·2
    + k·1 = 24, k = 16. For CY 3-folds the same principle χ(X) = ∑ over
    singular fibres holds for T³-fibrations: smooth T³ fibres contribute 0.
    Common mistakes: dualizing both circles (that gives back the same
    torus up to τ ↦ −1/τ); forgetting that SYZ is expected only near a
    large complex structure limit; counting χ(I₁) = 0.
    Now you try: T-dualize R₁ = 5, R₂ = 1/2: new (τ, ρ)? A K3 with only
    I₁ fibres has how many? ((5i/2, i/10); 24)

D11. (a) State Kontsevich's homological mirror symmetry and the
    Polishchuk-Zaslow dictionary for an elliptic curve E. (b) With E^∨ =
    R²/Z² and L_{(r,d)} a line of class (r, d), verify dim Hom(𝒪, 𝒪(3p))
    = #(L_{(1,0)} ∩ L_{(1,3)}), and compute χ(F, G) and the intersection
    number for F = E_{2,1} (stable, rank 2, degree 1), G = 𝒪(p), and for
    F = 𝒪_p, G = E_{2,1}. (c) Describe the action of ⊗𝒪(p), Fourier-
    Mukai Φ and [1] on (r, d) and their symplectic counterparts.
    Answer: (a) D^b Coh(E) ≅ D^π Fuk(E^∨). (b) 3 = 3; χ(E_{2,1}, 𝒪(p)) =
    1 = |2·1 − 1·1|; χ(𝒪_p, E_{2,1}) = −2 (= −#). (c) T = [[1,0],[1,1]],
    S = [[0,1],[−1,0]], −I.
    Teacher explains: (a) Kontsevich (ICM 1994): for mirror CY's, the
    bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on X is equivalent to
    the (split-closed, derived) Fukaya category of X^∨, whose objects are
    Lagrangians with flat bundles and whose morphisms are Floer complexes
    generated by intersection points, with A∞ products counting
    holomorphic polygons. For E (Polishchuk-Zaslow 1998) the dictionary
    sends a sheaf of rank r and degree d to a straight line of class
    (r, d): 𝒪 ↦ horizontal circle (1,0), 𝒪_p ↦ vertical circle (0,1),
    𝒪(dp) ↦ slope-d line (1,d), a stable bundle E_{r,d} (gcd = 1) ↦ the
    line of slope d/r. (b) By Riemann-Roch on a genus 1 curve, χ(F, G) =
    deg(F* ⊗ G) = r_F d_G − r_G d_F. Two lines of classes (a,b), (c,d)
    on R²/Z² meet |ad − bc| times. dim Hom(𝒪, 𝒪(3p)) = h⁰(𝒪(3p)) = 3,
    and L_{(1,0)}, L_{(1,3)} meet |1·3 − 0·1| = 3 times ✓. For E_{2,1} and
    𝒪(p): χ = 2·1 − 1·1 = 1; μ(E_{2,1}) = ½ < 1 = μ(𝒪(p)), both stable,
    so Ext¹ = Hom(𝒪(p), E_{2,1})* = 0 and dim Hom = 1 = #(L_{(2,1)} ∩
    L_{(1,1)}) = |2·1 − 1·1|. For 𝒪_p = (0,1) and E_{2,1}: χ = 0·1 −
    2·1 = −2: Hom(𝒪_p, E) = 0 (torsion into a bundle) and Ext¹ = 2 =
    #(L_{(0,1)} ∩ L_{(2,1)}) = |0·1 − 1·2|, now in Floer degree 1. (c)
    ⊗𝒪(p): (r, d) ↦ (r, d + r), matrix T = [[1,0],[1,1]] on column
    vectors (r, d): a DEHN TWIST along the vertical circle. Fourier-Mukai
    with the Poincaré bundle: 𝒪_p ↦ a degree-0 line bundle, 𝒪 ↦ 𝒪₀[−1],
    so (r, d) ↦ (d, −r), rotation by 90°. [1] ↦ −I (orientation reversal
    of L). These generate SL(2, Z) = the mapping class group of T², as
    Aut D^b(E) → SL(2, Z) is surjective. Composition m₂ : Hom(𝒪, 𝒪(p)) ⊗
    Hom(𝒪(p), 𝒪(2p)) → Hom(𝒪, 𝒪(2p)) is computed by triangles with
    weights e^{2πiρ·area}: THETA FUNCTIONS, matching products of sections.
    Common mistakes: forgetting orientations/signs in χ (it is
    antisymmetric); claiming dim Hom = χ without semistability and the
    slope condition; mixing rows/columns in the SL(2,Z) matrices.
    Now you try: χ(E_{3,2}, E_{2,1}) and χ(𝒪(−p), 𝒪(4p)). (3·1 − 2·2 =
    −1; 1·4 − 1·(−1) = 5)

D12. (a) Define reflexive polytopes and Batyrev's mirror construction.
    Check that Δ = conv{(−1,−1), (2,−1), (−1,2)} is reflexive, compute
    Δ°, and verify b(Δ) + b(Δ°) = 12. (b) Use Batyrev's formula to get
    the Hodge numbers of the quintic and its mirror. (c) Do the same for
    the (2,2,2,2) hypersurface in (P¹)⁴.
    Answer: (a) Δ° = conv{(1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1)}; 9 + 3 = 12. (b) (1, 101)
    and (101, 1). (c) (4, 68) and (68, 4).
    Teacher explains: (a) A lattice polytope Δ ⊂ M_R with 0 in its
    interior is REFLEXIVE iff every facet lies on a hyperplane ⟨·, u⟩ = −1
    with u ∈ N primitive; equivalently Δ° is a lattice polytope. Then
    (Δ°)° = Δ, P_Δ is Gorenstein Fano, and Batyrev (1994) shows that
    general Z_Δ ∈ |−K_{P_Δ}| and Z_{Δ°} (crepantly resolved) are mirror:
    h^{1,1}(Z_Δ) = h^{n−2,1}(Z_{Δ°}). Here the facets of Δ are x = −1,
    y = −1, x + y = 1, i.e. ⟨·, u⟩ ≥ −1 for u = (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1):
    these u are lattice points, so Δ is reflexive and Δ° is the fan
    polygon of P² (Δ = Newton polygon of cubics shifted by (−1,−1)).
    b(Δ): edges of lattice length 3, 3, 3: b = 9, interior 1 (Pick:
    area = 1 + 9/2 − 1 = 9/2 ✓). b(Δ°) = 3. 9 + 3 = 12. (b) Δ = Newton
    polytope of quintics in P⁴ (shifted), Δ° = simplex conv{e₁, ..., e₄,
    −∑eᵢ}. l(Δ) = C(9,4) = 126. The 5 facets of Δ are the monomials with
    one exponent 0; interior points of a facet: aᵢ ≥ 1 for the other 4
    with sum 5: C(4, 3) = 4 each, total 20. The codim-2 faces Γ of Δ
    (2-dimensional) are dual to the 1-dimensional faces Γ° of Δ° (edges
    of the small simplex), which have no interior lattice points, so the
    correction vanishes: h^{2,1} = 126 − 5 − 20 = 101. For h^{1,1}
    swap roles: l(Δ°) = 6, the facets of Δ° have no interior points:
    h^{1,1} = 6 − 5 = 1. The mirror Z_{Δ°} has (101, 1). (c) Δ =
    [−1, 1]⁴ (multidegree (2,2,2,2) monomials), l(Δ) = 3⁴ = 81; its 8
    facets are 3-cubes with 1 interior point each; Δ° = conv{±eᵢ} (cross
    polytope) with l = 9 and no interior points in its faces. h^{2,1} =
    81 − 5 − 8 = 68, h^{1,1} = 9 − 5 − 0 = 4 (the four P¹ factors).
    Common mistakes: counting all lattice points of a facet instead of
    its interior; forgetting the −(n + 1) = −5 (the torus automorphisms
    (C*)⁴ and the scaling); confusing Δ (monomials) with Δ° (fan rays).
    Now you try: for Δ the Newton polygon of (2,2)-forms on P¹ × P¹,
    shifted, give Δ°, b(Δ), b(Δ°). (Δ = [−1,1]², Δ° = conv{±e₁, ±e₂};
    8 and 4)

M11. Toric geometry and Batyrev.

    e) Is conv{(−1,−1), (3,−1), (−1,3)} reflexive?
    f) Is the fan polygon of F₂ reflexive? Is F₂ Fano?

       2·1 + 4·3)

      e) no: 3 interior points (the quartic Newton polygon)
