M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) A complete manifold with Ric > 0 is compact.
     b) A compact manifold with K > 0 has finite π₁.
     c) A compact even-dimensional manifold with K > 0 is simply
        connected.
     d) Every Einstein manifold has constant sectional curvature.
     e) A compact manifold with K ≤ 0 has infinite, torsion-free π₁.
     f) A compact manifold with K < 0 can have Z² ⊆ π₁.
     g) The cut point along a geodesic is always a conjugate point.
     h) Ric ≥ (n − 1)g ⇒ vol(M) ≤ vol(Sⁿ(1)).
     i) A compact M with Ric ≥ (n − 1)g has λ₁ ≥ n.
     j) A closed M with Ric ≥ 0 has b₁ ≤ n.
     k) Ricci flow on a closed manifold preserves R > 0.
     l) Volume is non-increasing under the (unnormalized) Ricci flow.
     m) Every Ricci flow on a closed manifold exists for all time.
     n) The Gaussian soliton on Rⁿ is steady.
     o) Every compact complex manifold admits a Kähler metric.
     p) Every compact Kähler manifold is symplectic.
     q) Every closed symplectic manifold admits a Kähler structure.
     r) Every Fano surface admits a Kähler-Einstein metric.
     s) A compact Kähler manifold with c₁ = 0 carries a Ricci-flat
        Kähler metric in every Kähler class.
     t) S⁴ carries a symplectic form.
     u) B⁴(2) embeds into B²(1) × R² by a volume-preserving map, but not
        by a symplectic one.
     v) Every closed surface has a metric of constant curvature.
     Answers:
       a) F: the paraboloid z = x² + y²      b) T (Myers)
       c) F: RP²                              d) F: S²(1) × S²(1)
       e) T (Cartan-Hadamard)
       f) F: Preissman; e.g. T² × Σ₂ has no K < 0 metric
       g) F: flat R²/Z² (cut points, no conjugate points)
       h) T (Bishop)          i) T (Lichnerowicz)
       j) T (Bochner)         k) T (maximum principle)
       l) F: hyperbolic surfaces expand (d vol/dt = −∫R > 0)
       m) F: round S² dies at T = r₀²/2
       n) F: it is shrinking (λ = ½)
       o) F: the Hopf surface S¹ × S³   p) T
       q) F: the Kodaira-Thurston manifold (b₁ = 3)
       r) F: Bl₁CP² (Matsushima)          s) T (Yau)
       t) F: H²(S⁴) = 0                   u) T (Gromov nonsqueezing)
       v) T (uniformization; Ricci flow on surfaces)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep the three MODEL SPACES on the board with snκ(t) = sin
  t, t, sinh t, and reduce everything to them: curvature of a rotational
  metric is −f″/f, Jacobi fields are snκ·E, geodesic circles have length
  2π snκ(r), balls have volume ∫snκ^{n−1}, the Ricci flow simply rescales
  them, and the spectrum of the sphere comes from harmonic polynomials.
  Then teach each comparison theorem as "the model is the extreme case":
  Myers (the sphere is the longest), Bishop-Gromov (the model ball is the
  biggest), Lichnerowicz (the sphere has the smallest λ₁), Toponogov (the
  model triangle is the thinnest/fattest). For products, remember mixed
  planes are flat and Ricci splits. In Kähler geometry insist on one
  normalization (ω_FS = i∂∂̄ log|Z|², ∫_{CP¹} = 2π) and check every
  answer with [ρ] = 2πc₁; in symplectic geometry, first ask whether a
  cohomological obstruction ([ω]ⁿ ≠ 0) already decides. Harder: prove
  the Cheeger-Gromoll splitting theorem via Busemann functions and the
  Laplacian comparison; prove Klingenberg's injectivity radius estimate
  and the topological sphere theorem; derive the evolution equations of
  Ric and Rm under Ricci flow and Hamilton's pinching estimate in
  dimension 3; compute Perelman's W-entropy of the Gaussian soliton and
  prove no local collapsing; prove the Calabi conjecture's C⁰ estimate
  (Yau) for the complex Monge-Ampère equation (ω + i∂∂̄φ)ⁿ = e^F ωⁿ; and
  prove Gromov's nonsqueezing theorem with J-holomorphic spheres in
  S²(πR² + ε) × T^{2n−2}.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.9 — INDEX THEORY: THE ATIYAH-SINGER INDEX THEOREM (FREDHOLM
INDEX, ELLIPTIC SYMBOLS, GAUSS-BONNET-CHERN, SIGNATURE, RIEMANN-ROCH,
DIRAC OPERATORS, HEAT KERNELS, LEFSCHETZ, FAMILIES, ETA INVARIANTS)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - FREDHOLM OPERATORS: a bounded T : H₁ → H₂ with dim ker T < ∞, range
    closed, dim coker T < ∞; ind T = dim ker T − dim coker T = dim ker T
    − dim ker T*. ATKINSON: T is Fredholm ⇔ invertible modulo compacts.
    The index is locally constant (so a homotopy invariant), unchanged
    by compact perturbations (ind(1 + K) = 0), and ind(ST) = ind S +
    ind T. Finite dimensions: every A : Cᵖ → C^q has index p − q.
    UNILATERAL SHIFT S eₙ = eₙ₊₁ on ℓ²(N): ind S = −1, ind S* = +1.
  - TOEPLITZ INDEX: for f : S¹ → C* continuous, T_f = P M_f on the Hardy
    space H² (P the Szegő projection) is Fredholm with ind T_f =
    −wind(f) (so T_z = S has index −1); for matrix-valued F, ind T_F =
    −wind(det F). T_fT_g − T_{fg} is compact. Fred(H) classifies K-theory:
    π₀(Fred) = Z via the index (ATIYAH-JÄNICH: [X, Fred] = K⁰(X)).
  - DIFFERENTIAL OPERATORS AND SYMBOLS: P = ∑_{|α|≤m} a_α(x)∂^α between
    bundles E → F; PRINCIPAL SYMBOL σ_P(x, ξ) = ∑_{|α|=m} a_α(x)(iξ)^α
    ∈ Hom(E_x, F_x), ξ ∈ T*ₓM, homogeneous of degree m; σ_{PQ} = σ_Pσ_Q.
    ELLIPTIC: σ_P(x, ξ) invertible for all ξ ≠ 0. Examples: Laplacian
    (σ = |ξ|²), ∂̄ = ½(∂ₓ + i∂ᵧ) (σ = (i/2)(ξ₁ + iξ₂)), d + d*
    (σ = i(ξ∧ − ι_ξ), square |ξ|²), Dirac (σ = ic(ξ)). NOT elliptic: wave
    and heat operators, d : Ω⁰ → Ω¹ (n ≥ 2). No real scalar operator of
    odd order is elliptic in dimension n ≥ 2 (S^{n−1} is connected).
  - ELLIPTIC THEORY on a closed manifold M: a PARAMETRIX Q with PQ − 1,
    QP − 1 smoothing; ELLIPTIC REGULARITY (Pu smooth ⇒ u smooth; Gårding
    ‖u‖_{s+m} ≤ C(‖Pu‖_s + ‖u‖_s)); P : H^{s+m} → H^s is Fredholm with
    index independent of s: ind P = dim ker P − dim ker P* (both spaces
    of smooth sections). The index depends only on the homotopy class of
    the symbol; a formally self-adjoint P : E → E has index 0; every
    elliptic DIFFERENTIAL operator on a closed ODD-dimensional manifold
    has index 0 (false for pseudodifferential ones, cf. Toeplitz).
  - ATIYAH-SINGER (1963): the ANALYTIC index equals the TOPOLOGICAL index
    ind_t[σ_P], where [σ_P] ∈ K_c(T*M) and ind_t is defined by embedding
    M ⊂ R^N and the Thom isomorphism. COHOMOLOGICAL FORM: ind P =
    (−1)^n ∫_{T*M} ch[σ_P] ∪ π*Td(TM ⊗ C) (sign conventions vary). For
    Dirac-type operators it becomes an integral over M of characteristic
    classes (the four classical cases below).
  - CHARACTERISTIC CLASSES (formal roots xⱼ; for real bundles p₁ = ∑xⱼ²):
    ch(E) = ∑e^{xⱼ} = rk + c₁ + ½(c₁² − 2c₂) + ⅙(c₁³ − 3c₁c₂ + 3c₃) + ...;
    Td ↔ x/(1 − e^{−x}): Td = 1 + c₁/2 + (c₁² + c₂)/12 + c₁c₂/24 + ...;
    L ↔ x/tanh x: L = 1 + p₁/3 + (7p₂ − p₁²)/45 + (62p₃ − 13p₁p₂ +
    2p₁³)/945; Â ↔ (x/2)/sinh(x/2): Â = 1 − p₁/24 + (7p₁² − 4p₂)/5760 +
    ...; for complex manifolds p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂. c(CPⁿ) = (1 + h)^{n+1},
    p(CPⁿ) = (1 + h²)^{n+1}, p(HPⁿ) = (1 + u)^{2n+2}(1 + 4u)^{−1}.
  - GAUSS-BONNET-CHERN: d + d* : Ω^even → Ω^odd has index χ(M) (Hodge);
    χ(M²ᵐ) = (2π)^{−m}∫Pf(Ω) = ∫e(TM). Surfaces: ∫K dA = 2πχ. Dim 4:
    χ = (1/32π²)∫(|Rm|² − 4|Ric|² + R²)dV. Hyperbolic: area = −2πχ in
    dim 2, vol = (4π²/3)χ in dim 4. HOPF: ∑ indices of a vector field
    with isolated zeros = χ(M).
  - HIRZEBRUCH SIGNATURE THEOREM: σ(M⁴ᵏ) = signature of the intersection
    form on H²ᵏ = index of the signature operator d + d* : Ω⁺ → Ω⁻ (±1
    eigenspaces of τ = i^{p(p−1)+2k}*) = ∫L(TM). Dim 4: σ = p₁/3 (so p₁
    ≡ 0 mod 3); dim 8: 45σ = 7p₂ − p₁². σ(CP²ᵏ) = 1, σ(K3) = −16,
    complex surfaces σ = (c₁² − 2c₂)/3. σ is multiplicative under finite
    covers. MILNOR: exotic 7-spheres from integrality of p₂.
  - HIRZEBRUCH-RIEMANN-ROCH: X compact complex, E holomorphic: χ(X, E) =
    ∑(−1)^q h^q(X, E) = ∫ch(E)Td(X) = index of ∂̄ + ∂̄* on Ω^{0,even}(E).
    Curves: χ(L) = deg L + 1 − g; surfaces: χ(L) = χ(O) + ½L·(L − K),
    NOETHER χ(O) = (c₁² + c₂)/12; CPⁿ: χ(O(k)) = C(n + k, n). The TODD
    GENUS χ(O_X) is an integer (not obvious from the formula).
  - SPIN DIRAC OPERATOR: on a spin manifold, spinor bundle S = S⁺ ⊕ S⁻
    (even n), D = ∑c(eᵢ)∇_{eᵢ}, D⁺ : S⁺ → S⁻; ind D⁺ = Â(M) = ∫Â(TM),
    twisted ind D⁺_E = ∫Â(TM)ch(E). Dim 4: Â = −p₁/24 = −σ/8; S± are
    quaternionic in dim 4, so the index is even: ROKHLIN σ ≡ 0 mod 16 for
    smooth closed spin 4-manifolds. Â(K3) = 2, Â(HP²) = 0, Â(CP²) = −⅛
    ∉ Z (CP² is not spin). Kähler: S = Λ^{0,*} ⊗ K^{1/2}, D ~ √2(∂̄ + ∂̄*),
    so Â = Td when c₁ = 0. Spin 8-manifolds: 896Â = p₁² − 4σ.
  - LICHNEROWICZ: D² = ∇*∇ + R/4 (R scalar curvature). Closed spin with R
    > 0 ⇒ ker D = 0 ⇒ Â(M) = 0. So K3, spin surfaces of degree 6 in CP³,
    Bott manifolds (Â = 1) carry no metric of positive scalar curvature
    (PSC). R ≥ 0 and ker D ≠ 0 ⇒ parallel spinor ⇒ Ric = 0. Â = 0 does
    NOT imply PSC (T⁴). Dirac spectrum on round Sⁿ: ±(n/2 + k), k ≥ 0;
    FRIEDRICH: λ² ≥ nR_min/(4(n − 1)), sharp on Sⁿ.
  - HEAT KERNEL PROOF: MCKEAN-SINGER ind D⁺ = Tr e^{−tD⁻D⁺} − Tr e^{−tD⁺D⁻}
    = str e^{−tD²} for every t > 0 (non-zero eigenspaces cancel in pairs).
    ASYMPTOTICS: Tr e^{−tΔ} ~ (4πt)^{−n/2}∑ tᵏ∫aₖ; for Δ = ∇*∇ + 𝓔:
    a₀ = rk, a₁ = tr(R/6 − 𝓔). Hence ind = ∫ str a_{n/2}(x) dx is LOCAL;
    GETZLER rescaling plus MEHLER's formula (harmonic oscillator: Tr e^{−tH}
    = 1/(2 sinh t)) turns str a_{n/2} into the Â-form (LOCAL INDEX
    THEOREM). On unit S²: Tr e^{−tΔ} = 1/t + 1/3 + t/15 + O(t²).
  - LEFSCHETZ FIXED POINT FORMULAS: L(f) = ∑(−1)^i tr(f*|Hⁱ) =
    ∑_{f(p)=p} sign det(1 − df_p) (transverse fixed points). ATIYAH-BOTT
    for an elliptic complex: holomorphic case L(f, O) = ∑_p 1/det_C(1 −
    df_p), with a factor tr(f|E_p) for a twisted bundle; G-SIGNATURE: an
    isolated fixed point with rotation angles θ₁, θ₂ in dim 4 contributes
    −cot(θ₁/2)cot(θ₂/2). EQUIVARIANT INDEX ind_G P ∈ R(G) with character
    g ↦ L(g, P); gives the WEYL CHARACTER FORMULA; ATIYAH-HIRZEBRUCH: a
    closed spin manifold with a non-trivial S¹-action has Â = 0.
  - FAMILIES INDEX: a continuous family Pₓ (x ∈ X) of Fredholm or
    elliptic operators has an INDEX BUNDLE ind P ∈ K⁰(X) (= [ker] −
    [coker] when dim ker is constant; kernels may jump). ATIYAH-SINGER
    FAMILIES THEOREM: ch(ind D) = ∫_{M/X} Â(T_{M/X})ch(E) (a Grothendieck-
    Riemann-Roch). For self-adjoint families over S¹: SPECTRAL FLOW (net
    number of eigenvalues crossing 0 upward) = odd index.
  - APS (ATIYAH-PATODI-SINGER) awareness: on M with boundary Y, D =
    σ(∂ᵤ + A) near Y, with the global boundary condition P_{≥0}(u|Y) = 0:
    ind D = ∫_M α₀ − (h + η(0))/2, h = dim ker A, η(s) = ∑_{λ≠0} sign λ
    |λ|^{−s} (the ETA INVARIANT, spectral asymmetry, non-local).
    Signature: σ(M) = ∫_M L − η(Y); η(Y) = 0 if Y has an orientation-
    reversing isometry. Example: A = −i d/dθ + a on S¹ (0 < a < 1) has
    η(0) = 1 − 2a; spectral flow = jumps of ξ = (h + η)/2.
  - APPLICATIONS: dimensions of moduli spaces as indices (M_g: 3g − 3;
    SU(2) instantons: 8k − 3(1 − b₁ + b⁺)); integrality theorems (Â, Td,
    L); non-existence of PSC metrics; fixed-point theorems; exotic
    spheres; Rokhlin; χ and σ divisibility for coverings.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show that the unilateral shift S on ℓ²(N) has index −1 and
    explain why ind T_f = −wind(f) for Toeplitz operators on H²(S¹).
    (b) Compute ind T_f for f₁ = (2z − 1)/(z − 3), f₂ = cos θ + 2i sin θ,
    f₃ = z̄²(z − 3) (z = e^{iθ}). (c) Show that a = x + d/dx on L²(R)
    (domain {u : u, xu, u′ ∈ L²}) is Fredholm and find its index.
    Answer: (a) ker S = 0, coker S = span{e₀}. (b) −1, −1, +2. (c) 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) S(x₀, x₁, ...) = (0, x₀, x₁, ...) is an
    isometry, so ker S = 0 and ran S = {x : x₀ = 0} = e₀^⊥ is closed of
    codimension 1: ind S = 0 − 1 = −1. S* is the backward shift, ker S* =
    span{e₀}, S* onto: ind S* = +1 = −ind S (always ind T* = −ind T).
    Identify H² with ℓ²(N) via eₙ = zⁿ: then T_z = S and T_{zᵏ} = Sᵏ has
    index −k (ind(ST) = ind S + ind T), T_{z̄ᵏ} = (S*)ᵏ has index +k.
    For continuous f, g, the operator T_fT_g − T_{fg} is compact (check it
    for trigonometric polynomials, where it has finite rank, then
    approximate). Hence if f never vanishes, T_{1/f} inverts T_f modulo
    compacts, T_f is Fredholm (Atkinson), and f ↦ ind T_f is a
    homomorphism (C(S¹, C*), ·) → Z constant on homotopy classes.
    Homotopy classes of maps S¹ → C* are labelled by the winding number
    and f ≃ z^{wind f}, so ind T_f = ind T_{z^{wind f}} = −wind f.
    (b) For rational f, wind f = (zeros − poles) in the open disc
    (argument principle). f₁: zero ½ inside, pole 3 outside, wind = 1,
    ind = −1. f₂: on |z| = 1, cos θ = (z + z̄)/2, sin θ = (z − z̄)/(2i),
    so f₂ = (z + z̄)/2 + (z − z̄) = (3z − z̄)/2 = (3z² − 1)/(2z): zeros
    ±1/√3 inside, a pole at 0: wind = 2 − 1 = 1 (an ellipse traversed
    once anticlockwise), ind = −1. f₃ = (z − 3)/z² on the circle: no zero
    inside, double pole: wind = −2, ind = +2.
    (c) ker a: u′ = −xu, u = Ce^{−x²/2} ∈ L², dim 1. a* = x − d/dx:
    u′ = xu, u = Ce^{x²/2} ∉ L², so ker a* = 0. The range is closed
    because a*a = H − 1 with H = −d²/dx² + x² (eigenvalues 2n + 1,
    Hermite functions), so a*a ≥ 2 on (ker a)^⊥. ind a = 1 − 0 = 1.
    Common mistakes: sign conventions (with the Hardy space of NON-
    NEGATIVE frequencies, ind T_z = −1); forgetting that the range must
    be closed (d/dx on L²(R) is injective with dense, non-closed range,
    NOT Fredholm); counting only zeros and forgetting poles in wind f.
    Now you try: ind T_f for f = z³ + 3, and ind T_F for the 2 × 2
    symbol F = [[z, 1], [0, z]]. (0, zeros have |z| = 3^{1/3} > 1; −2,
    since det F = z²)

D2. (a) Define the principal symbol and ellipticity; prove σ_{PQ} =
    σ_Pσ_Q and that no scalar operator with REAL coefficients and ODD
    order is elliptic on Rⁿ, n ≥ 2. (b) Which are elliptic: Δ = −∂ₓ² −
    ∂ᵧ², ∂ₓ² − ∂ᵧ², ∂ₜ − ∂ₓ², ∂̄, ∂̄², d + d* on R², d : Ω⁰ → Ω¹ on R²?
    (c) On S¹, compute ind(d/dθ + a(θ)) : H¹ → L² for smooth real a, and
    ind of ∂̄ : C^∞(CP¹) → Ω^{0,1}(CP¹).
    Answer: (b) yes, no, no, yes, yes, yes, no. (c) 0 for every a; 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) Write P = ∑a_α∂^α; freezing coefficients and
    letting P act on e^{iλ⟨x,ξ⟩}: P(e^{iλ⟨x,ξ⟩}) = λᵐσ_P(x, ξ)e^{iλ⟨x,ξ⟩}
    + O(λ^{m−1}), so σ_P(x, ξ) = ∑_{|α|=m}a_α(iξ)^α is the leading
    coefficient in λ, and applying Q then P gives leading coefficient
    σ_P σ_Q: symbols multiply. If the a_α are real, σ_P = iᵐp(ξ) with p
    a real homogeneous polynomial of odd degree m; then p(−ξ) = −p(ξ), and
    since S^{n−1} is connected for n ≥ 2, the intermediate value theorem
    gives p(ξ₀) = 0 for some |ξ₀| = 1: not elliptic. (For n = 1, d/dx
    with σ = iξ is elliptic.)
    (b) σ(−∂ₓ² − ∂ᵧ²) = ξ₁² + ξ₂² ≠ 0: elliptic. σ(∂ₓ² − ∂ᵧ²) = −ξ₁² +
    ξ₂², zero on ξ₁ = ±ξ₂: hyperbolic. Heat: order 2, σ = ξₓ², zero at
    (ξₜ, ξₓ) = (1, 0): parabolic, not elliptic. ∂̄: (i/2)(ξ₁ + iξ₂) = 0
    only at ξ = 0: elliptic (complex coefficients escape (a)). ∂̄²:
    −¼(ξ₁ + iξ₂)², elliptic. d + d* on Ω⁰ ⊕ Ω¹ ⊕ Ω²: σ = i(ξ∧ − ι_ξ) and
    (ξ∧ − ι_ξ)² = −(ξ∧ι_ξ + ι_ξξ∧) = −|ξ|², so σ² = |ξ|²: invertible.
    d : Ω⁰ → Ω¹ has σ(ξ)f = iξf, a map C → C² never onto: not elliptic.
    (c) ker: u′ = −au, u = C exp(−∫₀^θ a); periodic iff c := ∫₀^{2π}a = 0.
    The formal adjoint is −d/dθ + a with kernel C exp(∫₀^θ a), again
    periodic iff c = 0. So dim ker = dim coker = 1 if c = 0, and both are
    0 otherwise: index 0 always — as it must be, since P is homotopic
    through elliptic operators to d/dθ (first-order part unchanged), and
    S¹ is odd-dimensional. On CP¹: ker ∂̄ = holomorphic functions =
    constants, coker = H^{0,1} = H¹(CP¹, O) = 0: index 1 = 1 − g.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the factor i in σ (it matters for signs,
    not for ellipticity); declaring the heat operator elliptic because
    the "spatial part" is; thinking ellipticity needs square matrices
    (it does: an elliptic σ is an isomorphism, so rk E = rk F).
    Now you try: is the Cauchy-Riemann system (u, v) ↦ (uₓ − vᵧ, uᵧ + vₓ)
    elliptic? Find det σ. (yes; det σ = −(ξ₁² + ξ₂²))

D3. (a) State the Atiyah-Singer index theorem, the multiplicative
    sequences Td, L, Â via their generating functions, and the four
    classical special cases. (b) Derive Td₁, Td₂, L₁, Â₁ and L₂ from the
    power series. (c) Compute Td(CP²), L(CP²), Â(CP²) and deduce that CP²
    is not spin.
    Answer: (b) Td = 1 + c₁/2 + (c₁² + c₂)/12; L₁ = p₁/3; Â₁ = −p₁/24;
    L₂ = (7p₂ − p₁²)/45. (c) 1, 1, −⅛ ∉ Z.
    Teacher explains: (a) For P elliptic on closed Mⁿ the symbol gives a
    class [σ_P] ∈ K_c(T*M) and ind P = ind_t[σ_P] = (−1)ⁿ∫_{T*M} ch[σ_P]
    π*Td(TM ⊗ C) (signs depend on orientation conventions for T*M). A
    power series Q(x) = 1 + q₁x + ... defines a genus:
    for formal roots, Q(E) = ∏Q(xⱼ), rewritten in symmetric functions.
       Td: x/(1 − e^{−x}) = 1 + x/2 + x²/12 − x⁴/720 + ...
       L:  x/tanh x = 1 + x²/3 − x⁴/45 + 2x⁶/945 − ...
       Â:  (x/2)/sinh(x/2) = 1 − x²/24 + 7x⁴/5760 − ...
    (L and Â are even, so they are series in p₁ = ∑xⱼ², p₂ = ∑_{i<j}xᵢ²xⱼ²,
    ...). CLASSICAL CASES: d + d* (Euler: ∫e), signature operator (∫L),
    ∂̄_E (∫ch(E)Td), spin Dirac D_E (∫Â ch(E)).
    (b) Two roots: (1 + x₁/2 + x₁²/12)(1 + x₂/2 + x₂²/12) = 1 + (x₁ + x₂)/2
    + (x₁² + x₂²)/12 + x₁x₂/4. With c₁ = x₁ + x₂, c₂ = x₁x₂, x₁² + x₂² =
    c₁² − 2c₂: degree 2 part = (c₁² − 2c₂ + 3c₂)/12 = (c₁² + c₂)/12. For L,
    Â the degree-4 part is ∑xⱼ²/3 = p₁/3 and −p₁/24. For L₂ put yⱼ = xⱼ²:
    the degree-8 part of ∏(1 + yⱼ/3 − yⱼ²/45) is −∑yⱼ²/45 + ∑_{i<j}yᵢyⱼ/9 =
    −(p₁² − 2p₂)/45 + 5p₂/45 = (7p₂ − p₁²)/45.
    (c) c(CP²) = (1 + h)³: c₁ = 3h, c₂ = 3h², ∫h² = 1. Td = 1 + (3/2)h +
    (9 + 3)/12 h² = 1 + (3/2)h + h², Todd genus 1 = χ(O) = h⁰(O) = 1.
    p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂ = 3h² (also from (1 + h²)³), so L = p₁/3 = 1 = σ(CP²),
    and Â = −3/24 = −⅛. If CP² were spin, Â would be ind D⁺ ∈ Z:
    contradiction. (Directly: w₂ = c₁ mod 2 = 3h mod 2 ≠ 0.)
    Common mistakes: integrating the wrong degree (only the top-degree
    component counts); using p₁ = c₁² + 2c₂; forgetting that ∫h² = 1 on
    CP² but ∫h³ = 1 on CP³; dropping the 1/2 in x/2 inside Â (x/sinh x
    gives −p₁/6, wrong by a factor 4).
    Now you try: compute L(CP⁴) using p(CP⁴) = 1 + 5h² + 10h⁴, and
    Â(CP⁴). ((7·10 − 25)/45 = 1; (7·25 − 4·10)/5760 = 3/128)

D4. (a) Show that d + d* : Ω^even(M) → Ω^odd(M) on a closed oriented
    Riemannian M is elliptic of index χ(M). State Gauss-Bonnet-Chern.
    (b) Find (1/2π)∫K dA for a genus-3 surface and the area of a closed
    hyperbolic (K = −1) surface of genus 3. (c) Verify the 4-dimensional
    formula χ = (1/32π²)∫(|Rm|² − 4|Ric|² + R²)dV for the unit S⁴ and for
    S²(1) × S²(1); find the volume of a closed hyperbolic 4-manifold with
    χ = 2.
    Answer: (b) −4; 8π. (c) 2 and 4 (integrands 24 and 8); 8π²/3.
    Teacher explains: (a) σ(d + d*)(ξ) = i(ξ∧ − ι_ξ) squares to |ξ|², so
    it is invertible for ξ ≠ 0 and swaps even/odd degrees. By Hodge
    theory (d + d*)² = Δ and ker(d + d*) = ker Δ = harmonic forms 𝓗, with
    𝓗ᵏ ≅ Hᵏ(M; R). On even forms the kernel is ⊕𝓗^{2j}; the cokernel is
    the kernel of the adjoint, which is d + d* on odd forms: ⊕𝓗^{2j+1}.
    Hence ind = ∑b_{2j} − ∑b_{2j+1} = χ(M). Atiyah-Singer then gives
    GAUSS-BONNET-CHERN χ(M²ᵐ) = (2π)^{−m}∫_M Pf(Ω) = ∫e(TM), and χ = 0
    for odd dimension.
    (b) χ(Σ₃) = 2 − 6 = −4, so (1/2π)∫K = −4; with K = −1: area =
    −2πχ = 8π.
    (c) Unit S⁴: R_{ijkl} = g_{ik}g_{jl} − g_{il}g_{jk}, |Rm|² = 2n(n − 1)
    = 24, Ric = 3g, |Ric|² = 9·4 = 36, R = 12, R² = 144. Integrand 24 −
    144 + 144 = 24; vol S⁴ = 8π²/3; χ = 24·(8π²/3)/(32π²) = 2 ✓. For
    S² × S²: each factor contributes |Rm|² = 2·2·1 = 4, total 8; Ric = g,
    |Ric|² = 4; R = 4, R² = 16: integrand 8 − 16 + 16 = 8; volume
    (4π)² = 16π²: χ = 8·16π²/(32π²) = 4 ✓ (= 2·2). For K ≡ −1 in dim 4
    the Pfaffian integrand is the same as for K ≡ +1 (degree 2 in the
    curvature): χ = 3vol/(4π²), so vol = 4π²·2/3 = 8π²/3.
    Common mistakes: using the index of d + d* on ALL forms (a
    self-adjoint operator: index 0); forgetting that hyperbolic volume
    is forced by topology only in even dimensions via Gauss-Bonnet; in
    dim 2 writing ∫K = 4πχ (that is ∫R, R = 2K).
    Now you try: for a vector field on Σ₂ with isolated zeros, what is
    the sum of the indices? Could it have a single zero of index −1?
    (χ = −2; no)

D5. (a) Define the signature σ(M⁴ᵏ), the signature operator, and state
    Hirzebruch's theorem σ = ∫L. (b) Check it for CP², CP⁴, HP² (p₁ =
    2u, p₂ = 7u²), and show that a smooth degree-d surface in CP³ has
    σ = −d(d² − 4)/3; evaluate for d = 1, ..., 6. (c) (Milnor) Let W⁸ be
    a D⁴-bundle over S⁴ with Euler number 1 and p₁ = 2k·u (k odd), whose
    boundary M⁷ is homeomorphic to S⁷. Show that if M⁷ were
    diffeomorphic to S⁷, then 45 + 4k² ≡ 0 mod 7; deduce that k = 3
    gives an exotic 7-sphere.
    Answer: (b) all three signatures are 1; d = 1..6: 1, 0, −5, −16, −35,
    −64. (c) p₂ = (45 + 4k²)/7 must be an integer; k = 3 gives 81/7.
    Teacher explains: (a) On H²ᵏ(M; R) the form (a, b) ↦ ∫a ∪ b is
    symmetric and non-degenerate (Poincaré duality); σ = b⁺ − b⁻. With
    τ = i^{p(p−1)+2k}* on p-forms (τ² = 1), d + d* anticommutes with τ,
    so it maps Ω⁺ → Ω⁻; harmonic forms of degree ≠ 2k cancel in pairs
    (ω ± τω), and in degree 2k τ = * splits 𝓗²ᵏ = 𝓗⁺ ⊕ 𝓗⁻ with
    dimensions b⁺, b⁻. So the index is σ(M), and Atiyah-Singer gives
    σ = ∫L(TM). Dim 4: σ = p₁/3; dim 8: σ = (7p₂ − p₁²)/45.
    (b) CP²: p₁ = 3h², σ = 1 ✓. CP⁴: (7·10 − 5²)/45 = 45/45 = 1 ✓. HP²:
    (7·7 − 2²)/45 = 1 ✓ (H⁴ = Zu, u² = generator). For X_d ⊂ CP³ with
    hyperplane h, ∫h² = d, adjunction: c(X) = (1 + h)⁴/(1 + dh), so c₁ =
    (4 − d)h, c₂ = (6 − 4d + d²)h²; c₁² = d(4 − d)², c₂ = d(d² − 4d + 6).
    p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂ = d[(16 − 8d + d²) − 2d² + 8d − 12] = d(4 − d²), so
    σ = −d(d² − 4)/3: d = 1: 1 (a plane); 2: 0 (P¹ × P¹); 3: −5 (CP² blown
    up in 6 points: 1 − 6); 4: −16 (K3); 5: −35; 6: −64.
    (c) W ∪_M D⁸ = N would be a closed smooth 8-manifold with H⁴ = Zu,
    u² = 1 (Euler number 1), so σ(N) = 1, p₁ = 2ku, p₁² = 4k². Then 45 =
    7p₂ − 4k², p₂ = (45 + 4k²)/7 must be an integer. k = 1: 49/7 = 7 (no
    contradiction; this is the Hopf fibration, M = S⁷). k = 3: (45 +
    36)/7 = 81/7 ∉ Z. So M⁷ (homeomorphic to S⁷ by Morse theory) is not
    diffeomorphic to S⁷: an EXOTIC SPHERE.
    Common mistakes: forgetting that σ(M) is defined only in dimensions
    4k; applying the signature theorem to topological manifolds without
    rational Pontryagin classes; confusing p₁(X_d) with c₁².
    Now you try: σ(CP²#kCP̄²) via c₁² = 9 − k, c₂ = 3 + k, and σ(CP⁶)
    from L₃ with p = (1 + h²)⁷. (1 − k; (62·35 − 13·7·21 + 2·343)/945 = 1)

D6. (a) Explain how Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch χ(X, E) = ∫ch(E)Td(X) is the
    Atiyah-Singer theorem for ∂̄ + ∂̄*, and derive Riemann-Roch for a line
    bundle L on a curve of genus g. (b) Derive χ(L) = χ(O) + ½L·(L − K)
    on a surface and compute χ(O(a, b)) on P¹ × P¹, χ(O(k)) on CP², and
    χ(O(1)) on a quartic K3. (c) Compute χ(O(k)) on CP³ and on the quintic
    threefold X₅ ⊂ CP⁴ (H³ = 5, c₁ = 0, c₂·H = 50); check k = 1, 2.
    Answer: (a) χ(L) = deg L + 1 − g. (b) (a + 1)(b + 1); (k + 1)(k + 2)/2;
    4. (c) (k + 1)(k + 2)(k + 3)/6; (5k³ + 25k)/6: 5 and 15.
    Teacher explains: (a) The Dolbeault complex 0 → Ω^{0,0}(E) → Ω^{0,1}(E)
    → ... is elliptic; rolling it up, ∂̄ + ∂̄* : Ω^{0,even}(E) →
    Ω^{0,odd}(E) is elliptic and by Hodge theory its index is ∑(−1)^q
    h^q(X, E). The topological side gives ∫ch(E)Td(TX). Curve: Td = 1 +
    c₁(TX)/2, ∫c₁(TX) = 2 − 2g; ch(L) = 1 + c₁(L). Degree-2 part: c₁(L)
    + c₁(TX)/2, integral d + 1 − g.
    (b) ch(L)Td = (1 + L + L²/2)(1 + c₁/2 + (c₁² + c₂)/12). Top part:
    L²/2 + L·c₁/2 + (c₁² + c₂)/12. With c₁ = −K: χ(L) = χ(O) + (L² −
    L·K)/2 and χ(O) = (c₁² + c₂)/12 (NOETHER). P¹ × P¹: c₁² = 8, c₂ = 4,
    χ(O) = 1; L = (a, b), K = (−2, −2), L² = 2ab, L·K = −2a − 2b:
    χ = 1 + ab + a + b = (a + 1)(b + 1) (= h⁰ = bidegree polynomials when
    a, b ≥ 0). CP²: χ(O) = (9 + 3)/12 = 1, L = kh, K = −3h: χ = 1 +
    (k² + 3k)/2 = (k + 1)(k + 2)/2. Checks: k = −1, −2 give 0, k = −3
    gives 1 = h²(O(−3)) = h⁰(O) (Serre duality). K3: c₁ = 0, c₂ = 24,
    χ(O) = 2; L = O(1)|, L² = 4, K = 0: χ = 2 + 2 = 4 = h⁰ (4 linear
    forms on P³).
    (c) CP³: c = (1 + h)⁴: c₁ = 4h, c₂ = 6h². For a line bundle L on a
    threefold, χ(L) = L³/6 + c₁L²/4 + (c₁² + c₂)L/12 + c₁c₂/24. With L =
    kh: k³/6 + k² + 22k/12 + 24/24 = (k³ + 6k² + 11k + 6)/6 = (k + 1)(k +
    2)(k + 3)/6. X₅: c₁ = 0 so χ = 5k³/6 + 50k/12 = (5k³ + 25k)/6. k = 1:
    30/6 = 5 = number of linear forms on P⁴; k = 2: (40 + 50)/6 = 15 =
    number of quadrics in 5 variables (no quadric contains X₅) ✓.
    Common mistakes: using c₁ = K instead of −K; forgetting that χ is an
    alternating sum (h⁰ = χ only after a vanishing theorem); integrating
    h³ = 1 on X₅ (it is 5).
    Now you try: h⁰(K³) for a curve of genus 2 (use h¹(K³) = h⁰(K⁻²) =
    0), and χ(O_X) of a quintic surface (c₁² = 5, c₂ = 55). (5; 5)

D7. (a) Describe the spin Dirac operator D⁺ : S⁺ → S⁻ and why ind D⁺ =
    Â(M). In dimension 4 show ind D⁺ = −σ/8 and deduce ROKHLIN'S THEOREM
    from the quaternionic structure of S±. (b) Compute ind D⁺ for K3, T⁴
    and the degree-6 surface X₆ ⊂ CP³; can a smooth closed spin
    4-manifold have σ = 8? σ = −32? (c) On a spin 4-manifold, ind D⁺_E =
    −rk(E)σ/8 + ∫ch₂(E). Compute it for an SU(2)-bundle with c₂ = k on S⁴
    and on K3, and ind D⁺_L on a spin surface of genus g twisted by a line
    bundle L of degree d.
    Answer: (b) 2, 0, 8; σ = 8 no; σ = −32 yes (K3 # K3). (c) −k; 4 − k;
    d.
    Teacher explains: (a) The Clifford relation c(v)² = −|v|² is realized
    on the spinor bundle S, which exists iff w₂(M) = 0 (a SPIN
    STRUCTURE). The chirality element ω = i^{n/2}c(e₁)⋯c(eₙ) (n even)
    satisfies ω² = 1 and anticommutes with c(v), so S = S⁺ ⊕ S⁻ and D =
    ∑c(eᵢ)∇_{eᵢ} swaps S±. Its symbol ic(ξ) squares to |ξ|²: elliptic. The
    topological index of this symbol is ∫Â(TM) (the heat kernel proof of
    D9 produces exactly the series (x/2)/sinh(x/2)). In dim 4, Â = −p₁/24
    and p₁ = 3σ, so ind D⁺ = −σ/8. Since Spin(4) = SU(2) × SU(2) and S±
    are the defining representations on H = C², there is a quaternionic
    structure j (j² = −1, antilinear) commuting with D; ker D⁺ and ker D⁻
    are H-vector spaces, so their complex dimensions are even. Hence
    σ/8 is even: σ ≡ 0 mod 16 (ROKHLIN).
    (b) K3: σ = −16, ind = 2 (ker D⁺ = H^{0,0} ⊕ H^{0,2} = C², ker D⁻ =
    H^{0,1} = 0 — Kähler with c₁ = 0 identifies D with ∂̄ + ∂̄*). T⁴:
    σ = 0, ind = 0. X₆: w₂ = c₁ mod 2 = −2h mod 2 = 0, spin; σ = −64
    (D5), ind = 8. σ = 8 would give index −1, odd: impossible (so the E₈
    form is not the form of a smooth spin 4-manifold). σ = −32 is allowed
    and realized by K3 # K3 (index 4).
    (c) For SU(2), c₁ = 0 so ch₂ = (c₁² − 2c₂)/2 = −c₂. S⁴: σ = 0, ind =
    −k; so dim ker D⁻_E ≥ k for any connection (the classical count of
    zero modes in an instanton background). K3: rk·2 − k = 4 − k. Spin
    surface: Â = 1 + (nothing in degree 2), ch(L) = 1 + c₁(L), so ind =
    ∫c₁(L) = d. Holomorphic check: S⁺ = K^{1/2} (a square root of K, deg
    g − 1), D⁺ ~ ∂̄ on K^{1/2} ⊗ L, RR: (g − 1 + d) + 1 − g = d ✓.
    Common mistakes: applying the Â-formula to a non-spin manifold (CP²
    has "Â = −⅛"); believing that integrality of Â alone gives Rokhlin
    (it gives only σ ≡ 0 mod 8); the sign of ch₂ for SU(2)-bundles.
    Now you try: a spin 8-manifold has p₁ = 0, p₂ = −1440. Find Â and
    σ and check 896Â = p₁² − 4σ. (Â = 5760/5760 = 1; σ = −10080/45 =
    −224; 0 + 896 = 896 ✓)

D8. (a) Prove the LICHNEROWICZ formula D² = ∇*∇ + R/4 (sketch) and
    deduce: on a closed spin manifold with R > 0, Â(M) = 0; with R ≥ 0
    and ker D ≠ 0 there is a parallel spinor and Ric = 0. (b) Which of
    K3, X₆, the Enriques surface, CP², HP², S² × Σ₂, T⁴ admit a metric of
    positive scalar curvature, and what decides it? (c) The Dirac
    eigenvalues on the unit Sⁿ are ±(n/2 + k), k ≥ 0. Compare the
    smallest λ² with the Lichnerowicz bound R/4 and Friedrich's bound
    nR/(4(n − 1)) for n = 2, 3, 4.
    Answer: (b) no, no, no, yes, yes, yes, no. (c) λ²_min = n²/4 (1, 9/4,
    4); R/4 = n(n − 1)/4 (½, 3/2, 3); Friedrich = n²/4 (sharp).
    Teacher explains: (a) At p choose an orthonormal frame with ∇eᵢ = 0
    at p. Then D² = ∑ᵢⱼc(eᵢ)c(eⱼ)∇ᵢ∇ⱼ = ∑ᵢc(eᵢ)²∇ᵢ∇ᵢ + ∑_{i<j}c(eᵢ)c(eⱼ)
    (∇ᵢ∇ⱼ − ∇ⱼ∇ᵢ) = ∇*∇ + ∑_{i<j}c(eᵢ)c(eⱼ)R^S(eᵢ, eⱼ). The spin curvature
    is R^S(eᵢ, eⱼ) = ¼∑ₖₗR_{ijkl}c(eₖ)c(eₗ) (up to sign convention), so
    the last term is ⅛∑R_{ijkl}c(eᵢ)c(eⱼ)c(eₖ)c(eₗ); the terms with i, j,
    k distinct cancel by the first Bianchi identity and the rest add up
    to R/4. Integrating ⟨D²ψ, ψ⟩: ‖Dψ‖² = ‖∇ψ‖² + ∫(R/4)|ψ|². If R > 0,
    Dψ = 0 forces ψ = 0; so ker D⁺ = ker D⁻ = 0 and Â = ind D⁺ = 0. If
    R ≥ 0 and Dψ = 0 then ∇ψ = 0; a parallel spinor is killed by the
    curvature, and ∑ⱼc(eⱼ)Ric(eⱼ)ψ = 0 forces Ric = 0.
    (b) K3: spin, Â = 2 ≠ 0: no PSC (and by (a) a scalar-flat metric is
    Ricci-flat: the Calabi-Yau metrics). X₆: spin, Â = 8: no. Enriques
    E: not spin (σ = −8), but its universal (double) cover is K3; a PSC
    metric on E would lift to K3: no. CP²: Fubini-Study has R > 0; no
    contradiction since CP² is not spin. HP²: the symmetric metric has
    R > 0, consistent with Â(HP²) = 0. S²(r) × Σ₂ (K = −1 on Σ₂): R =
    2/r² − 2 > 0 for r < 1: yes. T⁴: Â = 0, so Lichnerowicz says nothing,
    but T⁴ has NO PSC metric (Schoen-Yau, Gromov-Lawson).
    (c) λ²_min = (n/2)². Lichnerowicz: λ² ≥ min R/4 = n(n − 1)/4, i.e. ½,
    3/2, 3, not sharp. Friedrich (using a modified connection ∇ₓ + λc(X)/n):
    λ² ≥ n·n(n − 1)/(4(n − 1)) = n²/4: 1, 9/4, 4, EQUALITY on the sphere
    (Killing spinors).
    Common mistakes: "Â(CP²) ≠ 0 so CP² has no PSC" (not spin!);
    "Â = 0 ⇒ PSC" (T⁴); pushing PSC DOWN a covering (it lifts UP, which
    is what the Enriques argument uses).
    Now you try: does K3 × K3 admit PSC? K3 × S²? (no: spin with Â =
    2·2 = 4; yes: shrink the S² factor)

D9. (a) Prove the McKEAN-SINGER formula ind D⁺ = Tr e^{−tD⁻D⁺} −
    Tr e^{−tD⁺D⁻} for all t > 0 and explain why this makes the index
    LOCAL. (b) On the unit S² (eigenvalues l(l + 1), multiplicity 2l + 1)
    show Tr e^{−tΔ} = 1/t + 1/3 + t/15 + O(t²) and check a₁ = R/6. Show
    that the supertrace ∑(−1)^p Tr e^{−tΔ_p} equals 2 exactly, and derive
    Gauss-Bonnet for surfaces from the a₁-coefficients. (c) Show Tr e^{−tH}
    = 1/(2 sinh t) for H = −d²/dx² + x² and explain its role (Mehler,
    Getzler).
    Answer: (a), (b), (c) below; a₁-supertrace density = R, χ = (1/4π)∫R.
    Teacher explains: (a) D⁻D⁺ and D⁺D⁻ are non-negative self-adjoint
    elliptic, with discrete spectra. If D⁻D⁺u = λu, λ > 0, then v = D⁺u
    ≠ 0 and D⁺D⁻v = λv; so D⁺ is an isomorphism E_λ(D⁻D⁺) → E_λ(D⁺D⁻)
    (inverse λ⁻¹D⁻). Hence Tr e^{−tD⁻D⁺} − Tr e^{−tD⁺D⁻} = ∑_λ e^{−tλ}
    (dim E_λ⁺ − dim E_λ⁻) = dim ker D⁺ − dim ker D⁻, independent of t. As
    t → 0 the heat kernels have expansions k_t(x, x) ~ (4πt)^{−n/2}∑tʲ
    aⱼ(x) with aⱼ polynomial in curvature and derivatives, so ind D⁺ =
    (4π)^{−n/2}∫ str a_{n/2}(x)dx: an integral of a LOCAL density (and the
    singular terms must cancel).
    (b) Euler-Maclaurin with f(l) = (2l + 1)e^{−tl(l+1)}: ∫₀^∞f = 1/t,
    + f(0)/2 = ½, − (1/12)f′(0) = −(2 − t)/12, + (1/720)f‴(0) = (1/720)
    (−12t) + O(t²) (as f = 2l + 1 − t(2l³ + 3l² + l) + O(t²)). Total:
    1/t + ½ − 1/6 + t/12 − t/60 = 1/t + 1/3 + t/15. Check: (4πt)^{−1}
    (∫1 + t∫R/6) = (4πt)^{−1}(4π + t·(2/6)·4π) = 1/t + 1/3 ✓ (R = 2K = 2).
    Forms: Δ₂ ≅ Δ₀ (Hodge *), and the 1-eigenforms are df, *df for
    non-constant eigenfunctions f: Tr e^{−tΔ₁} = 2(Tr e^{−tΔ₀} − 1). So
    Θ − 2(Θ − 1) + Θ = 2 = χ(S²) for all t ✓. Locally, for Laplace-type
    ∇*∇ + 𝓔, a₁ = tr(R/6 − 𝓔): functions and 2-forms give R/6; 1-forms
    (Weitzenböck 𝓔 = Ric, rank 2) give 2R/6 − R = −2R/3. Supertrace
    density R/6 + 2R/3 + R/6 = R, so χ = (1/4π)∫R dA = (1/2π)∫K dA.
    (c) H has eigenvalues 2n + 1 (Hermite functions), so Tr e^{−tH} =
    ∑e^{−t(2n+1)} = e^{−t}/(1 − e^{−2t}) = 1/(2 sinh t). Getzler rescaling
    (x ↦ x/√t, t^{−1/2}Clifford degree) turns D² at a point into a
    harmonic oscillator whose "frequencies" are the curvature 2-forms;
    Mehler's kernel at the origin gives (4πt)^{−n/2}det^{1/2}((Ω/2)/
    sinh(Ω/2)): the Â-form, proving the local index theorem.
    Common mistakes: expecting the individual traces (not the supertrace)
    to be t-independent; forgetting the multiplicity 2 of 1-form
    eigenvalues; sign of 𝓔 in a₁ = R/6 − 𝓔.
    Now you try: the constant term of Tr e^{−tΔ₀} on a closed surface of
    genus g, and the leading term on a flat torus of area A. ((1 − g)/3,
    i.e. χ/6; A/(4πt), with exponentially small error)

D10. (a) State the Lefschetz fixed point formula and the Atiyah-Bott
    formula; show that every continuous self-map of CP²ᵐ has a fixed
    point. (b) Compute L(f) and the fixed points with their indices for
    the torus maps f_A(x) = Ax mod Z², A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]], −I, 3I, and
    for f(z) = z^d (d ≥ 2) on CP¹. (c) Let g(z) = λz (|λ| = 1, λ ≠ 1)
    act on H⁰(CP¹, O(k)) = polynomials of degree ≤ k by pullback. Verify
    the holomorphic Lefschetz formula and explain the link with the Weyl
    character formula.
    Answer: (b) −1 (1 point, index −1); 4 (4 points, +1 each); 4 (4
    points, +1 each); 1 + d (d + 1 points, +1 each). (c) ∑ⱼλʲ =
    1/(1 − λ) + λᵏ/(1 − λ⁻¹).
    Teacher explains: (a) L(f) = ∑(−1)ⁱtr(f*|Hⁱ(M; Q)) = ∑_{f(p)=p}
    sign det(1 − df_p) for transverse fixed points. ATIYAH-BOTT: for an
    elliptic complex with a lift of f, L = ∑_p ∑ᵢ(−1)ⁱtr(φᵢ,ₚ)/|det(1 −
    df_p)|; for de Rham ∑(−1)ⁱtr Λⁱ(df_p) = det(1 − df_p), recovering the
    sign; for Dolbeault the contribution is 1/det_C(1 − df_p). CP²ᵐ:
    H* = Q[h]/(h^{2m+1}), f*h = dh, L = 1 + d + ... + d^{2m}. For d = 1
    this is 2m + 1; otherwise (d^{2m+1} − 1)/(d − 1), zero only if
    d^{2m+1} = 1, i.e. d = 1. So L ≠ 0 and f has a fixed point. (On CP³
    the map [z₀:z₁:z₂:z₃] ↦ [−z̄₁:z̄₀:−z̄₃:z̄₂] has d = −1 and no fixed
    point.)
    (b) On T², f* = A on H¹, det A on H², so L = 1 − tr A + det A =
    det(I − A). [[2, 1], [1, 1]]: 1 − 3 + 1 = −1; fixed points solve (A −
    I)x ∈ Z², |det(A − I)| = 1 of them (x = 0), index sign(−1) = −1.
    −I: det(2I) = 4; fixed points 2x ≡ 0: the four half-periods, each
    +1. 3I: det(−2I) = 4; again 2x ≡ 0, four points, sign det = +1.
    z^d: f* is 1 on H⁰ and d on H², L = 1 + d. Fixed points: 0, ∞ and
    the d − 1 roots of z^{d−1} = 1. At 0 and ∞, df = 0 and det(1 − 0) = 1;
    at a root df = dz^{d−1} = d, det_R(1 − d) = |1 − d|² > 0. So d + 1
    points of index +1 ✓ (holomorphic fixed points always count +1).
    (c) Trace on span{1, z, ..., zᵏ}: zʲ ↦ λʲzʲ, total ∑_{j=0}^k λʲ =
    (1 − λ^{k+1})/(1 − λ). Fixed points: 0 with dg = λ and fibre weight
    1 (frame 1); ∞ with coordinate w = 1/z, dg = λ⁻¹, fibre weight λᵏ
    (frame zᵏ). Contributions 1/(1 − λ) + λᵏ/(1 − λ⁻¹) = (1 − λ^{k+1})/
    (1 − λ) ✓ (h¹ = 0 for k ≥ 0). For λ = diag(e^{iθ}, e^{−iθ}) acting on
    CP¹ = SU(2)/T this is exactly the Weyl character formula for the
    (k + 1)-dimensional representation; the equivariant index theorem
    gives Borel-Weil-Bott this way. The same technique shows
    (ATIYAH-HIRZEBRUCH) Â(M) = 0 for spin M with a non-trivial S¹-action.
    Common mistakes: counting fixed points without signs; forgetting the
    fibre factor tr(g|E_p); using the holomorphic formula for a map that
    is not holomorphic.
    Now you try: g[x:y:z] = [x : e^{iα}y : e^{iβ}z] on CP². Fixed points
    have rotation angles (α, β), (−α, β − α), (−β, α − β); show ∑ −cot(θ₁/2)
    cot(θ₂/2) = 1. (It equals σ(CP²) since g acts trivially on H²; use
    cot(u − v) = (cot u cot v + 1)/(cot v − cot u).)

D11. (a) Define the index bundle of a family of Fredholm operators and
    state the Atiyah-Jänich theorem. Show by the family Aₜ = −i d/dθ + t
    on S¹ (t ∈ R) that kernels can jump while the index stays constant.
    (b) Define spectral flow and compute it for Aₜ over t ∈ [−½, 5/2];
    show that e^{−iθ}Aₜe^{iθ} = Aₜ₊₁ and explain why t ↦ Aₜ, t ∈ [0, 1],
    represents a generator of K¹(S¹) = Z. (c) State the families index
    theorem and describe the family of Dirac operators on T² twisted by
    the flat line bundles L_ϑ, ϑ ∈ T̂² (the dual torus).
    Answer: (a) dim ker Aₜ = 1 for t ∈ Z, 0 otherwise; ind = 0. (b) sf =
    3; the loop has sf = 1. (c) ind has rank 0 but c₁ = ± generator of
    H²(T̂²; Z): a non-trivial virtual bundle.
    Teacher explains: (a) For Pₓ : H → H′ Fredholm, x ∈ X compact, choose a
    finite-dimensional V ⊂ H′ with ran Pₓ + V = H′ for all x (possible by
    compactness). Then Eₓ = Pₓ⁻¹(V) has constant dimension ind Pₓ +
    dim V, forms a vector bundle, and ind P := [E] − [V] ∈ K⁰(X) (well
    defined; = [ker] − [coker] if these have constant rank).
    ATIYAH-JÄNICH: ind : [X, Fred(H)] → K⁰(X) is a bijection (X compact;
    H separable infinite-dimensional); for X = pt: π₀(Fred) = Z. Aₜ has
    eigenfunctions e^{inθ} with eigenvalues n + t: a zero eigenvalue
    exactly when t ∈ Z, so dim ker jumps 0 → 1 → 0; Aₜ is self-adjoint,
    so ind Aₜ = 0 throughout.
    (b) SPECTRAL FLOW sf = (number of eigenvalues crossing 0 upward) −
    (downward). n + t = 0 at t = −n; for t ∈ (−½, 5/2) this happens for
    n = 0, −1, −2, each upward: sf = 3. Conjugation: e^{−iθ}(−i d/dθ)(e^{iθ}u)
    = u − iu′, so e^{−iθ}Aₜe^{iθ} = Aₜ₊₁: the path on [0, 1] closes up to
    unitary equivalence, a loop in self-adjoint Fredholm operators with
    sf = 1 (one crossing, at t = 0 or t = 1 depending on endpoint
    conventions; take [−½, ½]). Spectral flow is the isomorphism π₁ ≅ Z
    = K¹(S¹), the "odd" index; it equals the index of ∂ₛ + A_{t(s)} on
    the cylinder R × S¹ (APS).
    (c) For a fibration M → X with fibres Z and a family D_x of Dirac
    operators twisted by E: ch(ind D) = ∫_{M/X}Â(T_{M/X})ch(E) in H*(X; Q)
    (for ∂̄: Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch). Torus: D_ϑ on T² with the
    trivial (periodic) spin structure twisted by L_ϑ has D_ϑ² = flat
    Laplacian on L_ϑ, so ker D_ϑ = 0 unless L_ϑ is trivial (ϑ = 0), where
    ker D⁺ = ker D⁻ = C. The index bundle has rank 0 (= ind on one
    fibre) but c₁(ind) is a generator of H²(T̂²) (the fibre integral of
    ch of the Poincaré bundle; sign depends on conventions): the jumping
    kernel at ϑ = 0 hides genuinely non-trivial topology.
    Common mistakes: defining ind as [ker] − [coker] when the ranks
    jump; calling a zero-index family trivial; ignoring endpoint
    conventions in spectral flow.
    Now you try: sf of Aₜ over [−3/2, −½] and over [5/2, −½] (reverse
    direction). (1; −3)

D12. (a) Define the ETA INVARIANT η(0) of a self-adjoint elliptic A and
    state the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem. (b) For A_a = −i d/dθ +
    a on S¹ (0 < a < 1) show η(0) = 1 − 2a using the Hurwitz zeta
    function ζ(s, a) = ∑_{n≥0}(n + a)^{−s}, ζ(0, a) = ½ − a; find η for
    a = ¼, ½, ¾ and for A₀ = −i d/dθ; describe the jumps of ξ = (h +
    η)/2. (c) State the APS signature formula and explain why η(Y) = 0
    when Y admits an orientation-reversing isometry; check it for D⁴.
    Answer: (b) ½, 0, −½; η(A₀) = 0 with h = 1; ξ = ½ − a on (0, 1),
    jumping by +1 at each integer. (c) σ(M) = ∫_M L − η(Y); σ(D⁴) = 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) η(s) = ∑_{λ≠0} sign(λ)|λ|^{−s} converges for
    Re s large, continues meromorphically, and is regular at s = 0 (a
    deep fact); η(0) measures the asymmetry of the spectrum. APS: on
    compact M with boundary Y, D = σ(∂ᵤ + A) in a collar, with the NON-
    LOCAL boundary condition P_{≥0}(u|_Y) = 0: D is Fredholm and
       ind D = ∫_M α₀(x)dx − (h + η_A(0))/2,  h = dim ker A,
    with α₀ the same local density as in the closed case. η is NOT given
    by a local integral (below), only its variation is.
    (b) Eigenvalues of A_a: n + a, n ∈ Z (eigenfunctions e^{inθ}).
    Positive ones: n + a, n ≥ 0, giving ζ(s, a). Negative ones: |λ| = m −
    a = (m − 1) + (1 − a), m ≥ 1, giving ζ(s, 1 − a). So η(s) = ζ(s, a)
    − ζ(s, 1 − a) and η(0) = (½ − a) − (½ − (1 − a)) = 1 − 2a. a = ¼: ½;
    a = ½: 0 (the spectrum ±½, ±3/2, ... is symmetric); a = ¾: −½. At a
    = 0 the spectrum is Z, symmetric: η = 0, h = 1, ξ = ½. For 0 < a < 1,
    ξ = ½ − a: it decreases continuously (dξ/da = −1, a local quantity)
    and jumps by +1 as a passes an integer, where an eigenvalue crosses
    0 upward: exactly the spectral flow of D11. Since all A_a are locally
    gauge-equivalent (u ↦ e^{−iaθ}u locally), yet η varies, η cannot be
    the integral of a local density.
    (c) For the signature operator the local term is L, and comparing
    the APS index with σ(M) (via L²-harmonic forms on M with a cylinder
    attached) absorbs h: σ(M) = ∫_M L − η(Y) (Y = ∂M, boundary
    orientation). Changing the orientation of Y replaces the odd
    signature operator A by −A, so η(−Y) = −η(Y); an orientation-reversing
    isometry gives η(Y) = −η(Y) = 0. For D⁴ take a rotationally
    symmetric metric that is a product near the round S³ = ∂D⁴. The
    reflection x₁ ↦ −x₁ is an orientation-reversing isometry of D⁴ and
    of S³: it fixes the L-form but reverses orientation, so ∫L = 0, and
    η(S³) = 0; indeed σ(D⁴) = 0 since H²(D⁴, S³) = 0 ✓.
    Common mistakes: forgetting h (the formula would jump in the wrong
    way); expecting η = 0 for symmetric-looking operators (A_a has
    non-symmetric spectrum); treating η like a local invariant.
    Now you try: compute ξ(A_a) at a = 0.9 and a = 1, and the jump.
    (ξ(0.9) = −0.4, ξ(1) = ½: jump +1 in the limit a ↑ 1)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
