      o) 5/2 − ½ = 2; ⅓ + ⅔ = 1

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) The index of a Fredholm operator is unchanged by compact
        perturbations.
     b) dim ker P is a homotopy invariant of elliptic operators P.
     c) A formally self-adjoint elliptic P : E → E on a closed manifold
        has index 0.
     d) Every elliptic differential operator on a closed odd-dimensional
        manifold has index 0.
     e) Every elliptic pseudodifferential operator on S¹ has index 0.
     f) Â(M) is an integer for every closed oriented 4k-manifold.
     g) Every smooth closed spin 4-manifold has σ ≡ 0 mod 16.
     h) Rokhlin's theorem excludes a smooth closed spin 4-manifold with
        intersection form E₈ ⊕ E₈.
     i) A closed spin manifold with Â ≠ 0 admits no PSC metric.
     j) A closed spin manifold with Â = 0 admits a PSC metric.
     k) The signature of a closed smooth 4-manifold is divisible by 3.
     l) p₁ of a closed smooth oriented 4-manifold is divisible by 3.
     m) The Todd genus of a compact complex manifold is an integer.
     n) str e^{−tD²} depends on t.
     o) The η-invariant is the integral of a local density.
     p) The index of the untwisted Dirac operator on a closed spin
        surface depends on the spin structure.
     q) Every continuous self-map of CP² has a fixed point.
     r) Every continuous self-map of CP³ has a fixed point.
     s) A closed spin manifold with a non-trivial S¹-action has Â = 0.
     t) σ and χ are multiplicative under finite covers of closed
        manifolds.
     u) The index bundle of a family is the vector bundle x ↦ ker Pₓ.
     v) Riemann-Roch for curves is the Atiyah-Singer theorem for ∂̄_L.
     Answers:
       a) T
       b) F: Aₜ = −i d/dθ + t on S¹ (kernel C at t ∈ Z, 0 otherwise)
       c) T         d) T (Atiyah-Singer)
       e) F: the Toeplitz-type operator T_z ⊕ 1 on H² ⊕ (H²)^⊥ = L²(S¹)
          has index −1
       f) F: Â(CP²) = −1/8         g) T (Rokhlin)
       h) F: σ = 16 is allowed; Donaldson's theorem excludes it
       i) T (Lichnerowicz)
       j) F: T⁴ (Â = 0, no PSC)    k) F: σ(CP²) = 1
       l) T (p₁ = 3σ)               m) T (it equals χ(X, O))
       n) F: McKean-Singer, it equals ind D⁺ for all t
       o) F: η(A_a) = 1 − 2a varies although all A_a are locally
          gauge-equivalent
       p) F: the index is always 0 (only dim ker D± depends on it)
       q) T (L = 1 + d + d² ≠ 0)
       r) F: [z₀:z₁:z₂:z₃] ↦ [−z̄₁:z̄₀:−z̄₃:z̄₂]
       s) T (Atiyah-Hirzebruch)
       t) T (for d-fold covers χ and σ multiply by d)
       u) F: dim ker Aₜ jumps; ind P lives only in K⁰(X)
       v) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build everything on the single identity "index = dim ker
  − dim coker, and it does not move under deformation". Start with
  matrices (index = p − q), then the shift and Toeplitz operators (index
  = −winding number, a picture of a loop around 0), then d/dθ + a on the
  circle, then the four classical operators with a table on the board:
  d + d* ↦ χ = ∫e, signature operator ↦ σ = ∫L, ∂̄_E ↦ χ(E) = ∫ch Td,
  Dirac ↦ Â = ∫Â ch. For every computation insist on three checks: the
  top-degree part only, the correct normalisation (p₁ = c₁² − 2c₂, x/2
  inside Â, ∫hⁿ = 1), and an integrality or known-value sanity check
  (Td(CPⁿ) = 1, σ(CP²) = 1, Â(K3) = 2, χ(S²) = 2). For heat kernels do
  the S² computation by hand with Euler-Maclaurin before any theory.
  Harder: prove the Toeplitz index theorem in full and deduce Bott
  periodicity for π₁(GL(C)); prove McKean-Singer and compute a₁ for a
  general Laplace-type operator; work through Getzler's rescaling and
  Mehler's formula to prove the local index theorem for the spin Dirac
  operator; derive the Atiyah-Bott fixed point formula from the heat
  kernel and recover the Weyl character formula for SU(3) on the flag
  manifold; prove Rokhlin's theorem via the quaternionic structure and
  Milnor's exotic spheres via the signature theorem; read the APS paper
  and compute η for lens spaces; and state and use the families index
  theorem to see why determinant line bundles and anomalies appear.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.10 — OPERATOR ALGEBRAS: C*-ALGEBRAS, VON NEUMANN ALGEBRAS,
NONCOMMUTATIVE GEOMETRY
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - C*-ALGEBRA: a Banach *-algebra with the C*-IDENTITY ‖a*a‖ = ‖a‖².
    Examples: B(H), K(H) (compact operators), Mₙ(C), C₀(X), closed
    *-subalgebras of B(H). ℓ¹(Z) with convolution is a Banach *-algebra
    but NOT a C*-algebra. The C*-norm is unique: for a = a*, ‖a‖ = r(a) =
    lim ‖aⁿ‖^{1/n}; more generally ‖a‖ = r(a) for NORMAL a, and ‖a‖² =
    r(a*a) always. *-HOMOMORPHISMS ARE CONTRACTIVE, injective ones are
    isometric, and their ranges are closed.
  - SPECTRUM σ(a) = {λ : a − λ not invertible}: non-empty and compact
    (Gelfand-Mazur: a Banach division algebra is C). SPECTRAL
    PERMANENCE: σ_B(a) = σ_A(a) for a C*-subalgebra A ⊆ B. a = a* ⇒ σ(a)
    ⊆ R; u unitary ⇒ σ(u) ⊆ T; p projection ⇒ σ(p) ⊆ {0, 1}. Unilateral
    shift S: σ(S) = closed unit disc D̄, σ(S*S) = {1}, σ(SS*) = {0, 1}.
  - GELFAND-NAIMARK (COMMUTATIVE): for A commutative, the CHARACTER SPACE
    Â (non-zero multiplicative functionals, weak-* topology) is locally
    compact Hausdorff and the GELFAND TRANSFORM a ↦ â, â(χ) = χ(a), is an
    isometric *-isomorphism A ≅ C₀(Â); σ(a) = â(Â) (∪{0} if non-unital).
    DUALITY: unital commutative C*-algebras ≅ (compact Hausdorff spaces)ᵒᵖ.
    Â for C(X) is X; for c (convergent sequences) N ∪ {∞}; unitization
    of C₀(X) is C(X⁺). For ℓ¹(Z) the Gelfand spectrum is T (Wiener).
  - CONTINUOUS FUNCTIONAL CALCULUS: a normal ⇒ C*(1, a) ≅ C(σ(a)), f ↦
    f(a), with SPECTRAL MAPPING σ(f(a)) = f(σ(a)). POSITIVE: a ≥ 0 ⇔ a =
    a* and σ(a) ⊆ [0, ∞) ⇔ a = b*b ⇔ a = h² with h = h*. Unique positive
    square root; |a| = (a*a)^{1/2}; a = a₊ − a₋ with a₊a₋ = 0. POLAR
    DECOMPOSITION a = v|a| (v a partial isometry) in B(H) or any vN
    algebra.
  - STATES: positive φ with ‖φ‖ = φ(1) = 1. GNS: every state gives a
    cyclic representation (π_φ, H_φ, ξ_φ) with φ(a) = ⟨π_φ(a)ξ_φ, ξ_φ⟩;
    H_φ = completion of A/N_φ, N_φ = {a : φ(a*a) = 0}. φ PURE (extreme)
    ⇔ π_φ irreducible. GELFAND-NAIMARK: every C*-algebra embeds
    isometrically in some B(H). States of C(X) = probability measures;
    pure states = point evaluations. States of Mₙ: a ↦ tr(ρa), ρ ≥ 0,
    tr ρ = 1; pure ⇔ rank ρ = 1; dim H_φ = n·rank(ρ).
  - COMPLETELY POSITIVE maps: Φ ⊗ id_{Mₖ} positive for all k. STINESPRING
    Φ(a) = V*π(a)V; CHOI: Φ : Mₙ → Mₘ is CP ⇔ Choi matrix ∑eᵢⱼ ⊗ Φ(eᵢⱼ)
    ≥ 0. The TRANSPOSE is positive but not CP. Positive maps with
    commutative domain or range are CP.
  - IDEALS: closed two-sided ideals are self-adjoint, quotients are
    C*-algebras; ideals of C(X) ↔ closed subsets. Mₙ and K(H) are simple;
    for separable infinite-dimensional H the only closed ideals of B(H)
    are 0, K(H), B(H). CALKIN algebra Q = B(H)/K(H).
  - FREDHOLM THEORY: T Fredholm ⇔ dim ker T, dim coker T < ∞ ⇔ (ATKINSON)
    T invertible in Q. ind T = dim ker T − dim ker T* is locally constant,
    invariant under compact perturbation, ind(ST) = ind S + ind T.
    TOEPLITZ: T_f = P M_f P on the Hardy space H²; for continuous f
    without zeros on T, T_f is Fredholm with ind T_f = −wind(f, 0).
    TOEPLITZ EXTENSION 0 → K → 𝒯 → C(T) → 0, 𝒯 = C*(S).
  - K-THEORY: K₀(A) = Grothendieck group of Murray-von Neumann classes of
    projections in M_∞(A) (unital A); K₁(A) = U_∞(A)/U_∞(A)₀. Homotopy
    invariant, stable (K(A ⊗ K) = K(A)), continuous under inductive
    limits, SIX-TERM EXACT SEQUENCE for 0 → J → A → A/J → 0 with index
    map ∂ : K₁(A/J) → K₀(J) (Fredholm index) and exponential map. BOTT
    PERIODICITY Kᵢ(C₀(R²) ⊗ A) ≅ Kᵢ(A). K₀(C(X)) = K⁰(X) (SERRE-SWAN:
    f.g. projective C(X)-modules = vector bundles). CUNTZ O_n (∑sᵢsᵢ* =
    1, sᵢ*sᵢ = 1): K₀ = Z/(n − 1), K₁ = 0.
  - AF ALGEBRAS: inductive limits of finite-dimensional C*-algebras
    ⊕M_{nᵢ}, encoded by BRATTELI diagrams (multiplicity matrices); K₀ is
    the inductive limit of Z^k along the multiplicity matrices, K₁ = 0.
    ELLIOTT: AF algebras are classified by (K₀, K₀⁺, [1]). UHF algebras
    are classified by the SUPERNATURAL number (GLIMM): CAR = M_{2^∞} has
    K₀ = Z[1/2], [1] = 1; M_{2^∞} ⊗ M_{3^∞} ≅ M_{6^∞}. A unital embedding
    M_k → Mₙ exists iff k | n.
  - VON NEUMANN ALGEBRAS: unital *-subalgebras M ⊆ B(H) closed in the
    weak (WOT) or strong operator topology. BICOMMUTANT THEOREM: M = M″
    ⇔ M WOT-closed ⇔ M SOT-closed. KAPLANSKY DENSITY. BOREL functional
    calculus; M is the norm-closed span of its projections. Examples:
    B(H), L^∞(X, μ) on L²(μ) (maximal abelian), finite dimensional
    ⊕M_{nᵢ} ⊗ 1_{mᵢ} with commutant ⊕1 ⊗ M_{mᵢ}.
  - FACTORS (Z(M) = C1) and MURRAY-VON NEUMANN TYPES via p ~ q (p = v*v,
    q = vv*): I_n (B(Cⁿ)), I_∞ (B(ℓ²)), II₁ (finite, faithful normal
    trace, dim range [0, 1]), II_∞ (II₁ ⊗ B(ℓ²), range [0, ∞]), III (all
    non-zero projections equivalent, no semifinite trace). In a II₁
    factor p ~ q ⇔ τ(p) = τ(q).
  - GROUP VON NEUMANN ALGEBRAS L(Γ) = {λ_g}″ ⊆ B(ℓ²(Γ)), trace τ(x) =
    ⟨xδₑ, δₑ⟩, commutant R(Γ). L(Γ) is a factor ⇔ Γ is ICC (every g ≠ e
    has an infinite conjugacy class); then it is II₁. L(Z) ≅ L^∞(T);
    L(G) for G finite ≅ ⊕ M_{dᵢ} (irreducible degrees). HYPERFINITE II₁
    factor R = ⊗(M₂, tr)″ ≅ L(S_∞) ≅ L(Γ) for every amenable ICC Γ
    (CONNES); L(F₂) is a non-hyperfinite II₁ factor. Compressions:
    L(F_r)_t ≅ L(F_{1+(r−1)/t²}), e.g. L(F₂)_{1/2} ≅ L(F₅).
  - JONES INDEX [M : N] = dim_N L²(M) for II₁ factors N ⊆ M: multiplicative
    in towers, [M : N] ∈ {4cos²(π/n) : n ≥ 3} ∪ [4, ∞]. Examples: R^G ⊆ R
    and R ⊆ R ⋊ G (G finite, outer action) have index |G|; N ⊆ Mₖ(N)
    has index k²; L(H) ⊆ L(Γ) has index [Γ : H].
  - TOMITA-TAKESAKI: for a faithful normal state φ, S(xξ_φ) = x*ξ_φ has
    polar decomposition S = JΔ^{1/2}; JMJ = M′, Δ^{it}MΔ^{−it} = M; the
    MODULAR GROUP σ_t^φ(x) = Δ^{it}xΔ^{−it} is characterized by the KMS
    CONDITION at β = −1 (physics: β-KMS states = equilibrium). On Mₙ
    with φ = tr(ρ ·): σ_t(x) = ρ^{it}xρ^{−it}, Sp Δ = {λᵢ/λⱼ}. CONNES:
    S(M) = ∩_φ Sp Δ_φ classifies III_λ: S = {0} ∪ λ^Z (0 < λ < 1),
    [0, ∞) (III₁), {0, 1} (III₀); POWERS factors R_λ are III_λ.
  - NONCOMMUTATIVE GEOMETRY: SPECTRAL TRIPLE (A, H, D): A acting on H, D
    self-adjoint with compact resolvent and [D, a] bounded. Model:
    (C^∞(M), L²(M, S), Dirac). CONNES DISTANCE d(p, q) = sup{|a(p) −
    a(q)| : ‖[D, a]‖ ≤ 1} recovers the geodesic distance. Two-point
    space A = C², D = (0 m; m 0): d = 1/|m|. Circle: D = −i d/dθ, Sp D =
    Z, ‖[D, f]‖ = ‖f′‖_∞. METRIC DIMENSION p: |D|^{−p} ∈ L^{1,∞}.
  - DIXMIER TRACE Tr_ω(T) = lim_ω (1/log N)∑_{k≤N} μ_k(T) on L^{1,∞}; it
    vanishes on trace class operators. CONNES TRACE THEOREM: Tr_ω of a
    classical pseudodifferential operator of order −n is the Wodzicki
    residue/(n(2π)ⁿ). Tr_ω(f|D|^{−n}) ∝ ∫_M f dvol; on S¹ (length 2π)
    Tr_ω(f|D|^{−1}) = (1/π)∫f dθ; on T² = R²/(2πZ)², Tr_ω((1 + Δ)^{−1})
    = π.
  - NONCOMMUTATIVE TORUS A_θ = C*(u, v unitaries : vu = e^{2πiθ}uv) =
    C(T) ⋊_θ Z. θ irrational ⇒ simple with unique trace τ(uᵐvⁿ) = δ_{m0}
    δ_{n0}; τ(K₀(A_θ)) = Z + θZ, and every value in (Z + θZ) ∩ [0, 1] is
    the trace of a projection (RIEFFEL). A_θ ≅ A_θ′ ⇔ θ′ ≡ ±θ mod Z;
    MORITA EQUIVALENT ⇔ θ′ = (aθ + b)/(cθ + d) ∈ GL₂(Z)·θ. PIMSNER-
    VOICULESCU for C(T) ⋊_θ Z gives K₀ = K₁ = Z².
  - CYCLIC COHOMOLOGY and the CHERN CHARACTER: HH₀(A) = A/[A, A],
    HH_*(C^∞(M)) = Ω^*(M) (HKR); HP_even/odd(C^∞(M)) = H_dR^even/odd(M)
    (CONNES); HC_{2k}(C) = C, HC_{odd}(C) = 0; Morita invariance
    (HH_*(Mₙ(A)) = HH_*(A)). The PAIRING ⟨φ, K_*(A)⟩ of cyclic cocycles
    with K-theory: φ(f⁰, f¹) = (1/2πi)∫f⁰df¹ on C^∞(S¹) gives ⟨φ,
    [e^{ikθ}]⟩ = k (winding number); the index pairing ⟨[u], [D]⟩ is a
    Fredholm index. The 2-cocycle of A_θ is integral on K₀ (quantized
    Hall conductance).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Let A be a unital commutative C*-algebra. Show that every
    character χ has ‖χ‖ = 1, that χ(a*) = conj χ(a), and deduce that the
    Gelfand transform A → C(Â) is an isometric *-isomorphism. (b) Find
    the character space and the image of the Gelfand transform for:
    (i) c = convergent sequences; (ii) C*(1, N) ⊆ M₄ with N = diag(1, i,
    −1, i); (iii) ℓ¹(Z) (a Banach *-algebra), and compute σ(δ₁ + δ₋₁) and
    σ(2δ₀ + δ₁) there.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) (i) N ∪ {∞}, c ≅ C(N ∪ {∞});
    (ii) σ(N) = {1, i, −1}, C*(1, N) ≅ C³; (iii) T, â(z) = ∑a(n)zⁿ;
    σ(δ₁ + δ₋₁) = [−2, 2], σ(2δ₀ + δ₁) = {λ : |λ − 2| = 1}.
    Teacher explains: (a) χ(a) ∈ σ(a) because χ(a − χ(a)1) = 0 and a
    kernel element of a character cannot be invertible; so |χ(a)| ≤ r(a)
    ≤ ‖a‖, and χ(1) = 1 gives ‖χ‖ = 1. For h = h*, e^{ith} is unitary
    (its adjoint is e^{−ith}), so |χ(e^{ith})| ≤ 1 and also |χ(e^{−ith})|
    ≤ 1; since χ(e^{ith}) = e^{itχ(h)} for all real t, Im χ(h) = 0. Write
    a = h + ik to get χ(a*) = conj χ(a). Then ‖â‖_∞ = r(a) and, for any a,
      ‖a‖² = ‖a*a‖ = r(a*a) = ‖(a*a)^‸‖_∞ = ‖|â|²‖_∞ = ‖â‖²_∞,
    using ‖h‖ = r(h) for h = h* (from ‖h²‖ = ‖h‖², so ‖h^{2ᵏ}‖^{2^{−k}} =
    ‖h‖). So the transform is an isometric *-homomorphism; its image is a
    closed *-subalgebra containing 1 and separating points of Â, hence
    equals C(Â) by Stone-Weierstrass.
    (b)(i) Characters of c: χₙ(x) = xₙ and χ_∞(x) = lim xₙ; χₙ → χ_∞
    weak-*, so Â = N ∪ {∞}, the one-point compactification.
    (ii) C*(1, N) ≅ C(σ(N)) with σ(N) = {1, i, −1} (the repeated
    eigenvalue i counts once): C*(1, N) = {diag(f(1), f(i), f(−1), f(i))}
    ≅ C³, dimension 3, not 4.
    (iii) A character of ℓ¹(Z) is determined by z = χ(δ₁); |z| ≤ 1 and
    |z⁻¹| = |χ(δ₋₁)| ≤ 1 force z ∈ T. So â(z) = ∑ a(n)zⁿ (Fourier
    series), and σ(a) = â(T): δ₁ + δ₋₁ ↦ z + z̄ = 2cos t, range [−2, 2];
    2δ₀ + δ₁ ↦ 2 + z, the circle of radius 1 about 2, so 0 ∉ σ and it is
    invertible, with inverse ½∑_{n≥0}(−½)ⁿδₙ.
    ℓ¹(Z) is not C*: f = δ₀ + δ₁ − δ₂ has ‖f*f‖₁ = 5 ≠ 9 = ‖f‖₁².
    Common mistakes: counting eigenvalues with multiplicity for C*(1, N);
    forgetting ∞ in the spectrum of c; thinking the Gelfand transform of
    ℓ¹(Z) is isometric (it is only injective, ‖â‖_∞ ≤ ‖a‖₁).
    Now try: character space of C*(1, S + S*)? (σ(S + S*) = [−2, 2], so
    C([−2, 2]).)

D2. (a) Prove that ‖a‖ = r(a) for every normal a in a C*-algebra, and
    that ‖a‖² = r(a*a) for all a; conclude a *-algebra has at most one
    C*-norm. (b) Compute ‖a‖ and r(a) for a = (0 1; 0 0), (1 1; 0 1),
    (1 2; 3 4), (2 1; 1 2), and describe σ(S), σ(S*S), σ(SS*) for the
    unilateral shift S on ℓ²(N).
    Answer: (b) 1 and 0; φ = (1 + √5)/2 and 1; √(15 + √221) ≈ 5.465 and
    (5 + √33)/2 ≈ 5.372; 3 and 3. σ(S) = D̄, σ(S*S) = {1}, σ(SS*) = {0, 1}.
    Teacher explains: (a) For h = h*: ‖h²‖ = ‖h*h‖ = ‖h‖², by induction
    ‖h^{2ᵏ}‖ = ‖h‖^{2ᵏ}, and Beurling-Gelfand r(h) = lim‖hⁿ‖^{1/n} gives
    r(h) = ‖h‖. For normal a: ‖aⁿ‖² = ‖(aⁿ)*aⁿ‖ = ‖(a*a)ⁿ‖ (normality),
    so r(a)² = lim‖(a*a)ⁿ‖^{1/n} = r(a*a) = ‖a*a‖ = ‖a‖². For general a,
    ‖a‖² = ‖a*a‖ = r(a*a). The spectrum is purely algebraic (invertibility),
    so the norm is determined by the algebra: uniqueness of C*-norms, and
    automatic contractivity of *-homomorphisms (σ(π(x)) ⊆ σ(x)).
    (b) N = (0 1; 0 0): N² = 0 so r = 0, while N*N = diag(0, 1) gives
    ‖N‖ = 1: the gap r < ‖·‖ is possible only for non-normal elements.
    J = (1 1; 0 1): eigenvalues 1, 1 so r = 1; J*J = (1 1; 1 2) has
    eigenvalues (3 ± √5)/2, so ‖J‖ = √((3 + √5)/2) = (1 + √5)/2 = φ
    (indeed φ² = φ + 1 = (3 + √5)/2).
    B = (1 2; 3 4): B*B = (10 14; 14 20), trace 30, determinant 200 − 196
    = 4, eigenvalues 15 ± √221, ‖B‖ = √(15 + √221) ≈ 5.465. The
    eigenvalues of B are (5 ± √33)/2, so r(B) ≈ 5.372 < ‖B‖.
    (2 1; 1 2) is self-adjoint with eigenvalues 3, 1: norm = r = 3.
    SHIFT: ‖S‖ = 1 so σ(S) ⊆ D̄. For |λ| < 1, x_λ = (1, λ, λ², …) ∈ ℓ²
    satisfies S*x_λ = λx_λ, so every |λ| < 1 is an eigenvalue of S*;
    hence the open disc lies in σ(S*) = conj σ(S), and closing up,
    σ(S) = D̄. S has no eigenvalues (S x = λx forces x = 0). S*S = 1,
    so σ = {1}; SS* = 1 − e₀e₀* is a projection ≠ 0, 1: σ = {0, 1}.
    Common mistakes: using ‖a‖ = max|eigenvalue| for non-normal matrices;
    believing S is unitary because it is isometric (S*S = 1 but SS* ≠ 1).
    Now try: ‖(0 2; 0 0)‖ and r? (2 and 0.) σ(S + S*)? ([−2, 2].)

D3. (a) Describe the GNS construction for a state φ on A, and prove that
    if φ is pure then π_φ is irreducible (you may quote the converse).
    (b) For the states φ_ρ(a) = tr(ρa) on M₂ with ρ = diag(1, 0),
    ρ = ½·1, ρ = (½ ½; ½ ½), ρ = (¾ ¼; ¼ ¼), find N_φ, dim H_φ and
    whether φ is pure. (c) Is the transpose t : M₂ → M₂ completely
    positive? Is Φ(a) = tr(a)·1 − a?
    Answer: (b) dims 2, 4, 2, 4; pure, not, pure, not.
    (c) Neither is CP; both are positive (Choi eigenvalues 1, 1, 1, −1).
    Teacher explains: (a) ⟨a, b⟩ = φ(b*a) is a positive semidefinite form;
    by Cauchy-Schwarz N_φ = {a : φ(a*a) = 0} is a left ideal, so A/N_φ is
    a pre-Hilbert space and π(a)[b] = [ab] is well defined and bounded
    (φ(b*a*ab) ≤ ‖a‖²φ(b*b) since ‖a‖² − a*a ≥ 0). Complete to H_φ, put
    ξ = [1]: ⟨π(a)ξ, ξ⟩ = φ(a), and π(A)ξ is dense. If π is reducible,
    take a projection P ∈ π(A)′, P ≠ 0, 1; then φ₁(a) = ⟨π(a)Pξ, Pξ⟩ and
    φ₂ built from (1 − P)ξ are positive with φ = φ₁ + φ₂; Pξ ≠ 0 and
    (1 − P)ξ ≠ 0 by cyclicity, and φ₁/‖φ₁‖ ≠ φ (else P would be a
    scalar), so φ is a proper convex combination: not pure.
    (b) φ(a*a) = tr(ρa*a) = ‖a ρ^{1/2}‖²_HS, so N_φ = {a : aρ = 0} and
    dim H_φ = dim M₂ρ = 2·rank ρ.
      ρ = diag(1, 0): N_φ = {a : a e₁ = 0} = matrices with first column 0;
      H_φ ≅ C² (a ↦ a e₁), π = identity representation: irreducible, pure.
      ρ = ½·1: N_φ = 0, H_φ = M₂ with ⟨a, b⟩ = ½tr(b*a) (dim 4); π(a) is
      left multiplication = 2 copies of C²: reducible, not pure.
      ρ = (½ ½; ½ ½) = projection onto (1, 1)/√2, rank 1: vector state,
      dim 2, pure.
      ρ = (¾ ¼; ¼ ¼): trace 1, determinant 3/16 − 1/16 = 1/8 > 0, so
      eigenvalues (2 ± √2)/4 > 0, rank 2: faithful, dim 4, not pure.
    (c) Choi matrix of t: C = ∑eᵢⱼ ⊗ eⱼᵢ = the SWAP operator on C² ⊗ C²,
    eigenvalues 1 (symmetric, multiplicity 3) and −1 (antisymmetric
    vector e₁⊗e₂ − e₂⊗e₁). Not ≥ 0 ⇒ t not CP, though t maps positives
    to positives. For Φ: C = 1 ⊗ 1 − ∑eᵢⱼ ⊗ eᵢⱼ = 1 − ΩΩ*, Ω = e₁⊗e₁ +
    e₂⊗e₂, ‖Ω‖² = 2: eigenvalues 1, 1, 1, 1 − 2 = −1. Not CP; positive
    because for a ≥ 0 with eigenvalues λ₁, λ₂, Φ(a) has eigenvalues λ₂, λ₁.
    Common mistakes: forgetting to quotient by N_φ (dimension counts come
    out as 4 for every state); confusing "positive" with "CP".
    Now try: ρ = diag(½, ½, 0) on M₃: dim H_φ? (3·2 = 6.)

D4. (a) State Atkinson's theorem and prove that the Fredholm index is
    invariant under compact perturbations (using that it is locally
    constant). (b) For the Toeplitz operators on H²(T), compute ind T_f
    and decide invertibility for f = z², z̄, z − ½, z − 2, (z − ½)(z − 3),
    z³ + ½z. (c) Explain the exact sequence 0 → K → 𝒯 → C(T) → 0 and
    why ∂[z] = −1 in K₀(K) = Z.
    Answer: (b) −2, +1, −1, 0 (invertible), −1, −3; only T_{z−2} is
    invertible. (c) see below.
    Teacher explains: (a) ATKINSON: T is Fredholm ⇔ π(T) is invertible
    in Q = B(H)/K(H). The Fredholm operators form an open set and ind is
    locally constant on it (proof: near T, write T + E in block form
    relative to ker T ⊕ (ker T)^⊥ → (ran T)^⊥ ⊕ ran T and reduce to a
    finite-dimensional matrix, whose index is dim(domain) − dim(target)).
    For K compact, t ↦ T + tK, t ∈ [0, 1], stays Fredholm (π(T + tK) =
    π(T) is invertible), so ind(T + K) = ind T.
    (b) THEOREM: f ∈ C(T) nowhere zero ⇒ T_f Fredholm, ind T_f = −wind(f).
    Proof idea: T_fT_g − T_{fg} is compact, so f ↦ ind T_f is a
    homomorphism from invertible functions, homotopy invariant, and
    T_z = S has index 0 − 1 = −1.
      f = z²: T_f = S², index −2 (kernel 0, cokernel span{1, z}).
      f = z̄: T_f = S*, kernel span{1}, index +1.
      f = z − ½: winding number 1 (zero ½ inside the disc): index −1.
      f = z − 2: winding 0; in fact T_{z−2} = S − 2 is invertible since
      ‖S‖ = 1 < 2 (Neumann series).
      f = (z − ½)(z − 3): zeros inside: only ½: winding 1, index −1.
      f = z³ + ½z = z(z² + ½): zeros 0, ±i/√2, all of modulus < 1:
      winding 3, index −3.
    Index ≠ 0 ⇒ not invertible; index 0 is necessary but for Toeplitz
    operators with continuous symbol it is also sufficient (Coburn: ker
    T_f or ker T_f* is 0).
    (c) 𝒯 = C*(S) contains 1 − SS* = e₀e₀*, hence all of K (ideal
    generated by a rank-one projection in an irreducible algebra), and
    𝒯/K ≅ C(T) via S ↦ z (the image is generated by a unitary with full
    spectrum T). In the six-term sequence the INDEX MAP ∂ : K₁(C(T)) →
    K₀(K) sends [u] to ind(lift of u): the lift of z is S, index −1.
    Common mistakes: sign of the index (T_z has index −1, not +1);
    counting zeros outside the disc; claiming index 0 ⇒ invertible in
    general (a compact perturbation of 1 with kernel has index 0).
    Now try: ind(T_{z²}T_{z̄}) and ind T_{z̄²z⁵}. (−1 and −3.)

D5. (a) Define K₀ and K₁ for a unital C*-algebra and prove K₀(Mₙ) ≅ Z,
    K₀(C(T)) ≅ Z. (b) Using the Toeplitz six-term sequence compute
    K_*(𝒯). (c) Show that in O_n one has [1] = n[1] in K₀, and (quoting
    that [1] generates) conclude K₀(O_n) ≅ Z/(n − 1).
    Answer: (a) proofs below. (b) K₀(𝒯) = Z (generated by [1]), K₁(𝒯)
    = 0. (c) K₀(O₂) = 0, K₀(O₃) = Z/2, K₀(O_n) = Z/(n − 1).
    Teacher explains: (a) K₀(A): classes of projections p ∈ Mₖ(A) up to
    MURRAY-VON NEUMANN equivalence p ~ q (p = v*v, q = vv*), with [p] +
    [q] = [p ⊕ q]; K₀ is the Grothendieck group of this monoid. K₁(A):
    unitaries in ⋃Uₖ(A) modulo homotopy after stabilizing u ~ u ⊕ 1.
    In Mₙ (and M_k(Mₙ) = M_{kn}), projections are equivalent iff they
    have equal rank, so the monoid is N and K₀ = Z via rank.
    In Mₖ(C(T)) a projection is a continuous family p(z) of projections;
    rank p(z) is continuous, so constant (T connected), and since every
    vector bundle over T is trivial (a map T → Grassmannian is homotopic
    to a constant because the Grassmannian is simply connected), p ~
    diag(1, …, 1, 0, …, 0). So K₀(C(T)) = Z by rank; K₁(C(T)) = Z via the
    winding number of det u(z).
    (b) Six-term sequence of 0 → K → 𝒯 → C(T) → 0:
       K₀(K) = Z → K₀(𝒯) → K₀(C(T)) = Z
         ↑∂                     ↓exp
       K₁(C(T)) = Z ← K₁(𝒯) ← K₁(K) = 0.
    ∂[z] = −1 is an isomorphism Z → Z, so K₁(𝒯) → K₁(C(T)) has image
    ker ∂ = 0 and K₁(𝒯) = 0 (it is squeezed between K₁(K) = 0 and ker ∂).
    exp : K₀(C(T)) → K₁(K) = 0. The map K₀(K) → K₀(𝒯) has kernel im ∂
    = Z, so it is 0, and K₀(𝒯) ≅ K₀(C(T)) = Z generated by [1].
    (c) The sᵢ are isometries: sᵢ*sᵢ = 1, so each sᵢsᵢ* ~ 1. The sᵢsᵢ*
    are mutually orthogonal projections summing to 1, so in K₀:
    [1] = ∑[sᵢsᵢ*] = n[1], i.e. (n − 1)[1] = 0. Cuntz proved K₀(O_n) is
    generated by [1] with exactly this relation, giving Z/(n − 1); K₁ =
    0. For O₂: [1] = 0 although 1 ≠ 0 (O_n is PROPERLY INFINITE).
    Common mistakes: treating K₀ as generated by projections of A itself
    (one needs matrices: in C(S²) the Bott projection lives in M₂);
    getting the arrows of the six-term sequence wrong.
    Now try: K_* of the Calkin algebra from 0 → K → B(H) → Q → 0 with
    K_*(B(H)) = 0. (K₀(Q) = 0, K₁(Q) ≅ Z via the Fredholm index.)

D6. (a) Explain how K₀ of an AF algebra is computed from its Bratteli
    diagram and state Elliott's classification. (b) Compute (K₀, [1])
    for: (i) the CAR algebra M_{2^∞} = lim M_{2ⁿ}; (ii) M_{2^∞} ⊗ M_{3^∞};
    (iii) C(Δ), Δ the Cantor set, as lim C^{2ⁿ}; (iv) M₃ ⊗ M_{2^∞}.
    Decide whether M_{2^∞} ≅ M₃ ⊗ M_{2^∞} and M_{2^∞} ⊗ M_{3^∞} ≅ M_{6^∞}.
    (c) List all unital embeddings C ⊕ M₂ → M₅ up to unitary equivalence.
    Answer: (i) (Z[1/2], 1); (ii) (Z[1/6], 1); (iii) (C(Δ, Z), 1);
    (iv) (Z[1/2], 3). First pair: not isomorphic (stably isomorphic);
    second pair: isomorphic. (c) multiplicities (m₁, m₂) = (3, 1) and
    (1, 2) ((5, 0) is unital but not injective).
    Teacher explains: (a) A = lim Aₖ, Aₖ = ⊕ᵢ M_{n_{k,i}}. A unital
    *-homomorphism Aₖ → Aₖ₊₁ is determined up to unitary equivalence by
    the MULTIPLICITY MATRIX Mₖ (how many times block i appears in block
    j), and n_{k+1,j} = ∑ᵢ (Mₖ)ⱼᵢ n_{k,i}. K₀(Aₖ) = Z^{rₖ} with positive
    cone N^{rₖ}, and K₀ is continuous: K₀(A) = lim(Z^{rₖ}, Mₖ) as an ordered
    group, with [1] the image of (n_{k,i})ᵢ. ELLIOTT: A ≅ B ⇔ (K₀(A),
    K₀(A)⁺, [1]) ≅ (K₀(B), K₀(B)⁺, [1]).
    (b)(i) Z →×2 Z →×2 …: the limit is Z[1/2] ⊂ Q, positive cone the
    non-negative dyadic rationals, [1] = 1 (normalize the rank at level
    n by 2⁻ⁿ; this is the unique trace).
    (ii) M_{2ᵃ} ⊗ M_{3ᵇ} = M_{2ᵃ3ᵇ}: the limit group is Z[1/2, 1/3] =
    Z[1/6], [1] = 1. Same for M_{6^∞}, so they are ISOMORPHIC (Glimm:
    both have supernatural number 2^∞3^∞).
    (iii) C^{2ⁿ} → C^{2ⁿ⁺¹}, each point splitting into two: K₀ = lim
    Z^{2ⁿ} = locally constant functions Δ → Z (a countable free abelian
    group), [1] = the constant function 1.
    (iv) K₀ = Z[1/2] again, but in units where a minimal projection of
    M₃ ⊗ M_{2ⁿ} has class 2⁻ⁿ, [1] = 3. An isomorphism would give an
    order automorphism of Z[1/2] mapping 3 to 1; order automorphisms are
    multiplication by 2ᵏ (k ∈ Z), and 3·2ᵏ = 1 is impossible.
    So NOT isomorphic, though K ⊗ both agree (stably isomorphic).
    (c) Unital means m₁·1 + m₂·2 = 5 with m₁, m₂ ≥ 0; injective means
    m₁, m₂ ≥ 1 ((5, 0) kills the M₂ summand: unital, NOT an embedding).
    Injective unital ones: (3, 1) and (1, 2) — two up to unitary
    equivalence.
    Common mistakes: thinking all UHF algebras are isomorphic (the
    supernatural number matters); forgetting the order unit [1].
    Now try: does M_{2^∞} contain a projection of trace 3/8? of 1/3?
    (Yes; no.)

D7. (a) Prove von Neumann's BICOMMUTANT THEOREM: for a unital
    *-subalgebra A ⊆ B(H), the strong closure of A equals A″. (b) Compute
    A′ and A″ (with dimensions) for: (i) A = {diag(λ, λ, μ)} ⊆ M₃;
    (ii) A = M₂ ⊗ 1₃ ⊆ M₆; (iii) the algebra generated by the cyclic
    permutation matrix P ∈ M₃. (c) Show that L^∞[0, 1] acting on
    L²[0, 1] by multiplication is maximal abelian, and that the von
    Neumann algebra generated by the unilateral shift S is B(ℓ²).
    Answer: (b)(i) A′ = M₂ ⊕ C (dim 5), A″ = A (dim 2); (ii) A′ = 1 ⊗ M₃
    (dim 9), A″ = A (dim 4); (iii) A = A′ = A″ = circulant matrices
    (dim 3). (c) proofs below.
    Teacher explains: (a) A ⊆ A″ and A″ is SOT-closed (commutants always
    are), so the closure is inside A″. Conversely let T ∈ A″, ξ ∈ H, and
    let P be the projection onto the closed subspace K = cl(Aξ). K is
    A-invariant and A is *-closed, so P ∈ A′; hence TP = PT and Tξ =
    TPξ = PTξ ∈ K: T ξ is approximated by some aξ. For finitely many
    vectors ξ₁, …, ξₙ apply this to T ⊕ … ⊕ T ∈ (A ⊗ 1ₙ)″ on Hⁿ (its
    commutant is Mₙ(A′)) and the vector (ξ₁, …, ξₙ): so every SOT-
    neighbourhood of T meets A. (Unit needed: 1 ∈ A gives ξ ∈ K.)
    (b)(i) X commutes with diag(λ, λ, μ) for all λ, μ ⇔ X commutes with
    the projection e = diag(1, 1, 0) ⇔ X is block-diagonal: A′ = M₂ ⊕
    M₁, dim 4 + 1 = 5. Then A″ = (M₂ ⊕ C)′ = C1₂ ⊕ C = A, dim 2.
    (FINITE-DIMENSIONAL RULE: A = ⊕ M_{nᵢ} ⊗ 1_{mᵢ} ⇒ A′ = ⊕ 1_{nᵢ} ⊗
    M_{mᵢ}; here (n, m) = (1, 2), (1, 1).)
    (ii) (n, m) = (2, 3): A′ = 1₂ ⊗ M₃, dim 9; A″ = M₂ ⊗ 1₃, dim 4.
    (iii) P has eigenvalues 1, ω, ω² (ω = e^{2πi/3}), all distinct, so
    X commutes with P iff X is diagonal in P's eigenbasis iff X is a
    polynomial in P: X = a + bP + cP², a circulant. A′ = A, dim 3: a
    MASA (maximal abelian self-adjoint subalgebra) of M₃.
    (c) Let T commute with every M_f and put g = T1 ∈ L². For f ∈ L^∞,
    Tf = TM_f1 = M_fT1 = fg, so ‖fg‖₂ ≤ ‖T‖‖f‖₂. If E = {|g| > ‖T‖ + ε}
    had positive measure, f = 𝟙_E would give ‖fg‖₂ > (‖T‖ + ε)‖f‖₂, a
    contradiction; so g ∈ L^∞ and T = M_g on the dense set L^∞, hence
    everywhere. Thus L^∞′ = L^∞.
    For S: W*(S) ∋ 1 − SS* = e₀e₀*, and S^m(e₀e₀*)S*ⁿ = e_m e_n* are all
    the matrix units; their span is WOT-dense in B(ℓ²). Equivalently
    {S, S*}′ = C1: T commuting with every e_m e_n* is scalar.
    Common mistakes: assuming A″ ⊋ A in finite dimensions (a finite-
    dimensional unital *-algebra is always its own bicommutant); forgetting
    that the theorem needs a *-algebra: the Jordan block J = (0 1; 0 0)
    has {J}′ = {a + bJ}, which is not self-adjoint.
    Now try: A = M₂ ⊗ 1₂ ⊕ C ⊗ 1₃ on C⁴ ⊕ C³ = C⁷: dim A′? (4 + 9 = 13.)

D8. (a) Prove that the group von Neumann algebra L(Γ) is a factor iff Γ
    is ICC, and that then τ(x) = ⟨xδₑ, δₑ⟩ is a faithful normal tracial
    state, so L(Γ) is of type II₁ (Γ infinite). (b) Decide for Γ = Z, S₃,
    F₂, S_∞ (finitary permutations of N), Z × F₂, the Heisenberg group
    H₃(Z), SL(2, Z), PSL(2, Z); for S₃ identify L(S₃). (c) Which numbers
    occur as traces of projections in a factor of type I₃, II₁, II_∞, III?
    Answer: (b) factors: F₂, S_∞, PSL(2, Z) (all II₁; L(S_∞) ≅ R, L(F₂)
    not hyperfinite). Not factors: Z (L(Z) ≅ L^∞(T)), S₃ (L(S₃) ≅ C ⊕ C ⊕
    M₂), Z × F₂, H₃(Z), SL(2, Z). (c) {0, 1/3, 2/3, 1}; [0, 1]; [0, ∞];
    {0, ∞} (dimension function; III has no trace).
    Teacher explains: (a) For x ∈ L(Γ) write xδₑ = ∑ c_g δ_g ∈ ℓ²(Γ); x is
    determined by c since x commutes with the right regular
    representation (xδ_h = xρ_{h⁻¹}δₑ = ρ_{h⁻¹}xδₑ). x is central iff
    λ_h x λ_h* = x for all h iff c(hgh⁻¹) = c(g): c is constant on
    conjugacy classes. As ∑|c_g|² < ∞, c vanishes on infinite classes.
    If Γ is ICC, c = c_e δ_e and x = c_e·1: FACTOR. If some class C ≠
    {e} is finite, z = ∑_{g∈C} λ_g is central and not scalar.
    τ(xy) = ⟨xyδₑ, δₑ⟩ = ∑ c_g(x)c_{g⁻¹}(y) is symmetric: τ is a trace;
    τ(x*x) = ‖xδₑ‖² so it is faithful. A factor with a faithful finite
    trace that is infinite-dimensional is II₁.
    (b) Z: abelian; L(Z) ≅ L^∞(T) by Fourier transform (λ₁ ↦ z).
    S₃ has 3 conjugacy classes (e, transpositions, 3-cycles) and
    irreducible degrees 1, 1, 2 (1² + 1² + 2² = 6): L(S₃) = C[S₃] ≅ C ⊕ C
    ⊕ M₂, centre of dim 3.
    F₂ = ⟨a, b⟩: if g ≠ e is not a power of a, the conjugates aⁿga⁻ⁿ are
    distinct (a^k commuting with g would force g, a in a common cyclic
    subgroup, impossible as a is primitive); if g = a^k, use bⁿgb⁻ⁿ. ICC.
    S_∞: σ ≠ e moves some i to j ≠ i; conjugating by the transposition
    (j N) gives a permutation sending i to N, distinct for all large N.
    ICC, amenable (union of finite groups) ⇒ L(S_∞) ≅ R.
    Z × F₂: (1, e) is central (class of size 1). H₃(Z): its centre ≅ Z.
    SL(2, Z): −I is central. PSL(2, Z) ≅ Z/2 ∗ Z/3 is ICC (free product
    of non-trivial groups, not Z/2 ∗ Z/2).
    (c) Normalize the dimension function: I₃: τ(p) = rank(p)/3; II₁:
    every t ∈ [0, 1]; II_∞: [0, ∞]; III: every non-zero projection is
    equivalent to 1, so only "0 or ∞".
    Common mistakes: "infinite group ⇒ factor" (Z is a counterexample);
    confusing L(Γ) with C*_r(Γ) (C*_r(Z) = C(T) but L(Z) = L^∞(T)).
    Now try: is the lamplighter group Z/2 ≀ Z ICC, and what is its group
    von Neumann algebra? (Yes; amenable, so R.)

D9. (a) Define the Jones index [M : N] of II₁ factors via the coupling
    constant dim_N L²(M), state Jones' theorem, and explain the basic
    construction M ⊆ M₁ = ⟨M, e_N⟩ with [M₁ : M] = [M : N]. (b) Compute
    the index of: N ⊗ 1 ⊆ N ⊗ M₂; R^G ⊆ R for an outer action of G =
    Z/3; L(Λ) ⊆ L(F₂) for Λ the kernel of F₂ → Z/2 (a ↦ 1, b ↦ 0); the
    composite R^G ⊆ R ⊆ R ⋊ G. (c) Which of 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, π, 3.5, 3.9,
    4, 4.5 can be indices of subfactors?
    Answer: (b) 4, 3, 2, 9. (c) 2, 3, 4, 4.5 only.
    Teacher explains: (a) For a II₁ factor N and a left N-module H
    (normal representation), dim_N H ∈ [0, ∞] is normalized by dim_N
    L²(N) = 1, dim_N L²(N)p = τ(p), additive. [M : N] = dim_N L²(M).
    JONES (1983): [M : N] ∈ {4cos²(π/n) : n = 3, 4, 5, …} ∪ [4, ∞], and
    every value occurs (for N ⊆ R). BASIC CONSTRUCTION: e_N = orthogonal
    projection of L²(M) onto L²(N) (it implements the conditional
    expectation E_N: e_N x e_N = E_N(x)e_N); M₁ = (M ∪ {e_N})″ =
    JN′J is a II₁ factor when the index is finite, τ(e_N) = [M : N]⁻¹,
    [M₁ : M] = [M : N]. Iterating gives the Jones tower and the
    Temperley-Lieb relations eᵢeᵢ±₁eᵢ = [M : N]⁻¹eᵢ, whose positivity
    forces the discrete series 4cos²(π/n).
    (b) L²(N ⊗ M₂) = L²(N) ⊗ M₂ ≅ 4 copies of L²(N) as N-modules:
    index 2² = 4 (generally Mₖ gives k²). For finite G acting outerly,
    [R : R^G] = |G| = 3 and [R ⋊ G : R] = |G| = 3. For groups,
    L²(L(Γ)) = ℓ²(Γ) = ⊕_{cosets} ℓ²(Λg), each a copy of ℓ²(Λ): index
    [Γ : Λ] = 2 (Λ is the kernel of a surjection to Z/2). Index is
    MULTIPLICATIVE: [R ⋊ G : R^G] = 3·3 = 9.
    (c) Values: 4cos²(π/n) = 2 + 2cos(2π/n): n = 3: 1, n = 4: 2, n = 5:
    (3 + √5)/2 ≈ 2.618, n = 6: 3, n = 7: ≈ 3.247, n = 8: 2 + √2 ≈ 3.414,
    n = 9: ≈ 3.532, n = 10: (5 + √5)/2 ≈ 3.618, …, n = 20: ≈ 3.902,
    increasing to 4. So 1.5 (between 1 and 2), 2.5 (between 2 and
    2.618), π (between 3 and 3.247), 3.5 (between 3.414 and 3.532) and
    3.9 (between n = 19: ≈ 3.8916 and n = 20: ≈ 3.9021) are NOT indices;
    2, 3, 4, 4.5 are.
    Common mistakes: thinking the index is |G| for R^G ⊆ R ⋊ G (it is
    |G|²); treating [M : N] as a dimension of vector spaces (it need not
    be an integer: the golden ratio squared occurs).
    Now try: index of N ⊆ M₃(N)? of R^{S₃} ⊆ R? (9; 6.)

D10. (a) For M = Mₙ acting on H = Mₙ (Hilbert-Schmidt) by left
    multiplication and the faithful state φ = tr(ρ ·), ξ = ρ^{1/2}, find
    S, J, Δ and the modular group σ_t, and verify the KMS condition
    φ(x σ_{−i}(y)) = φ(yx). (b) For ρ = diag(1/(1 + λ), λ/(1 + λ)), 0 <
    λ < 1, compute σ_t(e₁₂), Sp Δ and the period of σ. (c) For ρ =
    diag(½, ⅓, ⅙) list Sp Δ. (d) State the type of the Powers factor
    R_λ = ⊗(M₂, φ_λ)″ and its Connes invariants S and T.
    Answer: (a) Δη = ρηρ⁻¹, Jη = η*, σ_t(x) = ρ^{it}xρ^{−it}.
    (b) σ_t(e₁₂) = λ^{−it}e₁₂; Sp Δ = {1, λ, λ⁻¹}; period 2π/|log λ|.
    (c) {1, 2, ½, 3, ⅓, 3/2, 2/3}. (d) III_λ, S = {0} ∪ λ^Z, T =
    (2π/log λ)Z.
    Teacher explains: (a) S(xξ) = x*ξ, i.e. S(xρ^{1/2}) = x*ρ^{1/2}. Try
    Δη = ρηρ⁻¹ (positive, since Δ = L_ρR_{ρ⁻¹} is a product of commuting
    positive operators) and Jη = η* (antiunitary). Check:
    Δ^{1/2}(xρ^{1/2}) = ρ^{1/2}xρ^{1/2}ρ^{−1/2} = ρ^{1/2}x, and
    J(ρ^{1/2}x) = x*ρ^{1/2} = S(xρ^{1/2}). ✓ Then
    Δ^{it}L_xΔ^{−it}η = ρ^{it}xρ^{−it}ηρ^{it}ρ^{−it} = L_{σ_t(x)}η with
    σ_t(x) = ρ^{it}xρ^{−it}, and JL_xJη = ηx* = right multiplication:
    JMJ = M′ ✓. KMS: σ_{−i}(y) = ρyρ⁻¹, so φ(xσ_{−i}(y)) = tr(ρxρyρ⁻¹)
    = tr(xρy) = tr(ρyx) = φ(yx) ✓. In physics notation ρ = e^{−βH}/Z
    and φ is the β-KMS (Gibbs) state for the dynamics e^{itH}xe^{−itH}.
    (b) σ_t(eᵢⱼ) = ρᵢ^{it}ρⱼ^{−it}eᵢⱼ = (ρᵢ/ρⱼ)^{it}eᵢⱼ. Here ρ₁/ρ₂ =
    1/λ: σ_t(e₁₂) = λ^{−it}e₁₂, σ_t(e₂₁) = λ^{it}e₂₁, diagonal fixed.
    Eigenvalues of Δ on the eᵢⱼ: ρᵢ/ρⱼ ∈ {1, 1/λ, λ}. λ^{−it} = 1 ⇔
    t log λ ∈ 2πZ: period 2π/|log λ|. (Gibbs: H = diag(0, E), λ =
    e^{−βE}.)
    (c) Ratios of (½, ⅓, ⅙): ½/⅓ = 3/2, ½/⅙ = 3, ⅓/⅙ = 2, their
    inverses, and 1: seven values.
    (d) POWERS: R_λ is a III_λ factor. For product states Sp Δ contains
    λ^Z, and Connes' invariant S(M) = ∩ Sp Δ_φ = {0} ∪ λ^Z; T(M) = {t :
    σ_t inner} = (2π/log λ)Z. Different λ give non-isomorphic factors —
    Powers' uncountable family. (Traces: Δ = 1, σ_t = id; type II₁ has
    S = {1}.)
    Common mistakes: writing σ_t(x) = ρ^{−it}xρ^{it} (sign conventions:
    σ_t = Ad Δ^{it} with Δ = ρ ⊗ ρ⁻¹); forgetting that Sp Δ includes 1.
    Now try: ρ = diag(⅓, ⅔): Sp Δ? ({1, ½, 2}.)

D11. (a) For the spectral triple (C^∞(S¹), L²(S¹), D = −i d/dθ) show
    that ‖[D, f]‖ = ‖f′‖_∞ and that Connes' formula d(p, q) = sup{|f(p) −
    f(q)| : ‖[D, f]‖ ≤ 1} gives the arc-length distance. (b) Compute:
    (i) d(0, π/2), d(0, 3π/2), d(0, π); (ii) the distance for the
    two-point space A = C², H = C², D = (0 m; m 0), m ≠ 0; (iii) the
    Dixmier trace Tr_ω(|D|^{−1}) on S¹ (on (ker D)^⊥) and Tr_ω(M_f|D|^{−1})
    for f = cos²θ; (iv) the metric dimension of S² from its Dirac
    spectrum ±(k + 1), multiplicity 2(k + 1), k ≥ 0, and Tr_ω(D⁻²).
    Answer: (i) π/2, π/2, π. (ii) 1/|m|. (iii) 2 and 1. (iv) dimension 2;
    Tr_ω(D⁻²) = 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) [D, M_f]g = −i(fg)′ + if g′ = −if′g, so [D, f]
    = M_{−if′} and ‖[D, f]‖ = ‖f′‖_∞. For ‖f′‖_∞ ≤ 1 and the shorter arc
    γ from p to q, |f(p) − f(q)| = |∫_γ f′| ≤ length(γ) = d_geo(p, q).
    Conversely g(x) = d_geo(p, x) is 1-Lipschitz; smoothing it slightly
    (convolution on the circle keeps the Lipschitz constant) gives smooth
    f with ‖f′‖ ≤ 1 and f(q) − f(p) arbitrarily close to d_geo(p, q) (for
    real f; complex f do no better). So d = d_geo: the metric is encoded
    in the pair (A, D) without mentioning points.
    (b)(i) On the unit circle the geodesic distance is min(|Δθ|, 2π −
    |Δθ|): π/2, min(3π/2, π/2) = π/2, and π.
    (ii) Points = the two characters of C². For f = (a, b):
    [D, f] = (0 m(b − a); m(a − b) 0), norm |m||a − b|. The constraint
    |a − b| ≤ 1/|m| gives d = 1/|m| (e.g. m = 2: distance ½).
    (iii) |D|⁻¹ on (ker D)^⊥ = span{e^{inθ} : n ≠ 0} has singular values
    1, 1, ½, ½, ⅓, ⅓, …; the sum of the first 2K is 2(1 + ½ + … + 1/K) ~
    2 log K ~ 2 log(2K). Dividing by log N: Tr_ω(|D|⁻¹) = 2. For M_f,
    only the diagonal matters: ⟨M_f eₙ, eₙ⟩ = (1/2π)∫f, so Tr_ω(M_f|D|⁻¹)
    = 2·(1/2π)∫f dθ = (1/π)∫f dθ. For f = cos²θ: ∫₀^{2π}cos² = π, so the
    answer is 1. (Connes' trace theorem in the form Tr_ω(f|D|⁻¹) ∝ ∫f.)
    (iv) N(Λ) = #{eigenvalues of |D| ≤ Λ} = 2∑_{k=0}^{Λ−1} 2(k + 1) =
    2Λ(Λ + 1) ~ 2Λ² for integer Λ: WEYL growth Λ², dimension 2 (|D|^{−p}
    is in L^{1,∞} exactly for p ≥ 2). D⁻² has eigenvalues 1/(k + 1)² with
    multiplicity 4(k + 1): sum over k < K is 4(1 + ½ + … + 1/K) ~ 4 log K,
    while N ~ 2K², log N ~ 2 log K. Ratio → 2. Check with the general
    formula rank(S)·vol(S^{n−1})·vol(M)/(n(2π)ⁿ) = 2·2π·4π/(2·4π²) = 2. ✓
    Common mistakes: forgetting the multiplicity 2 of each |n| on S¹;
    using log of the eigenvalue index K instead of the number N of
    singular values (they differ by a constant factor in dimension ≥ 2);
    thinking Tr_ω sees trace-class operators (it vanishes on them).
    Now try: circle of radius r (D = −ir⁻¹d/dθ): d between antipodal
    points and Tr_ω(|D|⁻¹)? (πr and 2r = length/π.)

D12. (a) Present the noncommutative torus A_θ = C*(u, v : vu =
    e^{2πiθ}uv) as C(T) ⋊_θ Z, show that for θ ∉ Q the only tracial state
    is τ(∑a_{mn}uᵐvⁿ) = a₀₀, and compute K_*(A_θ) with Pimsner-
    Voiculescu. (b) Let θ = √2 − 1. Which of ½, 2 − √2, 2θ, θ², 3θ are
    traces of projections in A_θ? (c) For θ = √3 − 1 decide whether A_θ
    is isomorphic, resp. Morita equivalent, to A_{θ′} with θ′ = 1/θ.
    (d) Show φ(f⁰, f¹) = (1/2πi)∫_{S¹} f⁰df¹ is a cyclic 1-cocycle on
    C^∞(S¹) and compute ⟨φ, [e^{ikθ}]⟩.
    Answer: (a) K₀ = K₁ = Z². (b) 2 − √2, 2θ, θ² yes; ½, 3θ no.
    (c) Morita equivalent, not isomorphic. (d) k.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let u = z ∈ C(T) and let v implement rotation
    by 2πθ: vfv* = f(e^{2πiθ}·) (so vuv* = e^{2πiθ}u): then vu =
    e^{2πiθ}uv and A_θ = C(T) ⋊ Z. A trace satisfies τ(uᵐvⁿ) = τ(v(uᵐvⁿ)
    v*) = e^{2πimθ}τ(uᵐvⁿ) and similarly with u: conjugating by u gives
    τ(uᵐvⁿ) = e^{−2πinθ}τ(uᵐvⁿ). If (m, n) ≠ (0, 0) and θ ∉ Q, one of the
    factors ≠ 1, so τ(uᵐvⁿ) = 0: τ is unique.
    PV exact sequence for B ⋊_α Z with B = C(T): K₀(B) →(1 − α_*)→ K₀(B)
    → K₀(B ⋊ Z) → K₁(B) →(1 − α_*)→ K₁(B) → K₁(B ⋊ Z) → K₀(B). A rotation
    is homotopic to the identity, so α_* = id and 1 − α_* = 0: the
    sequence splits into 0 → Z → K₀(A_θ) → Z → 0 and the same for K₁;
    so K₀ = K₁ = Z² (free, so the extensions split).
    (b) τ(K₀(A_θ)) = Z + θZ and (RIEFFEL) every element of (Z + θZ) ∩
    [0, 1] is τ of a projection. θ = √2 − 1 ≈ 0.414:
      ½ ∉ Z + θZ (θ irrational): no;
      2 − √2 = 1 − θ ≈ 0.586: yes;
      2θ ≈ 0.828: yes;
      θ² = 3 − 2√2 = 1 − 2θ ≈ 0.172: yes (a hidden element of Z + θZ);
      3θ ≈ 1.243 > 1: no (τ(p) ≤ τ(1) = 1).
    (c) θ ≈ 0.732, 1/θ = (√3 + 1)/2 ≈ 1.366 ≡ (√3 − 1)/2 ≈ 0.366 mod Z.
    Isomorphism needs θ′ ≡ ±θ mod Z, i.e. θ′ mod 1 ∈ {0.732, 0.268}: no.
    MORITA: θ ↦ 1/θ is the GL₂(Z) matrix (0 1; 1 0): yes. (K₀ ordered
    groups Z + θZ and Z + θ′Z agree up to scaling: θ(Z + θ′Z) = Z + θZ.)
    (d) Cyclicity: φ(f¹, f⁰) = (1/2πi)∫f¹df⁰ = −(1/2πi)∫f⁰df¹ (integrate
    by parts on a closed curve). Cocycle: bφ(f⁰, f¹, f²) = φ(f⁰f¹, f²) −
    φ(f⁰, f¹f²) + φ(f²f⁰, f¹) = (1/2πi)∫(f⁰f¹df² − f⁰f¹df² − f⁰f²df¹ +
    f²f⁰df¹) = 0. Pairing with K₁: ⟨φ, [u]⟩ = φ(u⁻¹, u) = (1/2πi)∫u⁻¹du;
    for u = e^{ikθ}: (1/2πi)·ik·2π = k, the winding number. It is also
    minus the Fredholm index of T_u (D4): the index pairing of K₁(C(S¹))
    with the K-homology class of the Dirac operator −i d/dθ.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the constraint 0 ≤ τ(p) ≤ 1; treating
    isomorphism and Morita equivalence as the same; the sign of θ (A_θ ≅
    A_{−θ} by swapping u and v).
    Now try: is ⅓ the trace of a projection in A_{1/3}? (Yes: Rieffel's
    construction gives a projection of trace θ for every θ ∈ (0, 1).)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Norms and spectra: give ‖a‖ and σ(a) (and r(a) when it differs).
    a) diag(3, −5, 2)                b) (0 2; 0 0)
    c) (1 1; 0 1)                    d) (2 1; 1 2)
    e) (1 2; 3 4)                    f) (0 1; −1 0)
    g) (1 i; −i 1)                   h) (1 2; 2 4)
    i) N = 3×3 nilpotent Jordan block (1's above the diagonal); ‖N²‖
    j) unilateral shift S on ℓ²(N); eigenvalues of S and of S*
    k) M_x on L²[0, 1]              l) bilateral shift U on ℓ²(Z)
    m) S + S* on ℓ²(N)
    n) Volterra operator Vf(x) = ∫₀ˣ f on L²[0, 1]
    o) weighted shift Weₙ = eₙ₊₁/(n + 1), n ≥ 0 (also ‖Wⁿ‖)
    p) check ‖J*J‖ = ‖J‖² for J = (1 1; 0 1)
    Answers:
      a) 5; {3, −5, 2}                b) 2; {0}, r = 0
      c) (1 + √5)/2; {1}, r = 1       d) 3; {1, 3}
      e) √(15 + √221) ≈ 5.465; {(5 ± √33)/2}, r ≈ 5.372
      f) 1; {i, −i}                   g) 2; {0, 2}
      h) 5; {0, 5} (rank one)         i) 1; {0}; ‖N²‖ = 1 (N³ = 0)
      j) 1; D̄; S has none, S* has every |λ| < 1
      k) 1; [0, 1] (no eigenvalues)  l) 1; T (no eigenvalues)
      m) 2; [−2, 2]                   n) 2/π; {0}
      o) 1; {0}; ‖Wⁿ‖ = 1/n! (compact, quasinilpotent)
      p) J*J = (1 1; 1 2), norm (3 + √5)/2 = φ² ✓

M2. Identify the C*-algebra (up to isomorphism) or its Gelfand spectrum.
    a) C*(1, diag(1, 2, 2)) ⊆ M₃     b) C*(1, diag(1, i, −1, −i))
    c) C*(1, (0 1; 1 0))            d) C*((0 1; 0 0)) (non-unital)
    e) C*(a) for a = 1 ⊕ N ∈ M₃, N = (0 1; 0 0) (non-unital)
    f) C*(Z/n)       g) C*(S₃)      h) C*(D₄), |D₄| = 8
    i) C*(S₄)        j) C*(Z), C*(Z²), C*(Z × Z/2)
    k) character spaces of c₀, of c, of ℓ^∞
    l) minimal unitizations of C₀(R), C₀(R²), C₀((0, 1])
    m) in ℓ¹(Z): σ(δ₁ + δ₋₁), σ(2δ₀ + δ₁), σ(δ₀ + 2δ₁); which are
       invertible?
    n) ‖f*f‖₁ and ‖f‖₁² for f = δ₀ + δ₁ − δ₂ in ℓ¹(Z)
    o) C*(S)/K(ℓ²)
    p) C*(1, h), h = h* with σ(h) = [0, 1] ∪ {2}; does it contain a
       projection ≠ 0, 1?
    Answers:
      a) C² (σ = {1, 2})               b) C⁴
      c) C² (σ = {1, −1})              d) M₂
      e) C ⊕ M₂ (dim 5)                f) Cⁿ
      g) C ⊕ C ⊕ M₂ (1 + 1 + 4 = 6)    h) C⁴ ⊕ M₂ (4 + 4 = 8)
      i) C ⊕ C ⊕ M₂ ⊕ M₃ ⊕ M₃ (1 + 1 + 4 + 9 + 9 = 24)
      j) C(T), C(T²), C(T) ⊕ C(T)
      k) N; N ∪ {∞}; βN (Stone-Čech)
      l) C(S¹), C(S²), C([0, 1])
      m) [−2, 2] (not invertible); circle |λ − 2| = 1 (invertible);
         circle |λ − 1| = 2 ∋ 0 (not invertible)
      n) 5 and 9 (so ℓ¹(Z) is not a C*-algebra)
      o) C(T)
      p) C([0, 1] ∪ {2}); yes, χ_{2}(h) (the spectrum is disconnected)

      i) ½(1 1; 1 1)                   j) diag(2, 3), diag(2, 0), diag(0, 3)
      k) [0, 4], [e⁻¹, e²], [0, 2], [0, 2]

M4. States, GNS, complete positivity. For states on Mₙ give pure?
    and dim H_ω.
    a) ω(a) = a₁₁ on M₂              b) ω = ½tr on M₂
    c) ω(a) = (a₁₁ + 2a₂₂)/3         d) ω(a) = a₁₂ on M₂
    e) tr(ρ ·) on M₃, ρ = diag(½, ½, 0)
    f) ρ = (½ ½; ½ ½)                g) ρ = (¾ ¼; ¼ ¼)
    h) all states of C²; the pure ones
    i) f ↦ ∫₀¹ f dx on C[0, 1]: H_ω and π_ω
    j) f ↦ f(½)                      k) f ↦ ½(f(0) + f(1))
    l) the pure states of M₂ (in the Bloch ball picture)
    m) the transpose t on M₂: positive? CP? Choi eigenvalues
    n) Φ(a) = tr(a)·1 on M₂          o) Φ(a) = tr(a)·1 − a on M₂
    p) Φ(a) = V*aV                   q) Φ(a) = diagonal part of a on Mₙ
    Answers:
      a) pure, 2                       b) not pure (faithful), 4
      c) not pure (faithful), 4        d) not a state (ω(1) = 0)
      e) not pure, 6                   f) pure (rank-one ρ), 2
      g) not pure: eigenvalues (2 ± √2)/4 > 0, faithful, 4
      h) (x, y) ↦ tx + (1 − t)y, t ∈ [0, 1]; pure: t = 0, 1
      i) L²[0, 1], multiplication     j) C, f ↦ f(½); pure
      k) C², not pure
      l) the vector states = the unit sphere (Bloch sphere)
      m) positive, not CP; 1, 1, 1, −1
      n) CP (Choi matrix 1 ⊗ 1 ≥ 0)
      o) positive, not CP; Choi eigenvalues 1, 1, 1, −1
      p) CP (Stinespring form)        q) CP (conditional expectation)

M5. Fredholm indices on ℓ²(N) ≅ H²(T) (T_f Toeplitz, z = e^{iθ}).

M7. AF and UHF algebras, Bratteli diagrams.
    a) (K₀, [1]) of M_{3^∞}          b) of M_{2^∞} ⊗ M_{3^∞}
    c) M_{2^∞} ⊗ M_{2^∞} ≅ M_{2^∞}?  d) M_{6^∞} ≅ M_{2^∞}?
    e) M_{4^∞} ≅ M_{2^∞}?            f) M₂ ⊗ M_{2^∞} ≅ M_{2^∞}?
    g) M₃ ⊗ M_{2^∞} ≅ M_{2^∞}? stably isomorphic?
    h) unital embedding M₂ → M₅? M₂ → M₆ (multiplicity)?
    i) number of injective unital C ⊕ M₂ → M₅ up to unitary equivalence
    j) (K₀, [1]) of C ⊕ M₂
    k) (K₀, [1]) of C(Δ) = lim C^{2ⁿ}
    l) projections in M_{2^∞} of trace 3/8? 1/3? 5/4?
    m) projection of trace 1/3 in M_{6^∞}? of trace 1/5?
    n) A₁ = C ⊕ C, each step multiplicity matrix (1 1; 1 0): A₂, A₃,
       A₄ and K₀ of the limit as a group
    o) C ⊆ M₂ ⊆ M₆ ⊆ M₂₄ ⊆ … (multiplicities 2, 3, 4, …): which UHF?
    Answers:
      a) (Z[1/3], 1)          b) (Z[1/6], 1)
      c) yes                  d) no (Z[1/6] ≠ Z[1/2])
      e) yes (4^∞ = 2^∞)      f) yes (2·2^∞ = 2^∞)
      g) no ([1] = 3 vs 1 in Z[1/2]); yes, stably isomorphic
      h) no (2 ∤ 5); yes, multiplicity 3
      i) 2: (m₁, m₂) = (3, 1), (1, 2)
      j) (Z², (1, 2))
      k) (C(Δ, Z), 1)
      l) yes; no (1/3 ∉ Z[1/2]); no (> 1)
      m) yes; no
      n) M₂ ⊕ C, M₃ ⊕ M₂, M₅ ⊕ M₃ (Fibonacci); K₀ ≅ Z² (det = −1)
      o) supernatural number ∏p^∞ over all primes: the universal UHF
         algebra, K₀ = Q

M8. Commutants and bicommutants (give A′ and its dimension).
    a) Mₙ ⊆ B(Cⁿ)                   b) the diagonal matrices Dₙ ⊆ Mₙ
    c) {diag(λ, λ, μ)} ⊆ M₃          d) M₂ ⊗ 1₃ ⊆ M₆
    e) M₂ ⊕ M₂ acting block-diagonally on C⁴
    f) {diag(A, A, b) : A ∈ M₂, b ∈ C} on C⁵
    g) the single matrix diag(1, 2, 3)
    h) the single matrix diag(1, 1, 2)
    i) the cyclic permutation matrix P ∈ M₃
    j) the Jordan block (1 1; 0 1) (is the commutant a *-algebra?)
    k) λ(S₃) on ℓ²(S₃)               l) λ(Z/3) on ℓ²(Z/3)
    m) A = C ⊗ 1₃ ⊕ M₂ ⊗ 1₁: dim H, dim A, dim A′
    n) L^∞[0, 1] on L²[0, 1]
    o) W*(S), W*(U) (U bilateral shift on ℓ²(Z))
    p) centre of M₂ ⊕ M₃ ⊕ C
    Answers:
      a) C1, 1                 b) Dₙ, n
      c) M₂ ⊕ C, 5 (A″ = A)    d) 1₂ ⊗ M₃, 9
      e) C ⊕ C, 2              f) M₂ ⊕ C, 5
      g) D₃, 3                 h) M₂ ⊕ C, 5
      i) circulants a + bP + cP², 3
      j) {a + bN : N = (0 1; 0 0)}, 2; not a *-algebra
      k) ρ(S₃) ≅ C ⊕ C ⊕ M₂, 6
      l) λ(Z/3) itself ≅ C³, 3
      m) 5, 5, dim(M₃ ⊕ C) = 10
      n) L^∞[0, 1] itself (maximal abelian)
      o) B(ℓ²(N)) (commutant C1); L(Z) ≅ L^∞(T) (commutant itself)
      p) C³, 3

M9. Factors, types, group von Neumann algebras.
    a) B(C⁵)          b) B(ℓ²)          c) L^∞[0, 1]
    d) L(Z)           e) L(S₃)          f) L(F₂)
    g) L(S_∞)         h) L(Z/2 ≀ Z)     i) L(Z × F₂)
    j) L(H₃(Z))       k) L(SL(2, Z)), L(PSL(2, Z))

    o) Powers factor R_{1/2}
    p) trace values of projections in I₄, II₁, II_∞
    q) in a II₁ factor, τ(p) = 0.3, τ(q) = 0.7: is p ~ 1 − q? p ~ q?

       factors)

      g) hyperfinite II₁ factor R
