      j) not a factor (centre ⊇ L(Z))
      k) not a factor (−I central); II₁ factor
      l) II_∞ factor           m) II₁ factor ≅ R
      n) ≅ R                   o) III_{1/2} factor
      p) {0, ¼, ½, ¾, 1}; [0, 1]; [0, ∞]
      q) yes (0.3 = 1 − 0.7); no
      r) L(F₅), L(F₉), L(F₁₀)  s) 5 (one per irreducible representation)

M10. Jones index and modular theory.
    a) 4cos²(π/n) for n = 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12
    b) possible indices? 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, π, 3.5, 3.8, 4, 4.5, 100
    c) [N ⊗ M₃ : N ⊗ 1]             d) [R : R^{S₃}] (outer action)
    e) [R ⋊ G : R^G], G = Z/2        f) [L(F₂) : L(Λ)], [F₂ : Λ] = 5
    g) N ⊆ P ⊆ M, [M : N] = 6, [P : N] = 2: [M : P]
    h) Can N ⊆ M with [M : N] = 3 have an intermediate N ⊊ P ⊊ M? with
       index (3 + √5)/2?
    i) τ(e_N) in the basic construction when [M : N] = 3
    j) Sp Δ for φ = tr(ρ ·) on M₂, ρ = diag(⅓, ⅔)
    k) Sp Δ for ρ = diag(½, ⅓, ⅙) on M₃
    l) ρ = diag(1/(1 + λ), λ/(1 + λ)): σ_t(e₁₂), σ_t(e₂₁), σ_t(e₁₁)
    m) Gibbs state of H = diag(0, E) at inverse temperature β: λ and the
       period of σ_t
    n) Connes S(M) for I_n, II₁, R_{1/2}, III₁
    o) T(R_λ) for λ = e⁻¹ and λ = ½
    p) type of R_{1/2} ⊗ R_{1/3}
    Answers:
      a) 1, 2, (3 + √5)/2 ≈ 2.618, 3, 2 + √2 ≈ 3.414, (5 + √5)/2 ≈
         3.618, 2 + √3 ≈ 3.732
      b) 2, 3, 4, 4.5, 100 yes; 1.5, 2.5, π, 3.5, 3.8 no
      c) 9          d) 6          e) 4          f) 5          g) 3
      h) no in both cases (a proper factorization needs two allowed
         indices ≥ 2, product ≥ 4)
      i) 1/3        j) {1, ½, 2}
      k) {1, 3/2, 2/3, 3, 1/3, 2, 1/2}
      l) λ^{−it}e₁₂, λ^{it}e₂₁, e₁₁
      m) λ = e^{−βE}; 2π/(βE)
      n) {1}, {1}, {0} ∪ 2^Z, [0, ∞)
      o) 2πZ; (2π/log 2)Z
      p) III₁ (log 2/log 3 irrational)

M11. Noncommutative geometry: spectral triples, Dixmier traces, A_θ,
     cyclic theory.
    a) two-point space A = C², D = (0 m; m 0): d for m = 2, m = ⅓
    b) Sp(−i d/dθ) on L²(S¹) with multiplicities
    c) ‖[D, e^{ikθ}]‖ and ‖[D, cos θ]‖ for D = −i d/dθ
    d) Connes distances on the unit circle: d(0, π/2), d(0, 3π/2),
       d(0, π), d(π/3, 5π/3)
    e) circle of radius r, D = −ir⁻¹d/dθ: distance of antipodal points
    f) Tr_ω(M_f|D|⁻¹) on S¹ for f = 1, cos²θ, e^{iθ}, 1 + sin θ
    g) Tr_ω((1 + Δ)⁻¹) on T² = R²/(2πZ)²
    h) metric dimension of S¹, T³, S², a finite spectral triple
    i) Dirac on round S²: number of eigenvalues with |λ| ≤ 3; Tr_ω(D⁻²)
    j) θ = √2 − 1: which of ½, 1 − θ, 2θ, θ², 3θ are τ(p), p ∈ A_θ?
    k) θ = √3 − 1: compare A_θ with A_{2−√3}, A_{√3}, A_{(√3−1)/2}
       (isomorphic / Morita equivalent)
    l) τ(K₀(A_{1/3})) and τ(K₀(A_θ)) for θ irrational
    m) HH₀(Mₙ), HH₀(Cⁿ), HC_{2k}(C), HC_{2k+1}(C)
    n) HP_even, HP_odd of C^∞(S¹), C^∞(T²), C^∞(S²)
    o) ⟨φ, [e^{ikθ}]⟩ for φ(f⁰, f¹) = (1/2πi)∫f⁰df¹, k = 3, −2
    Answers:
      a) ½; 3                  b) Z, each multiplicity 1
      c) |k|; 1                d) π/2, π/2, π, 2π/3
      e) πr                    f) 2, 1, 0, 2
      g) π                     h) 1, 3, 2, 0
      i) 2(2 + 4 + 6) = 24; 2
      j) 1 − θ, 2θ, θ² = 1 − 2θ yes; ½, 3θ no
      k) A_{2−√3} ≅ A_θ (2 − √3 = 1 − θ); A_{√3} ≅ A_θ (√3 = θ + 1);
         A_{(√3−1)/2}: Morita equivalent (≡ 1/θ mod Z), not isomorphic
      l) (1/3)Z; Z + θZ
      m) C, Cⁿ, C, 0
      n) C, C; C², C²; C², 0
      o) 3, −2

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every C*-algebra is isomorphic to a norm-closed *-subalgebra of
        some B(H).
     b) In a C*-algebra ‖a‖ = r(a) for every a.
     c) Every positive map between C*-algebras is completely positive.
     d) A positive map into a commutative C*-algebra is CP.
     e) For H separable infinite-dimensional, K(H) is the only proper
        non-zero closed ideal of B(H).
     f) K₀(B(H)) ≅ Z for H infinite-dimensional.
     g) A Toeplitz operator T_f with f ∈ C(T) nowhere zero is invertible.
     h) Every C*-algebra is a von Neumann algebra.
     i) L(Γ) is a factor for every infinite group Γ.
     j) Two projections of equal trace in a II₁ factor are unitarily
        equivalent.
     k) 2.5 is the Jones index of some subfactor.
     l) Every II₁ factor is hyperfinite.
     m) R ⊗ R ≅ R.
     n) A_θ is simple for every θ.
     o) A_θ ≅ A_{1−θ}.
     p) K₀(O₃) ≅ Z/2.
     q) C*(F₂) = C*_r(F₂).
     r) Two UHF algebras with isomorphic K₀ groups (ignoring [1]) are
        isomorphic.
     s) The Dixmier trace of a trace-class operator is 0.
     t) A type III factor has a faithful normal semifinite trace.
     u) For the spectral triple (C^∞(S¹), L²(S¹), −i d/dθ) the Connes
        distance is the arc length.
     Answers:
       a) T (GNS)
       b) F: (0 1; 0 0) has norm 1 and r = 0
       c) F: the transpose on M₂       d) T
       e) T         f) F: K₀(B(H)) = 0 (Eilenberg swindle)
       g) F: T_z = S (index −1)
       h) F: C[0, 1] (no non-trivial projections, not a dual space)
       i) F: Γ = Z       j) T (p ~ q and 1 − p ~ 1 − q)
       k) F: 2 < 2.5 < 4cos²(π/5) ≈ 2.618
       l) F: L(F₂)       m) T
       n) F: θ = 0 gives C(T²)         o) T
       p) T ([1] = 3[1])
       q) F: F₂ is not amenable (the trivial representation does not
          factor through C*_r(F₂))
       r) F: M_{2^∞} and M₃ ⊗ M_{2^∞} (the unit class differs)
       s) T         t) F: e.g. R_{1/2} (type III has no such trace)
       u) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: work first entirely in finite dimensions, where every
  statement can be checked by hand: a finite-dimensional C*-algebra is
  ⊕M_{nᵢ}, its commutant on a representation is ⊕M_{mᵢ} (swap the sizes
  and multiplicities), its K₀ is Z^k counted by ranks, a state is a
  density matrix and its GNS space has dimension n·rank ρ, and the
  modular group is conjugation by ρ^{it}. Keep three "infinite"
  examples permanently on the board — the shift S (norm 1, spectrum D̄,
  index −1), the Toeplitz extension 0 → K → 𝒯 → C(T) → 0, and the
  irrational rotation algebra A_θ with its trace — and test every new
  theorem on them. For spectra and norms insist on the question "is the
  element normal?" before using r(a) = ‖a‖; for indices, count zeros
  inside the disc and put in the minus sign; for UHF questions, factor
  the supernatural number into primes and check the unit class; for
  Jones indices, list 4cos²(π/n) = 2 + 2cos(2π/n) for n ≤ 10 once and for
  all. Harder: prove the Gelfand-Naimark-Segal theorem in full and the
  Kaplansky density theorem; prove Cuntz's computation K₀(O_n) = Z/(n −
  1) via the Toeplitz-Cuntz extension; derive the Pimsner-Voiculescu
  sequence from the Toeplitz algebra of a crossed product; prove Jones'
  theorem that indices below 4 lie in {4cos²(π/n)} using the positivity
  of the Temperley-Lieb trace; show that the Powers factors R_λ are
  pairwise non-isomorphic by computing Connes' S invariant; prove the
  Connes distance formula for a compact spin manifold; compute the
  Connes-Chern character of the Dirac operator on T² and show that the
  2-cocycle on A_θ is integral on K₀ (the integer quantum Hall effect).


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.11 — NONLINEAR PDE AND GEOMETRIC ANALYSIS; FLUID DYNAMICS
(NAVIER-STOKES)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - SOBOLEV SPACES W^{k,p}(Ω): weak derivatives in Lᵖ. GAGLIARDO-NIRENBERG-
    SOBOLEV: 1 ≤ p < n ⇒ ‖u‖_{p*} ≤ C‖∇u‖_p on Rⁿ, p* = np/(n − p) (the
    ONLY exponent allowed by scaling u(λx)); 2* = 2n/(n − 2). MORREY:
    p > n ⇒ W^{1,p} ⊂ C^{0,1−n/p}. Borderline p = n: W^{1,n} ⊄ L^∞
    (log log(1/|x|) in 2D), but ⊂ BMO (Trudinger: exp(c|u|^{n/(n−1)})
    integrable). RELLICH-KONDRACHOV: Ω bounded ⇒ W₀^{1,p} ⊂ L^q COMPACT
    for q < p*; compactness FAILS at q = p* (concentrating bubbles) and on
    unbounded Ω (translations). POINCARÉ: ‖u‖₂ ≤ λ₁^{−1/2}‖∇u‖₂ on H₀¹.
  - CALCULUS OF VARIATIONS, DIRECT METHOD: J coercive and weakly lower
    semicontinuous on a reflexive space ⇒ a minimizer exists; J(u) =
    ∫F(x, u, ∇u) is weakly lsc iff (essentially) F is CONVEX in ∇u
    (quasiconvex for systems). EULER-LAGRANGE: −div F_ξ + F_u = 0.
    Dirichlet principle: ∫(½|∇u|² − fu) ↦ −Δu = f. Minimizers may fail to
    exist: Weierstrass ∫x²u′² (u(±1) = ±1, inf 0), Bolza ∫((u′² − 1)² +
    u²) (inf 0 via sawtooth, not attained).
  - SEMILINEAR ELLIPTIC −Δu = f(u) in Ω, u = 0 on ∂Ω: critical points of
    J(u) = ∫(½|∇u|² − F(u)), F′ = f. MOUNTAIN PASS (Ambrosetti-Rabinowitz):
    J ∈ C¹ with the PALAIS-SMALE condition, J(0) = 0, J ≥ α > 0 on a
    sphere ‖u‖ = ρ, J(e) ≤ 0 for some ‖e‖ > ρ ⇒ a critical value c ≥ α.
    Gives a positive solution of −Δu = |u|^{p−1}u for 1 < p < 2* − 1 =
    (n + 2)/(n − 2) (subcritical: PS holds by Rellich).
  - POHOZAEV IDENTITY: for −Δu = f(u), u = 0 on ∂Ω: n∫F(u) − ((n − 2)/2)
    ∫|∇u|² = ½∫_{∂Ω}(x·ν)(∂_νu)². Ω STAR-SHAPED and p ≥ (n + 2)/(n − 2) ⇒
    no nontrivial solution of −Δu = |u|^{p−1}u. On Rⁿ the critical
    equation −Δu = u^{(n+2)/(n−2)} has the AUBIN-TALENTI BUBBLES u =
    [n(n − 2)]^{(n−2)/4}(λ/(λ² + |x − x₀|²))^{(n−2)/2}, extremals of the
    Sobolev inequality, S_n = ¼n(n − 2)|Sⁿ|^{2/n} (S₃ = 3(π/2)^{4/3}).
    BREZIS-NIRENBERG: −Δu = u^{2*−1} + λu in a ball has a positive
    solution iff 0 < λ < λ₁ (n ≥ 4), iff λ₁/4 < λ < λ₁ (n = 3).
  - MAXIMUM PRINCIPLES: weak (Lu ≥ 0 ⇒ max on ∂Ω), strong, HOPF LEMMA
    (a positive supersolution vanishing at x₀ ∈ ∂Ω has ∂_νu(x₀) < 0),
    comparison, SUB/SUPERSOLUTIONS (u̲ ≤ ū ⇒ a solution in between),
    HARNACK. GIDAS-NI-NIRENBERG (moving planes): positive solutions of
    −Δu = f(u) in a ball, u = 0 on ∂B, are radial and decreasing.
    Examples: logistic −u″ = λu(1 − u) on (0, π) has a positive solution
    iff λ > λ₁ = 1; Bratu −u″ = λe^u on (0, 1): two solutions for 0 < λ <
    λ* ≈ 3.5138, one at λ*, none beyond.
  - ELLIPTIC REGULARITY: SCHAUDER (Lu ∈ C^α ⇒ u ∈ C^{2,α}), CALDERÓN-
    ZYGMUND (Δu ∈ Lᵖ ⇒ D²u ∈ Lᵖ for 1 < p < ∞; fails for p = ∞: u =
    x₁x₂ log|x|). DE GIORGI-NASH-MOSER: weak solutions of div(A∇u) = 0
    with A MEASURABLE, λ ≤ A ≤ Λ, are C^{0,α} and satisfy Harnack;
    solves HILBERT'S 19TH PROBLEM (minimizers of ∫F(∇u), F smooth
    uniformly convex, are smooth). Fails for systems (De Giorgi 1968).
    BOOTSTRAP: H¹ solutions of −Δu = u³ (n = 3) are C^∞.
  - CONFORMAL GEOMETRY: g̃ = e^{2u}g on a surface ⇒ K̃ = e^{−2u}(K − Δ_gu);
    constant K = 1 on R²: −Δu = e^{2u}; LIOUVILLE: all solutions of −Δv =
    e^v in R² with ∫e^v < ∞ are v = log(8λ²/(1 + λ²|x − x₀|²)²), ∫e^v =
    8π. YAMABE (n ≥ 3): g̃ = u^{4/(n−2)}g has R̃u^{(n+2)/(n−2)} =
    −(4(n − 1)/(n − 2))Δ_gu + R_gu; solved by Yamabe, Trudinger, Aubin,
    Schoen (positive mass theorem); Y(Sⁿ) = n(n − 1)|Sⁿ|^{2/n}.
    KAZDAN-WARNER obstruction on S²; Gauss-Bonnet ∫K̃dÃ = 2πχ.
  - HARMONIC MAPS u : (M, g) → (N, h): critical points of E(u) = ½∫|du|²;
    into Sᵏ ⊂ R^{k+1}: Δu + |∇u|²u = 0. u(x) = x/|x| : B³ → S² is weakly
    harmonic, E = 4π. E is CONFORMALLY INVARIANT in dimension 2; for
    u : S² → S², E(u) ≥ 4π|deg u|, equality iff u is ± holomorphic.
    EELLS-SAMPSON: N compact with K_N ≤ 0 ⇒ the heat flow ∂ₜu = τ(u)
    converges; every homotopy class has a harmonic representative.
    SACKS-UHLENBECK: bubbling of spheres; no degree-1 harmonic T² → S².
  - MINIMAL SURFACES: H = 0 ⇔ critical for area; graph equation
    div(∇u/√(1 + |∇u|²)) = 0; plane, catenoid, helicoid, Scherk z =
    log cos y − log cos x, Enneper. PLATEAU problem (Douglas, Radó).
    BERNSTEIN: entire minimal graphs over Rⁿ are affine iff n ≤ 7 (De
    Giorgi, Almgren, Simons); Bombieri-De Giorgi-Giusti counterexample
    for n ≥ 8, built on the SIMONS CONE in R⁸. Monotonicity: area in
    B_r(p) ≥ πr² for p on a minimal surface in R³. Two coaxial unit
    rings at distance d: catenoid exists iff d ≤ 1.3255.
  - MEAN CURVATURE FLOW ∂ₜx = −Hν (H = sum of principal curvatures, ν
    outward): sphere Sⁿ(r₀) ⊂ R^{n+1} has r² = r₀² − 2nt, T = r₀²/(2n);
    cylinder S^k × R^{n−k}: r² = r₀² − 2kt; SELF-SHRINKERS H = ⟨x, ν⟩/2
    (sphere of radius √(2n)); HUISKEN: convex ⇒ round point; HUISKEN
    MONOTONICITY. CURVE SHORTENING: dA/dt = −2π for embedded closed
    curves; GAGE-HAMILTON-GRAYSON: embedded ⇒ convex ⇒ round point;
    grim reaper y = t − log cos x translates with speed 1.
  - MONGE-AMPÈRE det D²u = f (u convex, elliptic only on convex u):
    Brenier optimal transport, Calabi-Yau; JÖRGENS-CALABI-POGORELOV:
    entire convex solutions of det D²u = 1 are quadratic polynomials.
  - SCALAR CONSERVATION LAWS uₜ + f(u)ₓ = 0: characteristics x = x₀ +
    f′(u₀(x₀))t; BREAKING TIME T* = −1/min(d/dx f′(u₀)) (Burgers: −1/min
    u₀′). RANKINE-HUGONIOT s = [f]/[u]; LAX ENTROPY f′(u_L) > s > f′(u_R);
    RAREFACTION u = (f′)⁻¹(x/t); weak solutions are NOT unique, KRUŽKOV
    entropy solutions are (L¹-contraction). VISCOUS BURGERS uₜ + uuₓ =
    νuₓₓ: HOPF-COLE u = −2νφₓ/φ with φₜ = νφₓₓ; viscous shock u = −tanh(x/
    (2ν)).
  - NONLINEAR EVOLUTION: SCALING u_λ = λ^{2/(p−1)}u(λx, λ²t) for uₜ = Δu
    + |u|^{p−1}u and iuₜ + Δu = ±|u|^{p−1}u; critical Sobolev index s_c =
    n/2 − 2/(p − 1); L²-critical p = 1 + 4/n, energy-critical p = (n + 2)/
    (n − 2). ODE blow-up u′ = u^p: T = u₀^{1−p}/(p − 1). FUJITA: on Rⁿ
    all positive solutions blow up iff 1 < p ≤ 1 + 2/n. NLS: mass and
    energy conserved; GLASSEY virial blow-up (focusing, negative energy,
    finite variance). KdV uₜ + 6uuₓ + uₓₓₓ = 0: soliton (c/2)sech²(√c(x −
    ct)/2), infinitely many conservation laws (integrable).
  - EULER (incompressible, ρ = 1): uₜ + (u·∇)u = −∇p, div u = 0. VORTICITY
    ω = curl u: ωₜ + (u·∇)ω = (ω·∇)u (VORTEX STRETCHING; absent in 2D,
    where ω is transported and ‖ω‖_∞ is conserved ⇒ global regularity,
    Wolibner, Yudovich). KELVIN: circulation around a material loop is
    constant; BERNOULLI: p + ½|u|² + gz constant on streamlines (steady).
    BEALE-KATO-MAJDA: a smooth solution blows up at T iff ∫₀ᵀ‖ω‖_∞dt =
    ∞. ONSAGER: C^α solutions conserve energy if α > 1/3 (Constantin-E-
    Titi); dissipative ones exist for α < 1/3 (Isett 2018).
  - NAVIER-STOKES uₜ + (u·∇)u − νΔu + ∇p = 0, div u = 0: pressure from
    −Δp = ∂ᵢ∂ⱼ(uᵢuⱼ); REYNOLDS NUMBER Re = UL/ν; ENERGY EQUALITY d/dt ½‖u‖²
    = −ν‖∇u‖². SCALING u_λ = λu(λx, λ²t), p_λ = λ²p(λx, λ²t); critical
    spaces L³(R³) ⊂ Ḃ^{−1+3/q}_{q,∞} ⊂ BMO⁻¹, Ḣ^{1/2}; the energy L² is
    SUPERCRITICAL in 3D (critical in 2D).
  - WEAK SOLUTIONS: LERAY (1934)-HOPF: global weak solutions for L² data
    with the energy INEQUALITY; unique and smooth in 2D, open in 3D.
    FUJITA-KATO: local mild solutions in Ḣ^{1/2}, global for small data;
    KOCH-TATARU for small BMO⁻¹ data. LADYZHENSKAYA-PRODI-SERRIN: u ∈
    LᵖₜL^qₓ, 2/p + 3/q = 1, 3 < q ≤ ∞ ⇒ smooth and unique; the endpoint
    L^∞L³ (Escauriaza-Seregin-Šverák). CAFFARELLI-KOHN-NIRENBERG: the
    singular set of a suitable weak solution has 1-dim parabolic
    Hausdorff measure 0. Global regularity in 3D is a Clay Millennium
    problem; very weak solutions are non-unique (Buckmaster-Vicol).
  - EXACT FLOWS: COUETTE u = Uy/h; PLANE POISEUILLE u = Gy(h − y)/(2μ);
    HAGEN-POISEUILLE u = G(R² − r²)/(4μ), Q = πGR⁴/(8μ); STOKES' FIRST
    PROBLEM u = U erfc(y/(2√(νt))); LAMB-OSEEN vortex ω = (Γ/(4πνt))
    e^{−r²/(4νt)}; TAYLOR-GREEN (sin x cos y, −cos x sin y)e^{−2νt};
    STOKES DRAG F = 6πμaU. KOLMOGOROV 1941: E(k) ≈ Cε^{2/3}k^{−5/3},
    dissipation scale η = (ν³/ε)^{1/4}, L/η ~ Re^{3/4}, 4/5 LAW
    S₃(ℓ) = −(4/5)εℓ.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show that an inequality ‖u‖_{L^q(Rⁿ)} ≤ C‖∇u‖_{L^p(Rⁿ)} valid for
    all u ∈ C_c^∞ forces q = p* = np/(n − p), and compute p* for (n, p) =
    (3, 2), (4, 2), (3, 1) and the Morrey exponent for (n, p) = (2, 4),
    (3, 6). (b) Show that u(x) = log log(1/|x|) on the disc B = B(0, 1/e)
    ⊂ R² lies in W^{1,2}(B) but not in L^∞, and compute ∫_B|∇u|².
    (c) Let u_λ(x) = λ^{(n−2)/2}u(λx). Show that ‖∇u_λ‖₂ and ‖u_λ‖_{2*} do
    not depend on λ and explain why H₀¹(B) ⊂ L^{2*}(B) is not compact.
    Answer: (a) q = p*; 6, 4, 3/2; C^{0,1/2}, C^{0,1/2}. (b) ∫|∇u|² = 2π.
    (c) Both scale like λ⁰; λ → ∞ concentrates mass, no convergent
    subsequence in L^{2*}.
    Teacher explains: (a) Replace u by u_λ(x) = u(λx). Then ∇u_λ(x) =
    λ(∇u)(λx), and substituting y = λx (dx = λ⁻ⁿdy):
       ‖u_λ‖_q = λ^{−n/q}‖u‖_q,     ‖∇u_λ‖_p = λ^{1−n/p}‖∇u‖_p.
    The inequality gives λ^{−n/q}‖u‖_q ≤ Cλ^{1−n/p}‖∇u‖_p for all λ > 0;
    letting λ → 0 or ∞ forces −n/q = 1 − n/p, i.e. 1/q = 1/p − 1/n, q =
    np/(n − p). (3, 2): 6/1 = 6; (4, 2): 8/2 = 4; (3, 1): 3/2. Morrey:
    α = 1 − n/p: (2, 4): 1 − 1/2 = 1/2; (3, 6): 1 − 1/2 = 1/2.
    (b) u is radial with u′(r) = (d/dr) log(log(1/r)) = −1/(r log(1/r)).
    Hence, with s = log(1/r) (ds = −dr/r, s from 1 to ∞),
       ∫_B|∇u|² = 2π∫₀^{1/e} dr/(r log²(1/r)) = 2π∫₁^∞ ds/s² = 2π.
    u² is integrable too (it grows only like (log s)² while dA = 2πr dr),
    and u → ∞ as r → 0, so u ∈ W^{1,2} ∖ L^∞: at p = n Morrey fails.
    (The weak derivative equals the pointwise one because a point has
    zero capacity in 2D.)
    (c) ∇u_λ = λ^{(n−2)/2+1}(∇u)(λx), so ‖∇u_λ‖₂² = λⁿλ^{−n}‖∇u‖₂² =
    ‖∇u‖₂². Also ‖u_λ‖_{2*}^{2*} = λ^{(n−2)/2·2n/(n−2)}λ^{−n}‖u‖^{2*} =
    ‖u‖^{2*}. Take u ∈ C_c^∞(B), u ≠ 0 and λₖ → ∞: u_{λₖ} is bounded in
    H₀¹(B), converges to 0 weakly and a.e., yet ‖u_{λₖ}‖_{2*} stays fixed:
    no subsequence converges in L^{2*}. For q < 2* Hölder gives
    ‖u_λ‖_q → 0, consistent with Rellich.
    Common mistakes: using p* = np/(n + p); forgetting that Morrey needs
    p > n strictly; thinking the counterexample in (b) needs u ∉ L²
    (it is in every Lᵖ, p < ∞).
    Now you try: for which α is |x|^{−α} in W^{1,2}(B(0, 1) ⊂ R³)?
    (α < 1/2)

D2. (a) Prove that J(u) = ∫_Ω(½|∇u|² − fu) (Ω bounded, f ∈ L²) has a
    unique minimizer on H₀¹(Ω) and that it solves −Δu = f weakly.
    (b) Show that J(u) = ∫_{−1}^1 x²u′(x)² dx with u(−1) = −1, u(1) = 1 has
    infimum 0 which is not attained (compute J on u_ε = clamp(x/ε)).
    (c) Find the Euler-Lagrange equation of ∫₀¹(u′² + u²) with u(0) = 0,
    u(1) = 1, solve it and compute the minimum value.
    Answer: (a) direct method below. (b) J(u_ε) = 2ε/3 → 0; J(u) = 0
    forces u′ = 0 a.e., impossible. (c) u″ = u, u = sinh x/sinh 1, min =
    coth 1 ≈ 1.3130.
    Teacher explains: (a) COERCIVE: by Poincaré ‖u‖₂ ≤ C_P‖∇u‖₂, so
       J(u) ≥ ½‖∇u‖₂² − ‖f‖₂C_P‖∇u‖₂ ≥ ¼‖∇u‖₂² − C_P²‖f‖₂²,
    hence m = inf J > −∞ and minimizing sequences are bounded in H₀¹.
    COMPACTNESS: a subsequence uₖ ⇀ u weakly in H₀¹ (reflexive).
    LOWER SEMICONTINUITY: the norm is weakly lsc, and u ↦ ∫fu is weakly
    continuous, so J(u) ≤ lim inf J(uₖ) = m: u is a minimizer. J is
    strictly convex, so the minimizer is unique. For v ∈ H₀¹ the
    function t ↦ J(u + tv) has a minimum at t = 0, so
       0 = (d/dt)J(u + tv)|₀ = ∫(∇u·∇v − fv),
    which is the weak form of −Δu = f.
    (b) J ≥ 0. u_ε = −1 for x ≤ −ε, x/ε on [−ε, ε], 1 for x ≥ ε is
    admissible (Lipschitz), and J(u_ε) = ∫_{−ε}^{ε}x²/ε² dx = 2ε³/(3ε²) =
    2ε/3 → 0. If J(u) = 0 for some admissible u ∈ W^{1,1} then x²u′² = 0
    a.e., so u′ = 0 a.e., u is constant, contradicting u(−1) ≠ u(1). The
    weight x² degenerates at 0: no coercivity.
    (c) F = u′² + u²: F_u − (F_{u′})′ = 2u − 2u″ = 0, so u″ = u, u = A
    cosh x + B sinh x; u(0) = 0 gives A = 0, u(1) = 1 gives B = 1/sinh 1.
    Integrating by parts, ∫(u′² + u²) = [uu′]₀¹ + ∫u(u − u″) = u(1)u′(1)
    = cosh 1/sinh 1 = coth 1 ≈ 1.3130. Convexity makes it the minimum.
    Common mistakes: forgetting lower semicontinuity (the direct method
    has THREE ingredients); claiming a minimizer exists in (b) because
    J ≥ 0; sign errors in the Euler-Lagrange equation (F_u − d/dx F_{u′}).
    Now you try: minimize ∫₀^π u′² over u(0) = u(π) = 0 with ∫₀^π u² = 1.
    (minimum 1, u = √(2/π) sin x)

D3. (a) Derive the POHOZAEV identity for a C² solution of −Δu = f(u) in a
    bounded smooth Ω, u = 0 on ∂Ω, and deduce that −Δu = |u|^{p−1}u has
    no nontrivial solution in a star-shaped Ω when p > (n + 2)/(n − 2).
    (b) Give the critical exponent (n + 2)/(n − 2) for n = 3, 4, 5, 6 and
    decide for which p there is a positive solution in the unit ball of
    R³. (c) Verify that U(x) = 3^{1/4}(1 + |x|²)^{−1/2} solves −ΔU = U⁵ in
    R³ and compute ∫U⁶.
    Answer: (a) n∫F(u) − ((n − 2)/2)∫|∇u|² = ½∫_{∂Ω}(x·ν)(∂_νu)².
    (b) 5, 3, 7/3, 2; for 1 < p < 5 only. (c) ∫U⁶ = 3^{3/2}π²/4 ≈ 12.82
    (= S₃^{3/2}).
    Teacher explains: (a) Multiply by x·∇u and integrate. On the right,
    f(u)x·∇u = x·∇F(u) (F′ = f, F(0) = 0), and ∫x·∇F(u) = −n∫F(u) since
    F(u) = 0 on ∂Ω. On the left, use ∂ᵢ(xⱼ∂ⱼu) = ∂ᵢu + xⱼ∂ᵢⱼu:
       ∫(x·∇u)Δu = ∫_{∂Ω}(x·∇u)∂_νu − ∫|∇u|² − ∫xⱼ∂ⱼ(½|∇u|²).
    The last integral is −(n/2)∫|∇u|² + ½∫_{∂Ω}(x·ν)|∇u|². On ∂Ω, u = 0
    so ∇u = (∂_νu)ν and x·∇u = (x·ν)∂_νu. Collecting,
       ∫(x·∇u)Δu = ((n − 2)/2)∫|∇u|² + ½∫_{∂Ω}(x·ν)(∂_νu)²,
    and with −Δu = f(u): n∫F(u) − ((n − 2)/2)∫|∇u|² = ½∫_{∂Ω}(x·ν)(∂_νu)².
    For f = |u|^{p−1}u, F = |u|^{p+1}/(p + 1); testing the equation with u
    gives ∫|∇u|² = ∫|u|^{p+1} =: A. Star-shaped means x·ν ≥ 0, so
       (n/(p + 1) − (n − 2)/2)A ≥ 0.
    If p > (n + 2)/(n − 2) the bracket is negative, so A = 0 and u = 0.
    (At p = (n + 2)/(n − 2) the boundary term vanishes; for positive u the
    Hopf lemma ∂_νu < 0 then gives a contradiction.)
    (b) n = 3: 5/1 = 5; n = 4: 6/2 = 3; n = 5: 7/3; n = 6: 8/4 = 2. In the
    ball of R³, for 1 < p < 5 the mountain pass gives a positive solution
    (subcritical, PS holds); for p ≥ 5 Pohozaev forbids it.
    (c) For radial w, Δw = w″ + (2/r)w′. With w = (1 + r²)^{−1/2}: w′ =
    −r(1 + r²)^{−3/2}, w″ = −(1 + r²)^{−3/2} + 3r²(1 + r²)^{−5/2}, so
       Δw = (1 + r²)^{−5/2}(−(1 + r²) + 3r² − 2(1 + r²)) = −3(1 + r²)^{−5/2}.
    Hence −ΔU = 3·3^{1/4}(1 + r²)^{−5/2} = 3^{5/4}(1 + r²)^{−5/2} = U⁵.
    ∫U⁶ = 3^{3/2}·4π∫₀^∞ r²(1 + r²)^{−3}dr = 3^{3/2}·4π·π/16 = 3^{3/2}π²/4.
    Common mistakes: dropping the boundary term (it is where the geometry
    of Ω enters: on an annulus solutions exist for all p > 1); writing
    Δ = w″ + w′/r in R³; thinking U ∈ L²(R³) (U ~ 1/r is not).
    Now you try: check that U = 2√2/(1 + |x|²) solves −ΔU = U³ in R⁴.
    (Δ(1 + r²)⁻¹ = −8(1 + r²)⁻³ in R⁴)

D4. (a) State the weak maximum principle for −Δu + cu ≥ 0 (c ≥ 0) and
    the sub/supersolution method. (b) Show that −u″ = λu(1 − u) on (0, π),
    u(0) = u(π) = 0, has a solution with 0 < u < 1 if λ > 1 and none if
    λ ≤ 1. (c) For the BRATU problem −u″ = λe^u on (0, 1), u(0) = u(1) =
    0, verify that u = 2 log(cosh(θ/4)/cosh(θ(x − ½)/2)) is a solution
    when θ = √(2λ) cosh(θ/4), and find the critical λ*.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) sub εsin x, super 1; λ ≤ 1 contradicts ∫
    against sin x. (c) λ = θ²/(2cosh²(θ/4)), maximal at θ ≈ 4.7987:
    λ* ≈ 3.5138; two solutions for 0 < λ < λ*.
    Teacher explains: (a) WEAK MAX PRINCIPLE: if Ω is bounded, c ≥ 0 and
    −Δu + cu ≥ 0 in Ω, u ≥ 0 on ∂Ω, then u ≥ 0 in Ω. SUB/SUPER: if u̲ ≤ ū
    satisfy −Δu̲ ≤ f(u̲), −Δū ≥ f(ū), u̲ ≤ 0 ≤ ū on ∂Ω, f Lipschitz, then
    monotone iteration −Δuₖ₊₁ + Muₖ₊₁ = f(uₖ) + Muₖ (M ≥ Lip f) from u̲
    increases to a solution u with u̲ ≤ u ≤ ū.
    (b) ū = 1: −ū″ = 0 = λ·1·0, a solution, so a supersolution. u̲ = ε sin
    x: −u̲″ = ε sin x and λu̲(1 − u̲) = λε sin x(1 − ε sin x) ≥ ε sin x iff
    λ(1 − ε sin x) ≥ 1, true for ε ≤ 1 − 1/λ when λ > 1. So a solution
    with ε sin x ≤ u ≤ 1 exists, and u < 1 by the strong max principle.
    Conversely, multiply by sin x and integrate by parts twice
    (sin″ = −sin, boundary terms vanish):
       ∫₀^π u sin x dx = λ∫₀^π u(1 − u) sin x dx < λ∫₀^π u sin x dx,
    so λ > 1 is necessary. Threshold = λ₁(0, π) = 1: bifurcation from 0.
    (c) Put v = θ(x − ½)/2, so u = 2 log cosh(θ/4) − 2 log cosh v. Then u′
    = −θ tanh v, u″ = −(θ²/2)sech²v, so −u″ = (θ²/2)sech²v, while λe^u =
    λcosh²(θ/4)sech²v. They agree iff λ = θ²/(2cosh²(θ/4)), i.e. θ =
    √(2λ) cosh(θ/4); u(0) = u(1) = 0 since v = ∓θ/4 there. The function
    g(θ) = θ²/(2cosh²(θ/4)) rises from 0 to a maximum and decays to 0;
    g′ = 0 gives θ tanh(θ/4) = 4, θ ≈ 4.7987, λ* = g(θ) ≈ 3.5138. For λ =
    1: θ ≈ 1.517 (max u ≈ 0.141, minimal solution) and θ ≈ 10.94 (max u ≈
    4.09, unstable).
    Common mistakes: using u̲ = sin x without the small ε; forgetting that
    the max principle needs c ≥ 0 (for −u″ − u on (0, π) it fails:
    u = −sin x); expecting uniqueness for Bratu.
    Now you try: for −u″ = λu(1 − u) on (0, L), find the threshold.
    (λ > π²/L²)

D5. (a) State the DE GIORGI-NASH-MOSER theorem and explain how it solves
    Hilbert's 19th problem. (b) Solve (a(x)u′)′ = 0 on (0, 1), u(0) = 0,
    u(1) = 1, with a = 1 on (0, ½) and a = 3 on (½, 1): find u′, u(½) and
    the regularity of u. (c) For u = x₁x₂ log|x| in R² compute Δu and
    ∂₁∂₂u; what does this say about Calderón-Zygmund at p = ∞?
    Answer: (a) below. (b) u′ = 3/2 then 1/2, u(½) = 3/4; Lipschitz, not
    C¹. (c) Δu = 4x₁x₂/|x|² (bounded), ∂₁∂₂u = log|x| + 1 − 2x₁²x₂²/|x|⁴
    (unbounded): D²u ∉ L^∞.
    Teacher explains: (a) If A(x) = (aᵢⱼ) is merely measurable with
    λ|ξ|² ≤ aᵢⱼξᵢξⱼ ≤ Λ|ξ|², every weak solution u ∈ H¹ of ∂ᵢ(aᵢⱼ∂ⱼu) = 0
    is locally C^{0,α}, α = α(n, λ, Λ) > 0, with ‖u‖_{C^α(B_{1/2})} ≤
    C‖u‖_{L²(B₁)}; positive solutions satisfy Harnack sup ≤ C inf. For a
    minimizer of ∫F(∇u), F smooth and uniformly convex, the E-L equation
    is ∂ᵢ(F_{ξᵢ}(∇u)) = 0. Differentiating in x_k (difference quotients),
    w = ∂ₖu solves ∂ᵢ(F_{ξᵢξⱼ}(∇u)∂ⱼw) = 0 — a linear equation with
    bounded measurable coefficients. De Giorgi ⇒ w ∈ C^α ⇒ u ∈ C^{1,α} ⇒
    coefficients C^α ⇒ Schauder ⇒ u ∈ C^{2,α} ⇒ ... ⇒ C^∞ (analytic if F
    is analytic).
    (b) The FLUX au′ is constant, = c. So u′ = c on (0, ½), u′ = c/3 on
    (½, 1). u(1) = c/2 + c/6 = 2c/3 = 1 gives c = 3/2: u′ = 3/2 then 1/2,
    u(½) = 3/4. u is Lipschitz (C^{0,1}), u′ jumps at ½: with only
    measurable coefficients one cannot expect C¹; Hölder continuity is
    what survives in every dimension.
    (c) Δ(fg) = fΔg + 2∇f·∇g + gΔf with f = x₁x₂ (harmonic), g = log r
    (harmonic off 0): Δu = 2(x₂, x₁)·(x₁, x₂)/r² = 4x₁x₂/r² = 2 sin 2θ,
    bounded but discontinuous at 0. Directly, ∂₂u = x₁ log r + x₁x₂²/r²,
    ∂₁∂₂u = log r + x₁²/r² + x₂²/r² − 2x₁²x₂²/r⁴ = log r + 1 − 2x₁²x₂²/r⁴,
    → −∞ as r → 0. So Δu ∈ L^∞ but D²u ∉ L^∞: Calderón-Zygmund holds
    for 1 < p < ∞ only (here D²u ∈ Lᵖ_loc for all p < ∞), and Schauder
    needs Hölder data, not just continuity.
    Common mistakes: thinking De Giorgi gives C¹ (it gives only C^α);
    applying it to systems (false: De Giorgi's 1968 counterexample);
    forgetting the 2∇f·∇g term.
    Now you try: bootstrap an H¹ weak solution of −Δu = u³ in B ⊂ R³.
    (u ∈ L⁶ ⇒ u³ ∈ L² ⇒ u ∈ W^{2,2} ⊂ C^{0,1/2} ⇒ Schauder ⇒ C^∞)

D6. (a) For g̃ = e^{2u}(dx² + dy²) show that the metrics with u =
    log(2/(1 + |x|²)) and u = log(2/(1 − |x|²)) have K = +1 and K = −1,
    and compute the total area of the first. (b) Show that v = 2u + log 2
    turns −Δu = e^{2u} into LIOUVILLE's equation −Δv = e^v; write the
    solution with v(0) = 0 and compute ∫_{R²}e^v. (c) On a closed surface
    Σ, which constant values of K̃ are possible for a metric e^{2u}g? For
    n ≥ 3 give the Yamabe equation constant 4(n − 1)/(n − 2) and the
    exponent (n + 2)/(n − 2) for n = 3, 4, 6.
    Answer: (a) 4π. (b) v = −2 log(1 + |x|²/8), ∫e^v = 8π. (c) sign of K̃
    = sign of χ(Σ) (K̃ = 0 iff χ = 0); constants 8, 6, 5 and exponents 5,
    3, 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) K = −e^{−2u}Δu. For radial f = log(1 + r²): f′
    = 2r/(1 + r²), Δf = f″ + f′/r = 4/(1 + r²)². So u = log 2 − f has Δu
    = −4/(1 + r²)², e^{−2u} = (1 + r²)²/4, and K = +1: this is the round
    unit sphere in stereographic coordinates. Area = ∫4/(1 + r²)²·2πr dr
    = 4π[−1/(1 + r²)]₀^∞ = 4π. For f = log(1 − r²): Δf = −4/(1 − r²)², u
    = log 2 − f gives Δu = 4/(1 − r²)² and K = −1: the Poincaré disc.
    (b) −Δv = −2Δu = 2e^{2u} = e^{2u + log 2} = e^v. From (a) with scaling
    x ↦ λx all solutions of −Δu = e^{2u} with finite area are log(2λ/(1
    + λ²|x − x₀|²)) (Chen-Li classification), so v = log(8λ²/(1 + λ²|x −
    x₀|²)²). v(0) = 0: x₀ = 0, 8λ² = 1, v = −2 log(1 + r²/8). Then ∫e^v =
    2∫e^{2u} = 2·4π = 8π for every λ, x₀.
    (c) Gauss-Bonnet: ∫K̃ dÃ = 2πχ(Σ), so a constant K̃ has the sign of χ:
    positive only on S² (and RP²), zero on T² and the Klein bottle,
    negative for genus ≥ 2. By uniformization every such sign is
    realized (Ricci flow, 13.8). YAMABE: 4(n − 1)/(n − 2) = 8, 6, 5 and
    (n + 2)/(n − 2) = 5, 3, 2 for n = 3, 4, 6.
    Common mistakes: sign of K = −e^{−2u}Δu; confusing −Δu = e^{2u} with
    −Δv = e^v (the total masses 4π and 8π differ by the factor 2);
    believing any positive K̃ on S² is realizable (Kazdan-Warner: K̃ = 1 +
    εx₃ is not).
    Now you try: K of e^{2u}|dx|² for u = x. (0: the metric e^{2x}|dx|² is
    flat)

D7. (a) Show that the Euler-Lagrange equation of E(u) = ½∫|∇u|² for maps
    u : Ω → S² ⊂ R³ is Δu + |∇u|²u = 0, and verify it for u(x) = x/|x| on
    R³ ∖ {0}; compute E(u) on the unit ball B³. Is x/|x| in W^{1,2}(B²)?
    (b) Prove that every smooth u : S² → S² satisfies E(u) ≥ 4π|deg u|,
    with equality iff u is conformal or anticonformal; find E of the
    identity and of z ↦ z³ (in stereographic coordinates). (c) State the
    Eells-Sampson theorem and explain what can go wrong for S² targets.
    Answer: (a) E = 4π on B³; not in W^{1,2}(B²). (b) 4π and 12π.
    (c) below.
    Teacher explains: (a) Vary u with the constraint |u| = 1: u_s = (u +
    sv)/|u + sv| has derivative v − (u·v)u at s = 0, the tangential part
    of v. So 0 = ∫∇u·∇(v − (u·v)u) for all v, i.e. −Δu is normal to S²:
    −Δu = μu. Dotting with u and using u·Δu = −|∇u|² (differentiate
    |u|² = 1 twice: u·∂ᵢu = 0, |∂ᵢu|² + u·∂ᵢ²u = 0) gives μ = |∇u|².
    For u = x/r in R³: Δ(xᵢ r⁻¹) = xᵢΔ(r⁻¹) + 2∇xᵢ·∇r⁻¹ = 0 − 2xᵢ/r³, and
    |∇u|² = ∑ᵢⱼ(δᵢⱼ/r − xᵢxⱼ/r³)² = (3 − 2 + 1)/r² = 2/r². So Δu + |∇u|²u
    = −2x/r³ + 2x/r³ = 0 off the origin. E = ½∫₀¹(2/r²)4πr² dr = 4π.
    In B² one gets |∇u|² = 1/r² and ∫₀¹(1/r²)2πr dr = ∞: not in W^{1,2}.
    (b) Pointwise |uₓ|² + |u_y|² ≥ 2|uₓ||u_y| ≥ 2|uₓ × u_y| = 2|u·(uₓ ×
    u_y)|, with equality iff uₓ ⊥ u_y and |uₓ| = |u_y| (conformality).
    Since E is conformally invariant in 2D, compute on R² via
    stereographic coordinates: E = ½∫(|uₓ|² + |u_y|²) ≥ |∫u·(uₓ × u_y)| =
    |pulled-back area| = 4π|deg u|. Identity: degree 1, conformal, E =
    4π (= ½∫_{S²}|∇id|² = ½·2·4π). z ↦ z³ is holomorphic of degree 3: E =
    12π.
    (c) EELLS-SAMPSON (1964): M, N compact, K_N ≤ 0 ⇒ the harmonic map
    heat flow ∂ₜu = τ(u) = Δu + A(u)(∇u, ∇u) exists for all time and
    subconverges to a harmonic map homotopic to u₀. The Bochner formula
    for the energy density e(u) has a good sign when K_N ≤ 0. For N =
    S² energy can concentrate at points and a sphere BUBBLES off
    (Sacks-Uhlenbeck); e.g. no harmonic map T² → S² of degree 1 exists
    (Eells-Wood), and the flow in finite energy classes may blow up.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the Lagrange multiplier term |∇u|²u;
    believing x/|x| is smooth-harmonic (it is only weakly harmonic, and
    in fact energy-minimizing, Brezis-Coron-Lieb); forgetting that
    E ≥ 4π|d| uses conformal invariance.
    Now you try: E(x/|x|) on the unit ball of R⁴. (|∇u|² = 3/r², E =
    ½·3·2π²·½ = 3π²/2)

D8. (a) Derive the minimal surface equation for graphs z = u(x, y) and
    verify it for SCHERK's surface u = log cos y − log cos x. (b) Two
    coaxial unit circles lie in the planes z = ±h. Show that a catenoid
    r = a cosh(z/a) spans them iff a cosh(h/a) = 1 has a solution, find
    the critical h*, and for h = ½ compare the catenoid areas with the
    two flat discs. (c) Under MEAN CURVATURE FLOW ∂ₜx = −Hν find r(t) for
    a round Sⁿ ⊂ R^{n+1} and for S¹(r) ⊂ R² (curve shortening); show that
    the area enclosed by an embedded closed curve under curve shortening
    decreases at rate 2π.
    Answer: (a) (1 + u_y²)uₓₓ − 2uₓu_yuₓ_y + (1 + uₓ²)u_yy = 0. (b) h* ≈
    0.6627 (distance 2h* ≈ 1.3255); h = ½: a ≈ 0.8483 (area ≈ 5.992) and a
    ≈ 0.2351 (area ≈ 6.846) versus 2π ≈ 6.283. (c) r² = r₀² − 2nt, T =
    r₀²/(2n); circle T = r₀²/2; dA/dt = −∫κ ds = −2π.
    Teacher explains: (a) Area A(u) = ∫√(1 + |∇u|²). E-L: div(∇u/W) = 0,
    W = √(1 + |∇u|²). Expanding and multiplying by W³ gives the
    displayed equation (it says the mean curvature H is 0). Scherk: uₓ =
    tan x, u_y = −tan y, uₓₓ = sec²x, u_yy = −sec²y, uₓ_y = 0:
       (1 + tan²y)sec²x + (1 + tan²x)(−sec²y) = sec²y sec²x − sec²x sec²y
    = 0. ✓ (defined on the square |x|, |y| < π/2 and its checkerboard
    translates).
    (b) A surface of revolution r = f(z) has area 2π∫f√(1 + f′²)dz; its
    E-L equation (Beltrami: f/√(1 + f′²) = const = a) gives f = a
    cosh((z − c)/a), symmetric: c = 0. Boundary: a cosh(h/a) = 1, i.e. h
    = a arccosh(1/a) =: φ(a), 0 < a ≤ 1. φ(0+) = 0 = φ(1), and φ has a
    single maximum: φ′(a) = 0 at a ≈ 0.5524, φ = h* ≈ 0.6627. For h > h*
    no catenoid exists (the soap film breaks into two discs: the
    GOLDSCHMIDT solution). Area of the catenoid: 2π∫_{−h}^{h}a cosh²(z/a)dz
    = πa(2h + a sinh(2h/a)). For h = ½ the roots are a ≈ 0.8483 (area ≈
    5.992 < 2π, stable, the one you see) and a ≈ 0.2351 (area ≈ 6.846,
    unstable).
    (c) For a sphere of radius r with outward ν, H = n/r and the flow is
    r′ = −n/r, so (r²)′ = −2n: r² = r₀² − 2nt, dies at T = r₀²/(2n);
    n = 1 (circle): T = r₀²/2. For a closed embedded curve with inward
    normal speed κ, dA/dt = −∫κ ds = −2π (turning number 1). A circle:
    A = πr², dA/dt = 2πrr′ = 2πr(−1/r) = −2π ✓. So every embedded curve
    enclosing area A₀ disappears at T = A₀/(2π) (Grayson: as a round
    point).
    Common mistakes: forgetting the cross term −2uₓu_yuₓ_y; picking the
    wrong catenoid (the thin one is unstable); using H = 1/r for Sⁿ
    (the SUM of principal curvatures is n/r).
    Now you try: extinction time of S²(3) ⊂ R³ and of an ellipse with
    semi-axes 2, 1 under curve shortening. (9/4; A₀/(2π) = 1)

D9. (a) For Burgers' equation uₜ + uuₓ = 0 find the characteristics and
    the breaking time for u₀(x) = 1/(1 + x²). (b) Solve the Riemann
    problems u_L = 1, u_R = 0 and u_L = 0, u_R = 1, and explain why the
    discontinuous weak solution of the second is rejected. (c) Solve
    Burgers with u₀ = 1 on (0, 1), 0 elsewhere for all t > 0. (d) Show
    that u = −2νφₓ/φ with φₜ = νφₓₓ solves uₜ + uuₓ = νuₓₓ, and check the
    viscous shock u = −tanh(x/(2ν)).
    Answer: (a) T* = 8/(3√3) ≈ 1.540. (b) shock of speed ½; rarefaction
    u = x/t on 0 < x < t. (c) below: shock meets fan at t = 2; then the
    shock is x = √(2t). (d) below.
    Teacher explains: (a) u is constant on x = x₀ + u₀(x₀)t. Two
    characteristics cross first when 1 + u₀′(x₀)t = 0, so T* = −1/min
    u₀′. u₀′ = −2x/(1 + x²)²; its derivative vanishes when (1 + x²) = 4x²,
    x = 1/√3: u₀′ = −(2/√3)/(16/9) = −3√3/8. T* = 8/(3√3) ≈ 1.540.
    (b) u_L = 1 > u_R = 0: RANKINE-HUGONIOT s = [u²/2]/[u] = (0 − ½)/(0 −
    1) = ½; Lax: u_L = 1 > s > 0 = u_R ✓. u_L = 0 < u_R = 1: the jump with
    s = ½ is a weak solution but characteristics EMERGE from it (0 < s <
    1), violating the entropy condition; the entropy solution is the
    rarefaction u = 0 (x ≤ 0), x/t (0 < x < t), 1 (x ≥ t).
    (c) At x = 0 a fan u = x/t (0 < x < t); at x = 1 a shock between 1
    and 0 with speed ½: x = 1 + t/2. The fan front x = t meets it when t =
    1 + t/2, t = 2, x = 2. Afterwards the shock separates u = x/t from 0:
    x′ = (x/t)/2, so x = C√t, C = √2: x = √(2t), u = x/t on (0, √(2t)).
    Mass check: ∫₀^{√(2t)}x/t dx = 2t/(2t) = 1 ✓ (conserved).
    (d) Put ψ = log φ, so u = −2νψₓ. From φₜ = νφₓₓ: ψₜ = ν(ψₓₓ + ψₓ²).
    Differentiate in x and multiply by −2ν:
       uₜ = −2ν²(ψₓₓₓ + 2ψₓψₓₓ) = νuₓₓ − 4ν²ψₓψₓₓ,
    and ψₓ = −u/(2ν), ψₓₓ = −uₓ/(2ν) give 4ν²ψₓψₓₓ = uuₓ: uₜ + uuₓ = νuₓₓ.
    For u = −tanh(x/(2ν)): u′ = −sech²/(2ν), u″ = sech²tanh/(2ν²), and
    uu′ = tanh sech²/(2ν) = νu″ ✓: a stationary shock from 1 to −1 of
    width ~ ν.
    Common mistakes: T* = −1/min u₀ instead of −1/min u₀′; taking s =
    (u_L + u_R)/2 for a non-Burgers flux; accepting the rarefaction shock.
    Now you try: breaking time for u₀ = e^{−x²}. (T* = √(e/2) ≈ 1.166)

D10. (a) Solve u′ = u^p, u(0) = u₀ > 0 (p > 1), find the blow-up time,
    and explain why every positive solution of uₜ = uₓₓ + u² on R blows
    up (FUJITA). (b) For uₜ = Δu + |u|^{p−1}u or iuₜ + Δu = ±|u|^{p−1}u
    show that u_λ = λ^{2/(p−1)}u(λx, λ²t) is again a solution, find the
    critical index s_c, and compute it for (n, p) = (3, 3), (3, 5),
    (2, 3), (1, 5). (c) Verify the KdV soliton u = (c/2)sech²(√c(x −
    ct)/2) and compute its mass ∫u; verify the NLS soliton √2 a
    sech(ax)e^{ia²t} of iuₜ + uₓₓ + |u|²u = 0 and its mass ∫|u|².
    Answer: (a) u = (u₀^{1−p} − (p − 1)t)^{−1/(p−1)}, T = u₀^{1−p}/(p − 1);
    p = 2 ≤ 1 + 2/n = 3. (b) s_c = n/2 − 2/(p − 1): ½, 1, 0, 0. (c) ∫u =
    2√c; ∫|u|² = 4a.
    Teacher explains: (a) Separate: u^{−p}du = dt, (u^{1−p} − u₀^{1−p})/(1
    − p) = t, so u^{1−p} = u₀^{1−p} − (p − 1)t, which hits 0 at T =
    u₀^{1−p}/(p − 1) (p = 2, u₀ = 1: u = 1/(1 − t), T = 1). For the PDE on
    Rⁿ, FUJITA (1966): if 1 < p < 1 + 2/n every positive solution blows
    up in finite time (also p = 1 + 2/n), while for p > 1 + 2/n small
    data give global solutions. n = 1: 1 + 2 = 3 > 2, so uₜ = uₓₓ + u²
    always blows up: heat spreading cannot beat the reaction. (Proof
    idea: ∫u(x, t)G(x, t₀ − t)dx satisfies a Jensen-type ODE inequality.)
    (b) ∂ₜu_λ = λ^{2/(p−1)+2}uₜ, Δu_λ = λ^{2/(p−1)+2}Δu, and |u_λ|^{p−1}u_λ =
    λ^{2p/(p−1)}|u|^{p−1}u with 2p/(p − 1) = 2/(p − 1) + 2 ✓. In Ḣˢ(Rⁿ),
    ‖u_λ(0)‖_{Ḣˢ} = λ^{2/(p−1)+s−n/2}‖u(0)‖_{Ḣˢ}, invariant iff s = s_c =
    n/2 − 2/(p − 1). (3, 3): 3/2 − 1 = ½; (3, 5): 3/2 − ½ = 1 (energy-
    critical); (2, 3): 1 − 1 = 0 and (1, 5): ½ − ½ = 0 (mass-critical,
    p = 1 + 4/n).
    (c) A traveling wave u = w(y), y = x − ct, satisfies −cw′ + 6ww′ + w‴
    = 0. Integrate (decay at ∞): −cw + 3w² + w″ = 0; multiply by w′ and
    integrate again: w′² = cw² − 2w³. For w = (c/2)S², S = sech(√cy/2),
    T = tanh(√cy/2): w′ = −(c^{3/2}/2)S²T, so w′² = (c³/4)S⁴T² =
    (c³/4)S⁴(1 − S²) = cw² − 2w³ ✓ (as cw² = c³S⁴/4, 2w³ = c³S⁶/4).
    Mass: ∫(c/2)sech²(√cy/2)dy = (c/2)(2/√c)·2 = 2√c.
    NLS: u = φ(x)e^{iωt} requires −ωφ + φ″ + φ³ = 0; for φ = A sech(ax),
    φ″ = a²φ − 2a²A sech³, so ω = a², A² = 2a². ∫|u|² = 2a²∫sech²(ax)dx =
    2a²·2/a = 4a.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the critical case p = 1 + 2/n also blows
    up; writing s_c = n/2 − 1/(p − 1); taller KdV solitons are FASTER
    (amplitude c/2, speed c), not slower.
    Now you try: s_c for the cubic NLS in R⁴ and the Fujita exponent for
    n = 3. (s_c = 1, energy-critical; p_F = 5/3)

D11. (a) Derive the energy equality d/dt ½‖u‖₂² = −ν‖∇u‖₂² for smooth
    Navier-Stokes solutions on R³ (or T³) and explain what a LERAY-HOPF
    weak solution is. (b) Show that u_λ(x, t) = λu(λx, λ²t) is again a
    solution, and find which of ‖u‖_{L²}, ‖u‖_{L³}, ‖u‖_{Ḣ^{1/2}} are
    invariant in R³; why is 3D regularity "supercritical"? (c) Find the
    HAGEN-POISEUILLE flow in a pipe of radius R under pressure gradient
    −∂ₓp = G, its flux, and Re = U_mean·2R/ν for water (ν = 10⁻⁶ m²/s)
    in a 2 cm pipe with mean speed 0.1 m/s.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) L³ and Ḣ^{1/2} invariant, L² scales like
    λ^{−1/2}. (c) u = G(R² − r²)/(4μ), Q = πGR⁴/(8μ); Re = 2000.
    Teacher explains: (a) Dot the equation with u and integrate. The
    nonlinear term: ∫u·(u·∇)u = ∫uⱼ∂ⱼ(½|u|²) = −∫(div u)½|u|² = 0. The
    pressure: ∫u·∇p = −∫p div u = 0. Viscous: ∫u·νΔu = −ν∫|∇u|². So
       d/dt ½‖u‖² = −ν‖∇u‖², ½‖u(t)‖² + ν∫₀ᵗ‖∇u‖² = ½‖u₀‖².
    A LERAY-HOPF solution is u ∈ L^∞(0, T; L²) ∩ L²(0, T; Ḣ¹), div u = 0,
    solving the equation in distributions and satisfying the energy
    INEQUALITY ½‖u(t)‖² + ν∫₀ᵗ‖∇u‖² ≤ ½‖u₀‖². Leray (1934) proved global
    existence for every u₀ ∈ L² (compactness from these bounds);
    uniqueness and smoothness are open in 3D, known in 2D (Ladyzhenskaya).
    (b) uₜ scales as λ³, (u·∇)u as λ·λ·λ = λ³, νΔu as λ·λ² = λ³, and ∇p
    with p_λ = λ²p(λx, λ²t) as λ³ ✓. Norms at t = 0: ‖u_λ‖_{L^q} =
    λ^{1−3/q}‖u‖_{L^q} (L²: λ^{−1/2}; L³: λ⁰), ‖u_λ‖_{Ḣˢ} = λ^{s−1/2}‖u‖_{Ḣˢ}
    (invariant at s = ½). The only coercive controlled quantities (energy
    L², dissipation ∫‖∇u‖², scaling λ^{−1}) get WEAKER at small scales λ
    → ∞: this is "supercritical"; small-data or critical-norm control
    (Fujita-Kato in Ḣ^{1/2}, Serrin, ESS L^∞L³) gives regularity. In 2D,
    ‖u_λ‖_{L²} = ‖u‖_{L²}: critical, global regularity holds.
    (c) Unidirectional u = u(r)eₓ: (u·∇)u = 0, so μ(u″ + u′/r) = −G,
    (ru′)′ = −Gr/μ, u = G(R² − r²)/(4μ) (bounded, no slip at r = R).
    Q = ∫₀ᴿ u·2πr dr = (πG/(2μ))(R⁴/2 − R⁴/4) = πGR⁴/(8μ); mean speed
    GR²/(8μ) = half the maximum. Re = 0.1·0.02/10⁻⁶ = 2000: near the
    classical laminar-turbulent transition (~2300).
    Common mistakes: forgetting div u = 0 kills both the nonlinear and
    the pressure term; confusing Leray-Hopf (energy inequality) with
    energy equality; getting Q ∝ R² instead of R⁴ (doubling R gives 16Q).
    Now you try: plane Poiseuille between y = 0, h: velocity and flux
    per unit width. (u = Gy(h − y)/(2μ); Q = Gh³/(12μ))

D12. (a) Derive the vorticity equation ωₜ + (u·∇)ω = (ω·∇)u + νΔω and
    explain why 2D Euler/Navier-Stokes has global smooth solutions while
    3D is open (BEALE-KATO-MAJDA). (b) Show that the LAMB-OSEEN vortex ω =
    (Γ/(4πνt))e^{−r²/(4νt)} solves the 2D vorticity equation; find its
    azimuthal velocity and the radius of maximal velocity. (c) Verify the
    TAYLOR-GREEN vortex u = (sin x cos y, −cos x sin y)e^{−2νt}, find its
    pressure and energy on [0, 2π]², and check the energy equality.
    (d) Which (p, q) satisfy the Ladyzhenskaya-Prodi-Serrin condition
    with q = 4, 6, ∞, 3?
    Answer: (a) below. (b) v_θ = (Γ/(2πr))(1 − e^{−r²/(4νt)}); r_max ≈
    2.2418√(νt). (c) p = ¼(cos 2x + cos 2y)e^{−4νt}, E = π²e^{−4νt}.
    (d) p = 8, 4, 2, ∞.
    Teacher explains: (a) Take the curl of uₜ + (u·∇)u + ∇p = νΔu, using
    (u·∇)u = ∇(½|u|²) − u × ω and curl ∇ = 0: ωₜ − curl(u × ω) = νΔω, and
    curl(u × ω) = (ω·∇)u − (u·∇)ω (div u = div ω = 0). In 2D, ω = ωe₃
    is perpendicular to the plane of motion, (ω·∇)u = 0 and ω is a
    transported-diffused SCALAR: ωₜ + u·∇ω = νΔω. The maximum principle
    gives ‖ω(t)‖_∞ ≤ ‖ω₀‖_∞, hence ∫₀ᵀ‖ω‖_∞ ≤ T‖ω₀‖_∞ < ∞, and BKM (a
    smooth solution continues past T unless ∫₀ᵀ‖ω‖_∞ = ∞) gives global
    regularity. In 3D the STRETCHING term (ω·∇)u ~ ω² can amplify ω.
    (b) ω is radial so u·∇ω = 0; it remains to check ωₜ = νΔω: this is
    the heat kernel in R² times Γ (∫ω dA = Γ for all t). Circulation
    inside radius r: Γ(r) = ∫₀ʳω·2πs ds = Γ(1 − e^{−r²/(4νt)}), so v_θ =
    Γ(r)/(2πr). Near 0 solid-body rotation, far away the point vortex
    Γ/(2πr). With x = r²/(4νt), dv_θ/dr = 0 ⇔ (1 + 2x)e^{−x} = 1, x ≈
    1.2564, r ≈ 2√(1.2564νt) ≈ 2.2418√(νt): the core grows like √(νt).
    (c) div u = cos x cos y − cos x cos y = 0. (u·∇)u = (½ sin 2x, ½ sin
    2y)e^{−4νt}, so −∇p must equal that: p = ¼(cos 2x + cos 2y)e^{−4νt}.
    Δu = −2u, so uₜ = −2νu = νΔu ✓. E = ½∫(sin²x cos²y + cos²x sin²y) =
    ½(π² + π²)e^{−4νt} = π²e^{−4νt}, dE/dt = −4νπ²e^{−4νt}; and ‖∇u‖² =
    ∫(cos²x cos²y + sin²x sin²y + sin²x sin²y + cos²x cos²y)e^{−4νt} =
    4π²e^{−4νt}, so −ν‖∇u‖² = dE/dt ✓. Vorticity ω = 2 sin x sin y e^{−2νt}.
    (d) 2/p + 3/q = 1: q = 4: 2/p = 1/4, p = 8; q = 6: p = 4; q = ∞: p =
    2; q = 3: p = ∞ (Escauriaza-Seregin-Šverák endpoint).
    Common mistakes: keeping (ω·∇)u in 2D; forgetting the 1 in 1 − e^{−x}
    (the velocity would blow up at 0); the sign of p: the pressure is LOW
    at the vortex centres (π/2, π/2) (p = −½, ω maximal) and HIGH at the
    stagnation points (0, 0) (p = ½), as ∇p must supply the centripetal
    force.
    Now you try: vorticity and pressure of the solid-body rotation u =
    (−y, x). (ω = 2; p = (x² + y²)/2 + const)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Sobolev exponents, embeddings, compactness.
    a) p* for n = 3, p = 2           b) p* for n = 4, p = 2
    c) p* for n = 5, p = 2           d) p* for n = 3, p = 1
    e) p* for n = 2, p = 1           f) p* for n = 4, p = 3
    g) p* for n = 6, p = 2           h) p* for n = 3, p = 3/2
    i) Morrey exponent, n = 2, p = 4 j) Morrey exponent, n = 3, p = 4
    k) Morrey exponent, n = 1, p = 2
    l) W^{2,2}(R³) embeds in which Hölder space?
    m) is W^{2,2}(R⁴) ⊂ L^∞? which L^q?
    n) least integer k with H^k(Rⁿ) ⊂ C⁰ for n = 1, 2, 3, 4
    o) q with Ḣˢ(R³) ⊂ L^q for s = ½, 1, ¾
    p) for which α is |x|^{−α} ∈ W^{1,2}(B(0, 1) ⊂ R³)?
    q) for which α is |x|^{−α} ∈ W^{1,2}(B(0, 1) ⊂ R⁴)?
    r) ∫|∇u|² for u = log log(1/|x|) on B(0, 1/e) ⊂ R²
    s) best C in ‖u‖₂ ≤ C‖u′‖₂ on H₀¹(0, L); value for L = 1
    t) is H₀¹(B) ⊂ L^q(B) compact (B ⊂ R³) for q = 4? q = 6?
    u) is H¹(R³) ⊂ L²(R³) compact?
    Answers:
      a) 6        b) 4        c) 10/3     d) 3/2      e) 2        f) 12
      g) 3        h) 3        i) C^{0,1/2}            j) C^{0,1/4}
      k) C^{0,1/2}            l) C^{0,1/2}
      m) no; every L^q with 2 ≤ q < ∞       n) 1, 2, 2, 3 (k > n/2)
      o) 3, 6, 4 (q = 6/(3 − 2s))           p) α < ½
      q) α < 1    r) 2π       s) L/π; 1/π
      t) yes (4 < 6); no (critical)         u) no (translates u(x − ke₁))

M2. Euler-Lagrange equations and the direct method.
    a) E-L of ∫(u′² + 2u)          b) E-L of ∫(u′² − u²)
    c) E-L of ∫(½|∇u|² − fu)       d) E-L of ∫(½|∇u|² − ¼u⁴)
    e) E-L of ∫(½|∇u|² + ½u² − λeᵘ)
    f) E-L of ∫(1/p)|∇u|ᵖ          g) E-L of ∫√(1 + |∇u|²)
    h) E-L of ∫√(1 + u′²) and its solutions
    i) E-L of ∫u√(1 + u′²) and its solutions
    j) E-L of ∫(½u′² + ¼(1 − u²)²) on R; the kink with u(0) = 0, u(±∞)
       = ±1, and its energy
    k) min ∫₀¹u′², u(0) = 0, u(1) = 1
    l) min ∫₀¹(u′² + u²), u(0) = 0, u(1) = 1
    m) min ∫₀²u′², u(0) = 0, u(2) = 4
    n) min ∫₀^π u′² with ∫₀^π u² = 1, u(0) = u(π) = 0
    o) min ∫_D|∇u|² on the unit disc with u = cos θ on ∂D
    p) same with u = cos 2θ on ∂D; with u = cos kθ
    q) inf ∫_{−1}^1 x²u′², u(±1) = ±1: value, attained?
    r) inf ∫₀¹((u′² − 1)² + u²), u(0) = u(1) = 0: value, attained?
    s) inf ∫₀¹(u′² − 1)², u(0) = u(1) = 0: value, attained?
    t) is det : R^{2×2} → R convex? what is ∫det ∇u's E-L equation?
    Answers:
      a) u″ = 1               b) u″ + u = 0          c) −Δu = f
      d) −Δu = u³             e) −Δu + u = λeᵘ
      f) −div(|∇u|^{p−2}∇u) = 0 (p-Laplacian)
      g) div(∇u/√(1 + |∇u|²)) = 0          h) u″ = 0: straight lines
      i) uu″ = 1 + u′²: catenaries u = a cosh((x − b)/a)
      j) u″ = u³ − u; u = tanh(x/√2); energy 2√2/3
      k) 1 (u = x)            l) coth 1 ≈ 1.3130     m) 8 (u = 2x)
      n) 1                    o) π (u = x)           p) 2π; kπ
      q) 0, not attained      r) 0, not attained (sawtooth sequence)
      s) 0, attained (e.g. ½ − |x − ½|; infinitely many minimizers)
      t) no (indefinite quadratic form); 0 = 0 (a NULL LAGRANGIAN:
         every map is critical)

M3. Critical exponents, Pohozaev, bubbles, mountain pass.
    a) (n + 2)/(n − 2) for n = 3, 4, 5, 6, 10
    b) sign of n/(p + 1) − (n − 2)/2 for (n, p) = (3, 3), (3, 5), (3, 7),
       (4, 2), (4, 5)
    c) positive solution of −Δu = u^p in the unit ball of R³ for p = 2,
       4, 5, 6?
    d) positive radial solution in an annulus of R³ for p = 7?
    e) the R³ bubble with λ = 1; its value at 0; the one with λ = 2
    f) the R⁴ bubble (λ = 1) and the R⁶ bubble (λ = 1)
    g) S₃ = ¾|S³|^{2/3}: exact and numerical
    h) ∫_{R³}U⁶ for U = 3^{1/4}(1 + |x|²)^{−1/2}
    i) Brezis-Nirenberg range of λ in the unit ball of R³; of R⁴
    j) max over t > 0 of J(tu) = at²/2 − bt⁴/4 for a = b = 1, and for
       a = 2, b = 1
    k) β with ‖∇(λ^βu(λx))‖₂ = ‖∇u‖₂ in R³, R⁴
    l) positive entire solutions of −Δu = u^p in R³ for p = 3, 5, 7?
    m) for which p is H₀¹ ∋ u ↦ ∫|u|^{p+1} weakly continuous (B ⊂ R³)?
    Answers:
      a) 5, 3, 7/3, 2, 3/2
      b) +¼, 0, −1/8, +1/3, −1/3 (≤ 0 ⇒ no positive solution in a
         star-shaped domain)
      c) yes, yes, no, no     d) yes (Pohozaev needs star-shaped)
      e) 3^{1/4}(1 + |x|²)^{−1/2}, 3^{1/4} ≈ 1.316; 3^{1/4}(2/(4 +
         |x|²))^{1/2}
      f) 2√2/(1 + |x|²); 24/(1 + |x|²)²
      g) 3(π/2)^{4/3} ≈ 5.478
      h) 3^{3/2}π²/4 ≈ 12.82 (= S₃^{3/2} = ∫|∇U|²)
      i) π²/4 < λ < π²; 0 < λ < j²₁,₁ ≈ 14.68
      j) ¼ at t = 1; 1 at t = √2        k) ½; 1
      l) none (Gidas-Spruck); the bubbles; yes (radial, slow decay)
      m) p + 1 < 6, i.e. p < 5

    a) λ₁ of −d²/dx² (Dirichlet) on (0, π), (0, 1), (0, 2)
